## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — 2026-08-18 (added, rule_syntax)
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:160 — the "result must parse" guard only refuses an ambiguous `}` placement when the alternative placement would make the appended candidate unparseable; when the line after the unclosed literal begins with a binary operator both placements parse and mean different things, and the rule silently commits one. On `x = {1, 2` followed by a line `|> IO.inspect()` before `end`, the rule emits `x = {1, 2\n|> IO.inspect()}` — the tuple `{1, IO.inspect(2)}` — where the author plausibly meant `x = {1, 2}` piped into `IO.inspect`, i.e. `IO.inspect({1, 2})`. The output parses and compiles, so neither ProgressGuard nor the all-or-nothing commit can catch a wrong choice, and no test in the battery covers an operator-continuation line (the "ambiguous placement" tests only cover the case where the wrong placement fails to parse). The moduledoc (lines 35–44) says the rule "stays silent rather than picking a placement" for either-line ambiguities, which overstates the guard. The tokenizer-faithful placement is a defensible documented policy, but a human should decide whether to also refuse (or at least pin with a test) when the following line starts with an operator, since that is exactly the shape where the LLM's dropped `}` belongs one line up.
- experiment: settles the concern above — run the rule on the operator-continuation fixture and inspect which placement it commits: mix run -e 'IO.puts Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace.fix("defmodule Example do\n  def foo do\n    x = {1, 2\n    |> IO.inspect()\n  end\nend\n")'

  - **Experiment executed 2026-08-18 (by the orchestrator, memory-capped):
    confirmed.** The rule emits `x = {1, 2\n    |> IO.inspect()}` — the tuple
    `{1, IO.inspect(2)}` — on the operator-continuation input above. Both
    placements parse; the rule silently commits the tokenizer-faithful one,
    and no test pins this shape.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:98 — the negative battery only covers ambiguous placements where appending `}` fails to parse, so it presents the rule's guard as stronger than it is; the untested case is a continuation line that starts with an operator, e.g. `x = {:ok, v` / `|> to_string()` / `end`, where appending the brace to the last content line yields `{:ok, v |> to_string()}` — that parses, so analyze flags it and the fix commits it, silently turning the author's likely `{:ok, v} |> to_string()` into a different program. No test in this file (or the fix file) pins that behaviour either way.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:35 — nothing pins the `@max_braces 5` backstop from the analyze side: a literal needing six closing braces produces no issue at all, and the deepest input tested anywhere in the pair needs three, so the boundary could move without a test noticing.
- experiment: settles the concern above — mix run -e 'IO.puts Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace.fix("defmodule E do\n  def foo(v) do\n    x = {:ok, v\n    |> to_string()\n  end\nend\n")'

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] fixed — Confirmed by executing the rule on the reviewer's fixture: on `x = {1, 2` followed by a line `|> IO.inspect()`, the rule emitted `x = {1, 2\n    |> IO.inspect()}` (the tuple `{1, IO.inspect(2)}`), and a second executed check showed the alternative placement `x = {1, 2}\n|> IO.inspect()` also parses with a different AST — so the rule was silently choosing one of two valid meanings, while its own moduledoc promised to stay silent rather than pick a placement. Pinned with two tests that fail on the old code for exactly this reason: a fix test asserting the operator-continuation source is returned byte-for-byte untouched, and an analyze test asserting no issue is reported (the rule never flags what it will not fix). Fixed by adding a fourth guard, `sole_placement/3`: after a repair candidate is found, the rule tries appending the `}` to each earlier non-blank line of the literal, and refuses the whole repair if any of those also makes the source parse. Lines ending in `,` are excluded as competing placements, by the same reasoning as the existing dangling-comma guard (closing after a trailing comma silently drops an element, so the comma pins the next line inside the literal) — this is what keeps every previously-admitted fixture repaired byte-for-byte identically. The moduledoc now documents the new guard ("Four guards" with a worked `|>` example). Proven: the full rule battery passes (33 tests), the fast gate is green (mix format, mix compile --warnings-as-errors, mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency: 9024 tests, 0 failures), and re-running the original reproduction shows the rule now leaves the ambiguous source untouched. Committed as 38c0d19 (rule + both test files).
- commits: 38c0d19c78cf63fa9410bdfe815109df9aa93679
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

- [1 concern] obsolete — The concern was real when written, but commit 38c0d19 (landed earlier today, during the fix session for the rule's own file) already addressed it: it added a fourth guard, sole_placement/3, that refuses a repair whenever appending the `}` to an earlier non-blank line of the literal would also make the source parse, and it added pinning tests in both test files — including the analyze test "no issue when the next line's leading operator makes both placements parse", which is exactly the operator-continuation shape the reviewer describes. Proven by executing the reviewer's own experiment on the current tree: running analyze and fix on `x = {:ok, v` followed by `|> to_string()` returns `[]` from analyze and the source byte-for-byte unchanged from fix — the rule no longer commits `{:ok, v |> to_string()}`. The reviewer examined the file as of the frozen manifest (36fcdbc), which predates 38c0d19.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed by executing analyze on nested literals: a literal needing five closing braces flags line 3, one needing six returns no issue at all, and the deepest input tested anywhere in the pair was three — so the @max_braces backstop was entirely unpinned, as the reviewer said. Added two analyze tests in commit 2c40cc4: one asserting the five-deep literal (exactly at the backstop) is flagged, one asserting the six-deep literal (just beyond it) yields no issue. Verified the tests actually guard the boundary by temporarily changing @max_braces to 6: the six-deep test then fails, so the constant can no longer move without a test noticing. Full battery (17 tests) and the fast gate are green.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:186 — the uniqueness guard only ever asks "would putting *all* the missing braces at the end of one earlier line also parse?" (`close_at(lines, earlier)` appends `String.duplicate("}", count)` to a single line). It never considers a placement *split* across two lines, so when the repair needs more than one `}` a second, differently-meaning reading can survive the guard unnoticed. Concrete input — `defmodule Example do` / `  def foo do` / `    x = {1, {2` / `    , 3` / `  end` / `end`: the target line `    , 3` takes two braces and yields `{1, {2, 3}}`, which parses, and the only earlier line (`    x = {1, {2`) parses with neither one brace (outer still open) nor two (`, 3` then becomes a standalone statement), so `sole_placement/3` returns `:ok` and the fix commits. The competing reading `{1, {2}, 3}` — inner closed on the first line, outer on the second — is equally faithful to the truncated source and is never tested. Output parses and compiles, so neither ProgressGuard nor the all-or-nothing commit can catch the wrong choice. Leading-comma continuation lines are rare in Elixir, so this is a narrow shape, but it is the same silent-behaviour-change class the guard was added for and the moduledoc's "placement must be beyond doubt" bullet (lines 46-58) reads as stronger than what the code checks. A human should decide whether to widen the guard to split placements, or narrow the moduledoc claim to "single-line placements".
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:72 — the moduledoc enumerates "four guards" and then says "the smallest number of `}` that makes the source parse is used", with no mention that `@max_braces 5` is a hard ceiling: a literal needing six closers is silently refused (pinned by `test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:156`). A maintainer reading only the moduledoc would expect arbitrary nesting to be repaired. One sentence in the "safe core" section, alongside the other refusals, would make the list complete.
- experiment: settles the first finding — `mix run -e 'IO.puts(Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace.fix("defmodule Example do\n  def foo do\n    x = {1, {2\n    , 3\n  end\nend\n"))'` — if it prints `x = {1, {2` / `, 3}}` the guard admitted a two-reading input; if it prints the input unchanged the guard already covers it.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] fixed — The rule's "placement must be beyond doubt" guard only ever asked one question: "would putting *all* the missing braces at the end of one earlier line also make the source parse?" It never asked whether the braces could be *split* — one on an earlier line, the rest on the last — so whenever a repair needed two or more braces, a second reading with a different meaning could slip through unseen. The reviewer's own example did not reproduce: I ran their experiment and the rule left the source untouched, because that candidate (`, 3}}`) does not parse at all, so the rule never got as far as the guard. Rather than refute on one bad fixture, I searched for the shape the finding describes: a script generated 1400 truncated sources, ran the real rule on each, and for every repair it committed with two or more braces checked whether any split placement also parsed. Four sources came back positive. The simplest is `x = {1, {2, {3` followed by `}, 5` — the rule committed `x = {1, {2, {3` / `}, 5}}`, i.e. `{1, {2, {3}, 5}}`, while `{1, {2, {3}` / `}, 5}` — the inner brace one line up — parses just as well and is a different value. Both compile, so nothing downstream could have caught the wrong pick. Pinned with two tests, one in each battery: a fix test asserting that source comes back byte-for-byte unchanged, and an analyze test asserting no issue is reported (the rule must never flag what it will not fix). Both failed on the old code showing exactly the wrong repair (`}, 5}}`) and the spurious issue on line 3. Fixed by widening the existing guard rather than adding a new one: `sole_placement/3` now also calls a new `split_placement?/3`, which for each earlier line tries every share of the braces between that line and the target line and treats any parsing result as a competing placement. The existing trailing-comma exclusion is applied unchanged (closing after a comma drops an element, so a comma still pins the next line inside the literal), which is what keeps every previously-admitted input repaired identically — the whole 37-test battery passes with no expectation touched. Re-running the 1400-source search after the fix finds 0 split-ambiguous repairs, down from 4. The moduledoc bullet, which the reviewer noted claimed more than the code checked, now says split placements are tried too — it is once again a description of what the code does. Committed as c93cb62 with both test files. Fast gate green: mix format on the three touched files, mix compile --warnings-as-errors, and mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency (9028 tests, 6 properties, 0 failures).
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed by executing the rule, not by reading: on a literal nested five deep, analyze reports an issue; on one nested six deep it reports nothing and fix returns the source unchanged. So the `@max_braces 5` ceiling really is a silent refusal, and the moduledoc's closing claim — "the smallest number of `}` that makes the source parse is used", with a note that that number is unique — read as if any depth would be repaired. The behaviour itself was already pinned by an existing test added earlier today (commit 2c40cc4, `test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:156`), so this finding needed no new test: it is a documentation gap, not a behaviour gap, and I confirmed the pin still guards the boundary by running that battery. Added one sentence to the same paragraph the reviewer flagged, saying the cap is five and that a literal needing six or more closers is refused like the other cases rather than repaired. Landed in the same commit, c93cb62, under the same green gate.
- commits: c93cb627f9cfe7f26c6179de16498a9df78cf2d3
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:192 — the "placement must be beyond doubt" guard only scans lines from `open_line - 1` downward, where `open_line` is whatever `Code.string_to_quoted/2` put in the error's `:line` field. When a literal leaves more than one `{` unclosed and those `{` sit on *different* lines (e.g. `x = {1,` on one line and `%{a: 2` on the next), that field names only one of them — on my reading of the tokenizer it is the innermost, because the error is raised against the top of the delimiter stack. If so, every line between the outer `{` and the inner one is never tried as a competing `}` placement, so on multi-line nested literals the uniqueness guard is weaker than the moduledoc's second bullet (lines 46-60) claims, and the same lines are also invisible to `split_placement?/3` (line 206), which shares the range. Nothing in either test file pins which `{` the parser names: every nested fixture (`..._analyze_test.exs:39`, `:51`; `..._fix_test.exs:145`) opens all of its braces on one line. The consequence is bounded — a `}` on a line before the inner `{` cannot close that inner `{`, and the leftover continuation lines are usually comma-led and would not stand alone as statements — but it is a coverage hole in exactly the guard two fix rounds were spent widening. A second-order effect of the same question: the issue's `meta.line` (line 96) would point at the inner `%{` rather than at the literal the user actually has to close.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:190 — `repair/1` is quadratic-ish in reparses and the expensive path is the one that commits. For each candidate earlier line, `sole_placement/3` pays up to 5 whole-source `Code.string_to_quoted` calls in `close_at/2` plus up to 4x5 more in `split_placement?/3` — 25 reparses of the entire file per line — and `Enum.any?/2` only short-circuits when the input is ambiguous, i.e. when the rule *refuses*. `Credence.Syntax` then calls `analyze/1` and `fix/1` separately, so the whole scan is paid twice. An LLM-emitted attribute map spanning a few hundred lines whose lines do not end in `,` (values on their own continuation lines) therefore costs several thousand full-file parses before the rule fires. Memoizing `close_at/2` per line, or capping the scanned span, would remove it.
- experiment: settles the concern above — `mix run -e 'IO.inspect(Code.string_to_quoted("defmodule E do\n  def foo do\n    x = {1\n      , %{a: 2\n  end\nend\n", columns: true))'` — if the error meta's `:line` is 4 (the inner `%{`) rather than 3 (the outer `{`), `sole_placement/3` starts its scan one line too late and line 3 is never tested as a competing placement.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — fix round 3 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] fixed — Confirmed exactly as the reviewer guessed. Running their experiment, `Code.string_to_quoted` on a literal whose `{` sit on two different lines reports `line: 4` — the inner `%{` — not line 3, where the literal starts. The guard that checks "could the `}` have belonged on an earlier line?" started its scan at that reported line, so every line between the outer `{` and the inner one was never tried. I then executed the rule to see whether that hole actually lets a wrong repair through: on `x = {1` followed by `++ %{a: 2`, the rule committed `{1 ++ %{a: 2}}` (a one-element tuple), while `{1} ++ %{a: 2}` — one brace on each line — parses just as well and is a different value. I confirmed both readings parse and that their syntax trees differ, so nothing downstream could have caught the wrong pick. A sweep of 560 generated truncated sources found 6 such ambiguous-but-committed repairs. Pinned with three tests that failed on the old code for exactly this reason: a fix test asserting that source comes back byte-for-byte unchanged, an analyze test asserting no issue is reported, and an analyze test asserting the issue points at the line the literal opens on rather than the inner `{` (the reviewer's second-order point — it really did report line 4). Fixed by walking back out through the parser's delimiter stack: each `}` appended at the last line closes one more opening, so re-asking the parser with one, two, ... braces already in place names each opening in turn, and the earliest line any of them reports is where the literal really opens. Both the uniqueness scan and the issue's line now start there. Proven: the 560-source sweep drops from 6 ambiguous commits to 0, and a differential against the previous commit over 1960 generated sources shows 1924 byte-identical, 20 newly refused (all the finding's shape) and 16 with the same repair and only the reported line moved to the outer `{` — nothing newly repaired, no repair changed. Full battery 40 tests green, fast gate green (9031 tests, 0 failures). Committed as 4375ba5.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed by measurement, not by reading: repairing a multi-line map takes 0.14s at 200 lines, 0.52s at 400 and 2.2s at 800 — quadratic, and it is the *committing* path that pays, because the scan only stops early when it decides to refuse. The cause is what the reviewer described: for each earlier line the scan tried every brace count on that line, then every count on the last line for each of four shares — up to 25 reparses of the whole file per line. The fix rests on an argument the code now states: a competing reading closes the same openings, so it appends exactly as many braces as the repair did, and only their *share* between the two lines can differ. Trying just those shares is a couple of reparses per line instead of 25. One case the argument misses, which a differential caught before it shipped: a line ending in a comment, string or heredoc swallows the braces appended to it, and the old code refused those sources — nine of them in the corpus started being repaired. That refusal is the safer answer (it stops `x = {1 # }` / `|> g()` from becoming `{1 |> g()}`), so I kept it exactly, with a one-brace probe on top of the full repair that only parses when the extra brace was swallowed. Proven: two differentials against the previous commit — 3220 and 144 generated sources, including heredoc, sigil, charlist and trailing-comment shapes — give identical fix output and identical issues in every single case, so nothing the rule used to repair or refuse changed. The 800-line input goes from 2.2s to 0.19s. Pinned with a timing test on an 800-line literal with a 1s limit (the old code takes 2.2s and fails it, the new code 0.19s and passes with room to spare), plus a regression test for the swallowed-brace refusal, which nothing in the battery covered. Note the change lowers the constant, not the order: the scan is still quadratic in the length of the literal, just ~12x cheaper. Fast gate green (9033 tests, 0 failures). Committed as 866df81.
- note: a comment anywhere on an earlier line of a multi-line literal stops the rule firing at all. Executed: `x = %{` / `  a: 1, # first` / `  b: 2` / `end` is left untouched, while the same source with the comment removed is repaired. This is long-standing behaviour (I preserved it byte-for-byte and it is now explicit and documented in `competing_placement?/4` rather than an accident of the old brace grid), and it is the conservative direction. But LLM-emitted maps often carry inline comments, so a maintainer may want to decide whether a swallowed brace on a line that is *not* the last one really counts as doubt, or whether the mask primitive should be used to tell "the append landed in a comment" from "the append landed in code".
- commits: 4375ba58f5f2e1bf75efd65b08ef0fd9b769aa54 866df81622a359e8d9640e37cc887e736ec572e2
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:223 — the "placement must be beyond doubt" guard only ever appends `}` to the *end* of lines *before* the target line, so it is structurally inert whenever the literal sits on one line: for `x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()` with `end` on the next line, `target_line/3` picks that same line (index == open_line - 1), the scan range `(start_line - 1)..(index - 1)//1` is empty, and the rule commits `x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()}` — even though `x = {1, 2} |> IO.inspect()` also parses and means something else. The rule's own moduledoc and test `test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:242` declare exactly this ambiguity must be refused; it is, but only when the pipe happens to be on the next line. The same hole applies to a mid-line `}` on the target line of a multi-line literal (`x = {1,` / `2 |> IO.inspect()` is committed for the same reason).
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:248 — `competing_placement?/4` enumerates only two-line shares of the `count` braces (earlier line + target line) for one earlier line at a time, so a competing reading that splits the braces across three or more lines of the literal is never tried and the repair would be committed. I could not construct a concrete input where a 3-way split parses and no 2-way split does (every candidate I tried was already caught by the pair enumeration), so this needs a human to judge whether the gap is reachable.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:330 — the reparse-blow-up guard is a wall-clock assertion (`best < 1_000_000` µs) sitting between a measured 0.19s and the 2.2s regression it exists to catch, so it both false-fails on a ~5x-loaded machine and passes any future regression under 5x. It also exercises only the `count == 1` shape with half the literal's lines skipped by the dangling-comma rule (~2 reparses/line); the real worst case is `@max_braces + 1` reparses per line (~6x the tested work), which nothing covers.
- experiment: settles the blocker — `mix run -e 'src = "defmodule E do\n  def foo do\n    x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()\n  end\nend\n"; IO.puts(Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace.fix(src))'` (if it prints `x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()}` the rule committed one of two readings; the two-line form of the same input is refused)

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:270 — "no issue for a brace that only appears inside a string" feeds the rule a source that parses cleanly, so it returns `[]` for the same trivial reason as "leaves code without braces alone" (the parser reports no mismatched-delimiter error at all). It therefore does not demonstrate the moduledoc's claim that a `{` inside a string/heredoc/comment is never taken for the unclosed one. An input that actually has an unclosed literal *plus* a brace inside a string on another line (e.g. `s = "{"` on the line above `x = {1, 2`) would exercise the claim; today nothing in either test file does.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:236 — "no issue when the mismatched end sits on the opening line" cannot go red if the guard it appears to pin is removed. With `end_line == open_line`, `target_line/3` builds the range `(end_line - 2)..(open_line - 1)//-1`, which for the equal case is a descending range whose first element is below its last — i.e. empty — so `Enum.find` returns `nil` and the rule refuses anyway, guard or no guard. The test is a correct behavioural assertion, but it is not the regression net for `end_line > open_line` (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:143) that its name suggests.
- experiment: settles the second nit — `sed -i 's/ and end_line > open_line do/ do/' lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex && mix test test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs; git checkout -- lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex` (the test file is expected to stay green with the guard deleted, which is the point).

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — The existing test "no issue for a brace that only appears inside a string" hands the rule a source that parses perfectly well, so the rule returns "no issues" for the same trivial reason as "leaves code without braces alone": the parser never reports a mismatched delimiter, and the masking claim in the rule's documentation — that a `{` inside a string, heredoc or comment is never mistaken for the unclosed one — is never actually exercised. Confirmed by executing the rule on five sources that really are missing a `}` and also carry a `{` inside a string, heredoc or comment; nothing in either test file covered any of them. Added four tests to this battery: a `{` inside a string on the line above the broken literal (the issue must name the literal's line, 4, not the string's line 3); six `{` inside a string on the literal's own line (only one `}` is missing, so the issue must appear at all); a `{` inside a heredoc above the literal; and a `{` inside a comment above it. Because these are coverage tests they pass on the current code, so I proved they are a real net rather than decoration: I made the rule brace-blind on purpose — finding the opening line by scanning the text for a `{` and taking the number of missing braces from the textual `{`/`}` imbalance — and all four went red for exactly the right reasons (the string case reported line 3 instead of line 4; the six-brace case reported nothing at all, because a textual count wants seven closers, past the rule's cap of five). The pre-existing test stayed green under that same mutation, which is precisely the gap the reviewer described. The mutation was then reverted; only the tests were kept. Committed as 50fe277.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed by running the reviewer's experiment: with `end_line > open_line` deleted from the rule's detection guard, both batteries stay green (42 tests, 0 failures), so the test named "no issue when the mismatched end sits on the opening line" is not the regression net for that guard. It cannot be, and the reason is worth stating: the range the rule scans for a line to put the `}` on runs from two lines above the `end` back to the opening line, which is non-empty exactly when the `end` really is on a later line. The guard and the scan were therefore one decision written down twice, and the second copy had no observable consequence. I backed that with a differential over 410 generated broken sources — including ten shapes where the `end` sits on the opening line — comparing both what the rule reports and what it rewrites, with and without the guard: 0 differences. I also checked how over-determined the behaviour is by removing the guard *and* widening the scan so the same-line case is reached: still 0 differences over the same 410 sources and still 42 green tests, because appending a `}` after `end` never parses anyway. So no black-box test can ever pin that guard. Landed the removal of the duplicate, moved the reasoning into a comment on the function that actually enforces it, and added a comment to the test saying it pins the behaviour rather than any one guard expression — the assertion itself is unchanged. Re-ran the 410-source differential against the pre-fix baseline after the change: 0 differences, so nothing the rule used to repair or refuse moved. Committed as 50fe277.
- note: not fixed, for the maintainer — the earlier fix rounds for the rule itself left two findings open in findings.md that are still live on this tree (a blocker at lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:223 about single-line literals such as `x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()` being committed even though the two-line form of the same input is refused, plus its companion concern and nit). They are outside this file's scope and I did not touch them, but they belong to the same rule these tests cover, and the new tests I added do not overlap with them.
- commits: 50fe277cbae551d74007c9f0d1b914ab41cc9382
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:231 — the "placement must be beyond doubt" guard can only ever test end-of-line placements on lines *before* the target line, so it is structurally inert whenever the literal opens on the line the `}` is appended to. On `defmodule E do` / `  def foo do` / `    x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()` / `  end` / `end`, `target_line/3` picks that same line (index == open_line - 1), the scan range `(start_line - 1)..(index - 1)//1` is empty, `sole_placement/4` returns `:ok` unconditionally, and the rule commits `x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()}` — the tuple `{1, IO.inspect(2)}` — although `x = {1, 2} |> IO.inspect()` parses just as well and is a different program. The identical ambiguity spread over two lines is refused (pinned by test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:242), so the rule's answer depends on where the author happened to break the line. The same hole covers any mid-line placement on the target line of a multi-line literal: `x = {1,` / `    2 |> IO.inspect()` / `end` commits for the same reason (the `x = {1,` line is skipped by the trailing-comma rule and no mid-line `}` is ever tried). The moduledoc's "Only one placement is faithful: at the very end of the literal's last line" (lines 26-30) states this as policy, but the second guard's worked `|>` example (lines 46-58) promises the rule stays silent for exactly this shape.
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:235 — the trailing-comma exemption in `sole_placement/4` is a raw text test (`String.ends_with?(line, ",")`) that short-circuits *before* `competing_placement?/4`, so it also skips the swallowed-brace probe at :256. An earlier line of the literal whose trailing *comment* ends in a comma is therefore treated as "pinned by a trailing comma" and never probed. On `defmodule E do` / `  def foo do` / `    x = {1 # oops,` / `    |> g()` / `  end` / `end`, the target line is `    |> g()`, one `}` makes the whole source parse, the only earlier line ends in `,` and is skipped, and the rule commits `x = {1 # oops,` / `    |> g()}` — i.e. `{1 |> g()}` — where the author's `{1} |> g()` is equally faithful. The same source with the comma removed from the comment is refused, and that refusal is a deliberate, tested guard (test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:293, whose own comment names this exact corruption). The comma reasoning quoted in the moduledoc ("closing after a trailing comma drops an element") only holds for a comma in code; nothing here distinguishes code bytes from comment bytes. The same text-level test at :174 is harmless because it only over-refuses.
- experiment: settles the first blocker — `mix run -e 'IO.puts(Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace.fix("defmodule E do\n  def foo do\n    x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()\n  end\nend\n"))'` (if it prints `x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()}` the rule committed one of two readings the two-line form is refused for)
- experiment: settles the second blocker — `mix run -e 'f = fn s -> IO.inspect(Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace.fix(s)) end; f.("defmodule E do\n  def foo do\n    x = {1 # oops,\n    |> g()\n  end\nend\n"); f.("defmodule E do\n  def foo do\n    x = {1 # oops\n    |> g()\n  end\nend\n")'` (the second, control, source must come back unchanged; if the first comes back with `}` appended to the `|> g()` line, a comma inside a comment flipped the rule from refusing to committing)

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:9 — no flagging test in this file ever reaches the rule's competing-placement probe, so the file cannot tell "refuses ambiguous placements" from "refuses everything". Traced all eleven tests that assert an issue: for nine of them (lines 9, 23, 35, 47, 105, 293, 308, 322, 340) `sole_placement/4`'s scan range `(start_line-1)..(index-1)` is empty, and for the other two (lines 59, 72) the sole earlier line ends in `,` and is short-circuited by the trailing-comma guard before `competing_placement?/4` is called. Making `competing_placement?/4` return `true` unconditionally would therefore leave this whole file green — the three negative tests (lines 88, 159, 175) would still pass, and no positive test would notice. The sibling `close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:117` ("closes a multi-line map on its last entry") does exercise the probe returning false, which is why this is a nit and not a concern; the gap is that the analyze battery has no negative control of its own. One test fixes it: the same multi-line-map shape (`%{` alone on line 3, `a: 1,`, `b: 2`, `end`) asserting `meta: %{line: 3}` runs the probe on line 3 and expects it to decline.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:274 — "no issue for a brace that only appears inside a string" feeds the rule a source that parses cleanly, so `detect/1` returns `:none` before any brace reasoning happens; it returns `[]` for exactly the same reason as "leaves code without braces alone" two tests above, and proves nothing about a `{` inside a string. The file's own header comment at line 288 admits this ("The test above only shows that a source which parses is left alone"), and the four real masking tests added below it do the work. Rename it to say what it pins (a source that parses is left alone) or fold it into the neighbouring valid-source tests, so the name stops reading as masking coverage.
- experiment: (settles nit 1) mix run -e 'IO.inspect Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace.analyze("defmodule Example do\n  def foo do\n    %{\n      a: 1,\n      b: 2\n  end\nend\n")' — this is the input whose earlier line (`    %{`, no trailing comma) actually invokes `competing_placement?/4`; it should print one issue with `line: 3`, which is the missing positive control.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs — fix round 3 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — The reviewer said no flagging test in this battery ever reaches the rule's "is there a rival place for the `}`?" probe, so the file could not tell a rule that refuses genuinely ambiguous placements from one that refuses everything. Confirmed by executing that mutation: I made `competing_placement?/4` return `true` unconditionally (so every earlier line of a literal counts as a rival placement) and the whole battery stayed green — 24 tests, 0 failures. Also ran the reviewer's experiment on the untouched rule: the multi-line map `%{` / `a: 1,` / `b: 2` / `end` does reach the probe and is flagged on line 3. Added that source as a new flagging test ("flags a multi-line map whose opening line is no competing placement"), with a comment explaining it is the positive control: line 3 holds the `%{` alone, so the rule really does try putting the `}` there, and `%{}` on line 3 strands the two entries and does not parse, which is why the probe declines and the repair stands. Watched it fail under the mutation for exactly that reason (it got `[]` where it wanted an issue on line 3), then reverted the mutation (`git diff lib/` empty) and watched it pass. Rule code unchanged; only the test file moved.
- [2 nit] fixed — The test named "no issue for a brace that only appears inside a string" fed the rule `IO.puts("{")` inside an otherwise valid module, which parses perfectly well, so the rule gives up in its first step and never looks at a brace at all — it returns nothing for the same trivial reason as "leaves code without braces alone" two tests above, and the name read as if it covered the rule's claim about braces hidden in strings. Confirmed twice by execution: `Code.string_to_quoted` on that exact source returns `{:ok, _}` and `analyze` returns `[]`, identical to the brace-free control; and when I deliberately made the rule blind to strings, heredocs and comments (finding the literal's opening line by scanning the text for a `{` instead of asking the parser), the three real masking tests added in the previous round went red while this test stayed green. Reverted that mutation and renamed the test to "leaves a source that parses alone, brace inside a string and all", with a comment saying it pins only that a parsing source is untouched and pointing at the four tests below it that do the masking work. The assertion and the source are unchanged, so nothing that was covered stopped being covered.
- note: not fixed, for the maintainer — the two blockers raised against the rule itself (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:231 and :235, recorded in findings.md) are still open on this tree: a single-line `x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()` is still repaired even though the identical ambiguity spread over two lines is refused, and a trailing comma inside a *comment* still short-circuits the rival-placement probe. Both are outside this file's scope and I did not touch them; the positive control I added does not overlap with either.
- commits: d403cfc2642c3f42ce36eba8ef8f6b436ad2dc09
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:163 — the section header "deliberately skipped: no single faithful placement for the `}`" heads ten tests, but only three of them (lines 165, 181, 197) are refused for placement ambiguity. The other seven are refused for unrelated reasons: a dangling comma drops an element (214), the nesting is past the @max_braces depth cap (228), no single-literal repair makes the whole source parse (242), there is no line between the `{` and the `end` (258), no candidate parses because a comment swallows the brace (272), the mismatch is not a `{`/`end` one at all (284), and the source already parses (296). A maintainer reading the header will attribute the depth cap and the unrelated-error skip to ambiguity reasoning they do not implement. Moving the header down to sit only over 165-212, or rewording it to "deliberately skipped, each for its own reason", fixes it.

Reviewed in full and found sound otherwise:
- Every flagging assertion (9, 23, 35, 47, 59, 72, 88, 127, 320, 335, 349, 367) and every silence assertion was traced by hand through detect/1 -> target_line/3 -> close_at/2 -> outermost_open_line/4 -> sole_placement/4; the reported line and the empty-vs-non-empty scan ranges match what each test asserts, and each comment's stated reason matches the branch that actually decides.
- Class 1 (inert rule): 12 flagging tests, so the check demonstrably fires; the @max_braces boundary is pinned in both directions (47 flags at five closers, 228 is silent at six), so lowering or raising the cap turns one of them red.
- Class 4 (text-vs-meaning): no `=~`, `String.contains?` or `Regex.match?` anywhere; positives are single-element list patterns on `%Issue{rule:, meta: %{line:}}` and negatives are exact `== []`. No vacuous `Enum.all?` batteries.
- Class 5 (`defmodule Example` races): every `defmodule Example` is inside a heredoc handed to `analyze/1`; nothing is defined at top level, so no cross-file collision.
- Class 6 (compiling is running): `analyze/1` reaches only `Code.string_to_quoted/1,2`. No fixture string is ever executed, so the bounded-compile requirement does not apply here.
- The positive control at line 88 is load-bearing, and its comment's claim checks out: in every other flagging test the sole_placement scan range is either empty or contains only a comma-terminated line, so a probe that returned "ambiguous" unconditionally would leave the rest of the battery green. This test is the one that goes red.
- The four masking tests (320-378) do what their header claims — each feeds a source that really is missing a `}` while carrying a `{` in a string, heredoc or comment, and pins the line in code. The pre-existing test at 296 explicitly disclaims being masking coverage rather than over-claiming, which is the right call.
- The comment at 258 correctly names target_line/3's empty scan range as the enforcer of "the `end` must be on a later line", matching the guard that commit 50fe277 removed from detect/1 as a duplicate.
- The `sole_placement` swallow probe (`or parses_with?(lines, earlier, 1, index, count)`) has no analyze-side test, but it is covered on the fix side ("leaves an earlier line that swallows the brace in a comment untouched") and both callbacks share `repair/1`, so this is not a two-copies-of-one-predicate gap.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:258 — the ambiguity battery only pins splits between ONE earlier line and the target line ("leaves a placement split across two lines untouched" is a 1+1 over two lines). The rule's `competing_placement?` (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:253) enumerates exactly those pairs — `taken` on one earlier line, `count - taken` on the target — so a rival reading that puts braces on two *different* earlier lines (e.g. 1 on line A, 1 on line B, 0-or-more on the target) is never tried. The rule's own comment at lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:242 asserts "those `count` shares are the whole alternative set", which holds only for two-line splits. If such a source exists, the rule commits one of two parsing meanings — the exact failure this rule promises never to make — and no test in this file would notice. I traced four candidate three-opening sources by hand and the pair scan caught the ambiguity in every one, so this is unverified by reading rather than demonstrated; it needs the search below.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:318 — the fixture comment says the entries are "each spread over two lines so no line ends in `,` but the last", but 399 of the 800 generated entry lines DO end in `,` (the value lines, `        1,`). The property that actually makes this test non-vacuous is the opposite one: splitting each entry over two lines creates 400 *key* lines (`      k1:`) that do not end in `,`, and only those get probed — `sole_placement` skips every comma-ended line (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex:235), so a one-line-per-entry fixture would make the scan do zero reparses and the timing assertion would measure nothing. Worth saying that, since the comment is the only thing telling a maintainer why the fixture has this shape.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:330 — `assert best < 1_000_000` is a wall-clock threshold against a claimed ~190ms baseline, i.e. ~5x headroom, and the work it times is ~800 whole-file reparses of an 805-line source. min-of-3 and a non-`async` module (so it runs alone, after every async module) keep the flake risk low, but a CI box 3-5x slower than the machine the 190ms was measured on lands at or past the limit, and there is no tag to exclude it — the repo already has a `@moduletag :corpus` convention for tests that are excluded by default. Either tag it or state the measuring machine next to the number.
- experiment: settles the three-line-split concern — searches for a source where the rule commits a repair even though a split across two earlier lines also parses; prints nothing if the pair scan is in fact complete over this fragment space. `mix run -e 'alias Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace, as: R; f = ["{1", "{1, {2", "%{a: {2", ", {3", ", 4", "|> g()", "}, 5"]; for a <- f, b <- f, c <- f, src = "defmodule Example do\n  def foo do\n    x = #{a}\n    #{b}\n    #{c}\n  end\nend\n", R.fix(src) != src do l = String.split(src, "\n"); alts = for i <- 1..3, j <- 1..3, k <- 0..3, s = l |> List.update_at(2, &(&1 <> String.duplicate("}", i))) |> List.update_at(3, &(&1 <> String.duplicate("}", j))) |> List.update_at(4, &(&1 <> String.duplicate("}", k))) |> Enum.join("\n"), match?({:ok, _}, Code.string_to_quoted(s)), do: s; if alts != [], do: IO.puts("rule committed, yet a two-earlier-line split also parses:\n" <> src) end'`

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] refuted — The concern was that the rule only ever weighs the missing braces shared between ONE earlier line and the target line, so a rival reading that puts braces on two *different* earlier lines is never tried; if such a source exists the rule silently picks one of two meanings. I searched for one instead of reasoning about it. First the reviewer's own experiment (343 sources built from their 7 fragments): nothing. Then a much wider search — every three-line function body built from 26 fragments covering nested openings, map/keyword entries, leading commas, operator continuations, a closing brace mid-line, and trailing comments/strings that swallow an appended `}` = 17,576 sources, of which the rule repairs 351; plus a four-line variant, 38,416 sources with 181 repairs. For each repair I enumerated every way of spreading the same number of closing braces over the body's lines using at least two of them (a completion always appends exactly as many `}` as there are `{` still open, since a `}` closes exactly one, so rivals with a different total are not readings of the same source) and parsed each. Zero rivals, in every run — including zero *pair* rivals, which would have been a bug in the scan the rule does perform. The defect does not reproduce. What was real is the second half of the finding — nothing in the file would have noticed — so the search is now a standing test in the battery ("commits nothing that a brace split over other lines could also mean", commit 2cb9b0a). It is not inert: with the rule's scan narrowed to a single share (a scratch copy, not committed) the same search reports 22 rivals, e.g. `x = {1, {2, {3` / `|> g()` / `}, 5`, where the rule would have emitted `}, 5}}` while `|> g()}` … `}, 5}` also parses. The test also asserts the search made the rule commit at least 100 times, so it cannot pass by finding nothing to check.
- [2 nit] fixed — The fixture comment said the 400 entries are spread over two lines "so no line ends in `,` but the last". Confirmed false by counting: 399 of the 800 generated lines end in `,` (the value lines). Confirmed what the shape is actually for by timing the alternative: with one line per entry the rule repairs the source in 1.5ms against 193ms for the two-line version, because `sole_placement` skips every comma-ended line, so a one-line-per-entry fixture would make the scan reparse nothing and the timing would measure an empty loop. The comment now says that, and the property is pinned by two assertions in the test rather than living only in prose: 400 key lines (which end in `:`, never `,`, and are the only lines the scan probes) and 399 of the 400 value lines ending in `,`. Reshaping the fixture to one line per entry now fails the first assertion instead of quietly hollowing out the measurement. Commit 7c575ac.
- [3 nit] fixed — `assert best < 1_000_000` is wall-clock against a ~190ms baseline. Confirmed the flake in both directions by executing. Slow-machine direction: with 48 busy loops on 24 cores the same measurement took 647ms and 781ms against the 1000ms limit, so a box only ~1.3x slower than that fails on healthy code. Missed-regression direction: a scratch copy of the rule with the old every-count scan restored (not committed; it emits byte-identical output, only slower) takes 2.6s, so a machine ~2.6x faster than this one would pass the regression the test exists to catch. The threshold is now measured in reparses of the same fixture rather than in milliseconds: the test times 400 whole-file reparses of the fixture the same way (min of 3) and allows the scan six of those, i.e. ~2400 reparses. Healthy scan measures 1.29-1.31x the control (~520 reparses), the restored every-count scan measures 17.5x (~7000), and the ratio held at 1.24-1.35 under the same load that pushed wall-clock to 781ms. So the assertion now means the same thing on a slow CI box as on a fast laptop, and no tag or machine footnote is needed. Commit 7c575ac.
- note: the parser calls in the two new/changed tests go through `valid_syntax?/1` from `Credence.RuleCase`, not `Code.string_to_quoted/1`, because `test/no_parser_calls_in_rule_tests_test.exs` forbids direct `Code.*`/`Sourceror.*` references in rule tests. `valid_syntax?/1` is `Sourceror.parse_string/1`, which is heavier than a plain parse on *successful* parses; for this fixture (which fails to parse) the two cost the same, so the calibration is unaffected, but a future perf test whose control parses valid source should know the control is a Sourceror parse, not a bare `Code.string_to_quoted/1`.
- commits: 2cb9b0add566ce35c12271c41b3bd1767dea8f2d 7c575ac8738d70c3f9292f346d091aa2e9322620
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:335 — the split-search test guards vacuity with `length(repaired) > 100`, but a repaired source can only produce a rival when its repair needed **two or more** braces: `brace_splits(count, 3)` demands `sum == count` with at least two non-zero lines, so it returns `[]` for every `count == 1` source and that source is searched against nothing. The guard counts the wrong quantity — if the fragment list ever drifted to single-`{` shapes (or the rule started refusing all nested cases), every one of the 15,625 sources could repair with one brace, `repaired` would be in the thousands, `rivals` would be empty because nothing was ever searched, and the test would stay green while proving nothing about the class its comment names. Guard on the number of repairs with `byte_size(fixed) - byte_size(source) >= 2`, or on the number of rival completions actually parsed.
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:310 — this test runs the rule over 25³ = 15,625 sources (each `fix/1` costs several whole-file parses) and then reparses up to ~18 rival completions per repair, and test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:415 times four `fix/1` calls plus 1,200 Sourceror parses of an 804-line fixture — both under ExUnit's default 60-second per-test timeout, with no `@tag timeout:`. Every other exhaustive test in this repo sets `@moduletag timeout: :timer.minutes(10)` (test/fix_meta_test.exs:32, test/pipeline_witness_test.exs:72). On a CI box a few times slower than the machine these were written on, the failure mode is a timeout that reads like a hang, not a regression signal.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:421 — the stated budget arithmetic does not add up, and the failure message at :459 repeats it. `sole_placement` probes the 400 key lines *and* the `    x = %{` line = 401 lines, two reparses each = ~802, not "~520". The comment's explanation ("the scan stops at the first share that parses") cannot apply here: a share that parses is exactly a competing placement, which makes the rule refuse — and the refusal is ruled out by `assert fix(code) != code` at :456. The real gap is the unit: `control` times `valid_syntax?` (Sourceror.parse_string, which also merges comments), while the scan reparses with `Code.string_to_quoted`, so a "reparse of the fixture" in the message costs more than a reparse in the scan. The 6× budget still fails the ~7000-reparse regression, so the gate works; the number a maintainer is handed when it goes red is the thing that is wrong.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:513 — every refusal in the analyze test is mirrored here as a byte-for-byte "left untouched" case (dangling comma, two literals, same-line `end`, comment swallow, unrelated error, all three ambiguity shapes) except the `@max_braces` backstop, which the analyze test covers at test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:228. Low risk since both callbacks route through `repair/1`, but the fix side has no case pinning that a six-brace nesting comes back unchanged.
- experiment: settles the first finding — `mix run -e 'f = ["{1", "{1, {2", "{1, {2, {3", "%{a: {2", "%{a: 1", "%U{a: 1", "%{a: %{b: 2", ", {3", ", 4", ", %{b: 5", ", b: 5", ", [1", ", fn -> 1 end", "|> g()", "}, 5", "}, {6", "} ++ [1]", "} = y", "+ 1", "5", "b: 6", "{}", "# c", "\"s\" <> t", "x", "3 => 4"]; s = for a <- f, b <- f, c <- f, do: "defmodule Example do\n  def foo do\n    x = " <> Enum.join([a, b, c], "\n    ") <> "\n  end\nend\n"; s |> Enum.map(&(byte_size(Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedBrace.fix(&1)) - byte_size(&1))) |> Enum.frequencies() |> IO.inspect(label: "braces appended => sources")' — anything other than a healthy count at 2+ means the rival search is searching almost nothing.
- experiment: settles the unit mismatch in the third finding — `mix run -e 'code = "defmodule Example do\n  def foo do\n    x = %{\n" <> Enum.map_join(1..400, "\n", fn i -> "      k#{i}:\n        #{i}#{if i < 400, do: ",", else: ""}" end) <> "\n  end\nend\n"; a = elem(:timer.tc(fn -> Enum.each(1..50, fn _ -> Code.string_to_quoted(code) end) end), 0); b = elem(:timer.tc(fn -> Enum.each(1..50, fn _ -> Sourceror.parse_string(code) end) end), 0); IO.inspect({a, b, b / a}, label: "code_vs_sourceror")' — the ratio is the factor by which the message's "reparses" number understates the scan's real reparse count.
- experiment: settles the timeout finding — `mix test test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs --seed 0 --trace` prints each test's wall time; the two tests above are the ones to compare against the 60s default.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] fixed — The big search test ("commits nothing that a brace split over other lines could also mean") guarded against becoming vacuous by asserting that the rule repaired more than 100 of the 17,576 generated sources. That is the wrong quantity: a source the rule repairs by adding a single `}` has no rival reading at all, because one brace cannot be spread over two lines, so `brace_splits/2` returns nothing for it and it is compared against nothing. Confirmed by execution, not by reading: I ran the same test with the fragment list drifted to shapes that never nest (a scratch copy, not committed) — 141 of 5832 sources were repaired, the `> 100` guard passed, the test went green, and ZERO alternative readings had been parsed. Counted the real numbers too: of the 351 repairs the committed fragment list produces, 152 need one brace (searched against nothing) and 199 need two or three. The guard now counts the alternative completions actually handed to the parser: 785 with the real fragments, and the assertion demands more than 500. Re-running the drifted list against the new guard fails with "only 0 rival completions were parsed", and the real file stays green. `rival_readings/2` became `rival_completions/2` (it no longer filters to the ones that parse — the caller does that), so a completion that was searched but did not parse still counts as search performed. Commit 8b97283.
- [2 concern] refuted — The claim was that the two heavy tests run under ExUnit's 60-second default with no `timeout:` tag, so a slower CI box would fail with a timeout that reads like a hang. Measured them instead of estimating. Alone (`mix test test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs --trace --formatter ExUnit.CLIFormatter`): the search test 2.13 s, the reparse-budget test 1.22 s. Inside the whole default gate (`mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency --trace`, 9045 tests, 294 s), where the reviewer's starvation worry would show: 2.11 s and 1.17 s — no slowdown at all, and the suite's genuinely slow tests (the ones that do carry `@moduletag timeout: :timer.minutes(10)`) run 21–24 s. Under deliberate CPU starvation — 48 busy loops on 24 cores, the same load that made an earlier round's wall-clock assertion fail — they take 8.2 s and 4.0 s. So the worst number I could produce is 8.2 s against a 60-second budget: a box would have to be another ~7× slower *while* 3× oversubscribed to time out. The defect does not reproduce, so I did not add the tag. Worth a maintainer's eye if CI ever gets much slower: the search test's cost is cubic in the fragment list (26³ sources today), so adding fragments is what would move this number, not a slower box.
- [3 nit] fixed — The comment said the scan does "~520 reparses (two per probed line), since the scan stops at the first share that parses", and the failure message repeated the 520. I traced `Code.string_to_quoted` through one `fix/1` call on the fixture: 804 parses — one for detection, one for the repair, and 802 for the scan, which is two each over 401 probed lines (the 400 key lines plus `    x = %{`). So "two per probed line" is 802, not 520, and nothing stops the scan early: a probe that parses IS a competing placement and would make the rule refuse, which the test's own `assert fix(code) != code` rules out. The reviewer's suspected cause — the control loop times `Sourceror.parse_string` while the scan uses `Code.string_to_quoted` — is not it: measured on this fixture the two cost the same (ratios 0.99 and 1.07), because the fixture fails to parse and Sourceror never reaches its comment-merging. The real reason 802 probes cost only about one control loop is that a probe's `}` sits on an earlier line, so the parser fails there and never reads the rest of the file: timed per position, a probe on the first key line costs 0.17 of a whole-file parse and one on the last costs 1.4. The comment and the failure message now state the probe count (802), the measured cost (about one control loop; 0.9–1.7 across runs, depending on which loop is timed first), and a re-measured regression figure — I replayed the pre-866df81 every-count scan (25 parses per probed line) over the same fixture and it costs 20 control loops, so the 6× budget still sits between healthy and broken. The assertion and the budget are unchanged; only the numbers a maintainer is handed changed. Commit 4b0066a.
- [4 nit] fixed — The fix battery mirrored every refusal the analyze battery pins except the `@max_braces` backstop, so nothing checked that `fix/1` hands a six-brace nesting back byte-for-byte. Confirmed by executing the rule on the six-opening shape: it does return the source unchanged, and no test in the file looked. Added both halves of the boundary, with the expected string taken from what the rule actually emitted rather than typed by hand: five closers are appended (`{:ok, %{a: %{b: %{c: %{d: 1}}}}}`) and six are refused. Proved neither case is inert by raising `@max_braces` from 5 to 6 in the rule (a scratch edit, reverted immediately — the file diffs byte-identical against a backup taken first): the refusal case then fails, showing the rule emitting six closers, while the five-brace case stays green. Commit 499ccff.
- note: (for the maintainer, not fixed) the reparse-budget test's control loop and the scan it is compared against drift by up to 40% depending on which of the two is timed first in a run — I measured the same healthy scan at 0.92, 1.12, 1.34 and 1.67 control loops across runs. The 6× budget absorbs that easily, and the every-count regression measures 20, so the gate is in no danger; but anyone tempted to tighten the budget towards 2× should know the noise floor is that wide.
- commits: 8b972837d8757fad2393608b593fdf9677a378c7 4b0066a78bb2445c5c8112af903f82502ce9ae3e 499ccfff5a1086d2a22c2def120fe67ee7d91e1d
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:429 — the two heavy tests in this module carry no raised timeout, and ExUnit's default is 60s per test. "scans a long literal without a blow-up in reparses" runs `fix/1` on an 803-line fixture four times (three timed plus the `!=` assert) and 1200 whole-file Sourceror parses in the control loops; by the file's own accounting one `fix/1` costs about one control loop, so the test spends roughly 3200 whole-file parses of that fixture. On a CI box a few times slower than the machine the numbers were measured on, that lands at or past the 60s cut and fails as a spurious `ExUnit.TimeoutError` rather than as a perf regression. Every other CPU-bound test file in this repo sets `@moduletag timeout:` for exactly this reason (test/no_parser_calls_in_rule_tests_test.exs:24 records the prior spurious timeout); this file sets none. The 15,625-source search at :310 sits in the same module and would be covered by the same tag.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:469 — the budget compares two different parsers. `best` times `fix/1`, which parses with `Code.string_to_quoted`; `control` times `valid_syntax?`, which parses with Sourceror (comments, token metadata and a literal encoder) and is therefore materially more expensive per parse of the same file. The comment at :461-468 explains the observed ~1 control loop entirely by probes failing early and never reading the rest of the file; the heavier control parse is a second, unmentioned contributor, and it means the 6× budget silently tracks Sourceror's overhead relative to the rule's parser (a dependency bump moves the ratio with nothing in the rule changing). The gate keeps working — 1 vs 20 has ample margin at 6 — but a maintainer re-tuning the number from this comment would be reasoning from half the cause. The test cannot call `Code.*` directly (test/no_parser_calls_in_rule_tests_test.exs forbids it), so the fix is to say this in the comment, not to change the control.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:227 — the comment says the skipped cases are "left byte-for-byte alone", and :563 says the test "pins that `fix/1` hands the source back byte-for-byte", but `confirm_fix/2` (test/support/rule_case.ex:96) trims trailing newlines from both sides by design. A regression that dropped or added the file's final newline would pass every assertion in this file, positive and negative alike. The rule is in fact newline-exact today (`String.split(source, "\n")` + `Enum.join("\n")` round-trips), so this is an overstated comment rather than an untested hazard — but it claims a guarantee no assertion here makes.
- experiment: settles the timeout concern — `mix test test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs --trace` prints each test's wall time, so the headroom under the 60s default is visible directly (and it confirms the search test at :310 and the perf test at :429 both pass, which reading alone cannot establish).

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs — fix round 3 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] refuted — The claim: the module's two heavy tests run under ExUnit's 60-second default with no `timeout:` tag, and on "a CI box a few times slower" they would blow it and read as a hang rather than a regression. Measured instead of estimated. `mix test test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs --trace --seed 0 --formatter ExUnit.CLIFormatter`: the brace-split search takes 2001 ms and the reparse-budget test 1191 ms (whole file 3.2 s, 25 tests green — which also settles the reviewer's "reading alone cannot establish that they pass"). Then the slow-box condition, made real rather than imagined: 48 busy loops on 24 cores, i.e. 3x oversubscription, gives 7267 ms and 4375 ms — the worst numbers I could produce, against a 60-second budget. The reviewer's own arithmetic points the same way: ~3200 whole-file parses of that fixture, and 400 Sourceror parses of it measure ~140 ms, so the parsing accounts for ~1.1 s — "a few times slower" is ~5 s, not 60. A box would have to be another ~8x slower *while* 3x oversubscribed. The defect does not reproduce, so no tag was added. (This is the third round the timeout concern has been raised and the second time it has been measured; round 2 got 8.2 s / 4.0 s under the same load, so the two independent measurements agree.) Worth a maintainer's eye only if the fragment list grows: the search test's cost is cubic in it (26^3 = 17,576 sources today), so that, not a slower box, is what would move this number.
- [2 nit] fixed — The budget's two sides do not use the same parser: `best` times `fix/1`, which reparses with `Code.string_to_quoted`, while `control` times `valid_syntax?/1`, which is a Sourceror parse (rule tests may not reference `Code.*` themselves — test/no_parser_calls_in_rule_tests_test.exs). The comment never said so, so "a control loop is 400 whole-file reparses of this fixture" quietly meant 400 parses by a *different* parser. Confirmed by timing both on the exact fixture, min-of-11 over 100 parses each: Sourceror costs 1.05-1.2x the plain parse. The reviewer's "materially more expensive" overstates it, and the reason is worth recording — the fixture fails at the `end`, so Sourceror never reaches its comment merging or literal encoder; with the same file's map closed so it parses, Sourceror costs 5.0x. So the second contributor is real but small, and sits inside the 0.9-1.7 run-to-run spread the comment already documents. The comment now states all of it: which parser each side uses and why, the measured 1.05-1.2x, the 5.0x on a parseable file, and the consequence — 6 is a coarse fence between a healthy 1 and a broken 20 precisely because pulling it towards 2 would start tracking Sourceror's overhead, which a dependency bump could move with nothing in the rule changing. Comment only; the assertion, the control and the 6x budget are byte-for-byte unchanged. Commit 793a353.
- [3 nit] fixed — The comments claim the source comes back "byte-for-byte", but every assertion in the file routes through `confirm_fix/2`, which trims trailing newlines off both sides by design (test/support/rule_case.ex:96). Confirmed by executing two scratch regressions on the rule (applied, measured, reverted; the rule diffs byte-identical against a backup taken first). Dropping the final newline — `fix/1` wrapped in `String.trim_trailing(_, "\n")` — leaves all eleven "left untouched" cases and both brace-cap cases green. Adding one to every repair leaves the positive cases green. The one test that does go red under either is the brace-split search, and it goes red for the wrong reason: it infers how many closers a repair added from `byte_size(fixed) - byte_size(source)`, so a stray newline makes it report the rule committing an ambiguous placement — a maintainer handed that message would look for a brace bug. New test "keeps the source's final newline exactly as it found it" compares raw bytes on a repair and on a refusal, and again on the same two sources with the final newline stripped, so it catches a lost newline and an added one; it fails under both regressions with the newline visible in the diff, and passes on the real rule. The two overclaiming comments (the "byte-for-byte alone" section header and the brace-cap refusal) now say where the newline is actually pinned. Commit 793a353.
- note: (for the maintainer, not fixed — repo-wide, outside this file's scope) `Credence.FixtureHealer.heal_dirs()` runs from test/test_helper.exs before the suite compiles and rewrites any `assert <fix call> == expected` under test/pattern, test/semantic and test/syntax into `confirm_fix(<fix call>, expected)` — on disk, silently, on every `mix test`. That means a byte-exact fix assertion cannot be written in the obvious shape anywhere in the rule test tree: I wrote `assert fix(input) == expected`, ran the suite, and found `confirm_fix(fix(input), expected)` in the file, with the newline guarantee gone and the test green. It is exactly the hazard finding [3] describes, made unfixable-by-the-obvious-means. The new test works around it by comparing tuples (`assert {fix(a), fix(b)} == {x, y}`), since the healer only recognises a fix call sitting directly on one side of the `==`; I verified the file comes back byte-identical from `mix test` in that shape. A maintainer may prefer the healer to leave assertions alone when the expected value's trailing newline differs from the actual's, rather than have every fix test in the repo silently opt out of newline-exactness.
- commits: 793a353a1a602dec02779169b453549e3cc54a27
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_brace_fix_test.exs:11 — no positive fix case where the line being repaired carries a `{` inside a string or comment. The analyze test pins such an input (`x = %{a: "{{{{{{"`, close_unclosed_brace_analyze_test.exs:335) but only asserts the reported *line*; nothing in this file pins the bytes `fix/1` emits for it. A regression that counted braces textually rather than taking the parser's answer would still be caught on the analyze side, but the fix side's byte-exact contract for masked input is unpinned. Adding one `confirm_fix` case with the expected `x = %{a: "{{{{{{"}` closes it.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — 2026-08-19 (unchanged_rule, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:73 — the rule's whole admission test is "the first non-blank line after `@doc \"\"\"` starts with `def`", which is also true of a *correctly closed* doc heredoc whose first content line is a `def` example. On such a file the fix inserts `\"\"\"` right after the opener, truncating the doc to empty, promoting the documented example to real code, and turning the doc's real closing `\"\"\"` into an *opening* heredoc that swallows everything down to the next bare `\"\"\"` line. That output can still parse — e.g. `@doc \"\"\"` / `def example do` / `:ok` / `end` / `\"\"\"` / `def a, do: 1` / blank / `@doc \"\"\"` / `Docs for b.` / `\"\"\"` / `def b, do: 2` becomes a module where `def a/0` has been eaten into a string literal and `def example/0` is a real function. Because the result parses, `ProgressGuard.verdict/4` short-circuits on `after_stage == :parses` (lib/syntax/progress_guard.ex:148) and `commit_or_roll_back/4` commits it, so neither the per-rule guard nor the round's all-or-nothing gate can see the deletion. The Syntax round only runs on source that does not parse, so reaching this needs one unrelated syntax error in the file — and if that error sits in the code the runaway heredoc swallows, swallowing it is precisely what makes the file parse again.
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:110 — `~r/^\s*def/` has no word boundary, so ordinary doc prose starting with the letters "def" counts as a `def` line: `@doc \"\"\"` / `  defaults to 0 when absent.` / `  \"\"\"` / `  def get(m), ...` is flagged and rewritten, as are `defines ...`, `deferred ...`, and code lines like `defaults = %{}`. This widens the blocker above from "doc containing a `def` example" to "doc whose first content line begins with those three letters". Adding `\b` (or `(?:def|defp|defmacro)\b`) narrows it but does not fix the blocker, which is about correctly-closed heredocs.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:116 — `closed_before_def?/1` can never return `true` at a call site that matters. Both it and `next_nonblank_is_def?/1` stop at the *same* first non-blank line, and that line cannot both match `^\s*\"\"\"` and `^\s*def`; so whenever `next_nonblank_is_def?` is true, `closed_before_def?` is false and `not closed_before_def?(...)` is a constant. The guard's name promises "the heredoc is closed before the def" — it does not check that, and a reader (or a later editor) will assume it does.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:63 — the only "leave it alone" fix test uses a doc body (`Finds the min and max.`) that could not trigger the rule under any reading. There is no test pinning `fix(x) == x` for a *correctly closed* `@doc \"\"\"` whose first content line is a `def` example or begins with "def…" — the exact input the blocker rewrites. Adding those two cases turns the blocker red.
- nit: test/syntax_round_safety_test.exs:131 — the round-safety case that `lib/syntax/progress_guard.ex:90` and `:232` attribute to this rule ("Observed: `CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc` closing an `@doc \"\"\"` that had swallowed the rest of the file") is exercised through a `String.replace` stunt double, not the real rule, and asserts with `=~` rather than a whole-string `==`. The real rule happens to produce the same bytes today, but nothing pins that: if it stopped firing on `@runaway_heredoc`, this test stays green while the documented interaction is gone.
- experiment: settles the blocker — `mix run -e 'src = ~s|defmodule Foo do\n  @doc """\n  def example do\n    :ok\n  end\n  """\n  def a, do: 1\n\n  @doc """\n  Docs for b.\n  """\n  def b, do: 2\nend\n|; out = Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix(src); IO.puts(out); IO.inspect({:parses?, match?({:ok, _}, Sourceror.parse_string(out)), :still_has_def_a?, String.contains?(Macro.to_string(elem(Sourceror.parse_string(out), 1)), "def a")})'`

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 blocker] refuted — Ran the reviewer's own experiment (a module whose `@doc """` is correctly closed and whose first content line is a `def` example). The rule does not touch it: `fix/1` returned the input byte-for-byte, `analyze/1` returned `[]`, and the output still contains `def a`. The finding's premise is that the rule admits a line when "the first non-blank line after `@doc \"\"\"` starts with `def`", but that is not what the code tests. `Enum.find_value/3` skips `false` as readily as `nil`, so neither of the rule's two scans stops at the first non-blank line — both run to the end of the file. The second one therefore asks "is there a `\"\"\"` line anywhere below?", and for a correctly closed heredoc the answer is always yes (its own terminator), so the rule declines. Measured directly on that input: first scan `true`, second scan `true`, guard admits `false`. To be sure the guard is what saves it, I re-ran the same fixture through a copy of the rule with the guard deleted: that copy produces exactly the corruption the finding describes (doc emptied, `def example` promoted to real code, the doc's own `"""` left opening a heredoc). So the damage is real but the shipped rule is protected against it — and finding [4] now pins that protection with tests.
- [2 concern] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule over source where the only "def"-looking line below an unclosed `@doc """` is not a definition. `defaults to 0 when the key is absent.` (ordinary doc prose) and `defaults = %{a: 1}` (a plain binding) both matched the bare `^\s*def`, so the rule reported an issue and inserted a closing `"""` immediately after the opener — emptying the doc and spilling its body into the module. In the `defaults = %{a: 1}` case the mangled output *parses*, so nothing downstream can catch it (a source that parses is never reverted). The reviewer's own two examples do not reproduce, because they keep a closing `"""` below and so hit the guard from [1]; the defect needs a file with no real definition below the opener. Pinned with two tests in `test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs` (watched both go red on the old rule, on the `analyze` assertion, which is the defect itself). Fix: replaced `~r/^\s*def/` with a `\b`-terminated alternation over every `def…` form Elixir defines, hoisted into one `@def_line` attribute used by both scans rather than kept as two copies. Committed 1a68448.
- [3 nit] refuted — The claim is that `closed_before_def?/1` can never return `true` where it is called, making `not closed_before_def?(...)` a constant. Measured on the exact input finding [1] is about, it returns `true` and is the sole reason the rule declines to rewrite that file (first scan `true`, second scan `true`, guard `false`; the real rule leaves the source unchanged). The premise — "both stop at the same first non-blank line" — is also wrong, for the `find_value` reason given in [1]: fed `["  Prose first.", "  def later, do: 1"]`, the def scan returns `true`, so it clearly did not stop at the prose line. The one true part of the finding is that the name promises something the function does not check; it tests "is there a triple-quote line anywhere below?". Since that misreading is what produced a false blocker against this rule, I renamed the two scans to `def_below?` / `closing_quotes_below?` and wrote the `find_value` semantics down next to them. No behaviour change — full suite (9047 tests) green before and after. Committed 3ebc97b.
- [4 nit] fixed — Added the two missing "leave it alone" cases to `test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs`: a correctly closed `@doc """` whose first content line is a `def` example (the reviewer's blocker input), and one whose first content line begins with the letters "def". Each asserts both `analyze == []` and that the fix returns the source unchanged. They pass on the shipped rule, which is finding [1]'s answer; to show they are not vacuous I ran the same fixture through a copy of the rule with the `closing_quotes_below?` guard removed, and it mangles it exactly as the blocker predicted — so if that guard is ever dropped, these tests go red. Committed with [2] in 1a68448.
- [5 nit] fixed — Confirmed the gap by pointing the rule's opener regex at a name that cannot match: the existing round-safety case stayed green (it only exercises the `String.replace` stunt double), so nothing at all pinned that the real rule still fires on `@runaway_heredoc`. Added a test in `test/syntax_round_safety_test.exs` that runs the real `CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc` through `fix_with_trace` and asserts it was applied once, that its output equals the expected bytes as a whole string, that those bytes are the same ones the stunt double produces, and that the result still does not parse (the repair moves the report off EOF to the fault the heredoc hid — which is the interaction the guard's docs credit to this rule). The new test goes red under the same regex sabotage. Also hoisted the expected source into `@runaway_heredoc_closed` and turned the existing `=~` assertion into a whole-string `==`. Committed 1d70ebc.
- note: the moduledoc says the fix inserts the closing quotes "immediately before the `def` line", but `fix/1` inserts them before the first *non-blank* line after the opener, whichever it is.
