On an unclosed `@doc """` that has real prose before the def — `@doc """` / `Finds the min and max.` / `def find_min_max(list), do: ...` — the closing quotes land above the prose, so the doc is emptied and its text becomes module-body code; the result does not parse, so the round's guard throws the whole repair away. The rule only repairs docs that are empty to begin with. Not fixed: outside the numbered findings.
- commits: 1a684489441e61b47887e01b451649860e4ccdc9 1d70ebc5ffd187bf7ab3d286332400d43203d5be 3ebc97b7fa0bd80ec814407d4db0d6a841221a05
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — 2026-08-19 (modified, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:83 — the test that admits the input and the place the fix edits are two different lines. `def_below?`/`closing_quotes_below?` scan to end of file (a `-> false` branch in `Enum.find_value/3` means "keep looking", as lines 115-119 now correctly state), so the rule fires whenever there is a `def` *anywhere* below and no `"""` anywhere below — but `find_next_nonblank_offset/1` then inserts the closing `"""` before the **first non-blank line**. Whenever the unclosed doc actually has text in it (the shape the moduledoc itself describes: the LLM wrote the doc and forgot the closer), those differ and the terminator lands above the doc text instead of above the `def`. On `defmodule Sample do\n  @doc """\n  1 + 1\n\n  def get(m, k), do: Map.get(m, k)\nend\n` the output is `@doc """` / `"""` / `1 + 1` / `def get…`: the doc is emptied and its content becomes live module-body code. That output *parses*, so neither guard can catch it — ProgressGuard sees tokenize_error → parse_error (rank up, always `:keep`), and the phase-level all-or-nothing gate only rolls back sources that still fail to parse. This is the exact failure mode the new comment on line 38-44 claims the `\b` prevents; the `\b` only narrows the no-`def`-at-all case, not this one. When the doc text does not parse (`Finds the min and max.`) the same rewrite is kept per-rule and then discarded with the whole round, so the rule silently repairs nothing on its commonest realistic input — and if another rule repairs the remaining error in the same round, the emptied doc commits. Pre-existing in shape (base `next_nonblank_is_def?` had the same whole-file scan), but this PR documents the whole-file scan as intended without reconciling the insertion point. Correct behaviour is to insert immediately before the line that matched `@def_line`, not before the first non-blank line.
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:11 — no test in either test file exercises an unclosed `@doc """` that has doc text before the `def`. Every firing fixture (lines 11, 36, 155, 168, 203) has `def` as the literal first non-blank line, so the insertion point and the admission condition coincide and the blocker above is invisible. The three new negative tests (lines 124, 141, 106) pin only the "no real `def` below" half. A firing test with prose-then-`def`, asserting the closer lands under the prose, would pin the rule's stated purpose ("closes a doc *before the next definition*").
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:143 — `Regex.match?(@def_line, next_line) -> false` in `closing_quotes_below?` is dead: `false` and the `true -> false` catch-all are indistinguishable to `Enum.find_value/3`, so this clause has no effect on any input. The PR edits it (swapping `~r/^\s*def/` for `@def_line`) as if it were load-bearing. It reads as "stop scanning at the next definition", which the function does not do. Delete the clause, or make it a real bound.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:43 — "longer spellings come first so `defmacrop` is not read as `defmacro` followed by a stray `p`" is not what the pattern does: the list starts with the *shortest* alternative (`p`), and the trailing `\b` makes ordering irrelevant anyway — `defmacro` + `\b` fails between `o` and `p`, and the engine backtracks to `macrop`. The rationale as written would mislead anyone editing the alternation.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:138 — because `closing_quotes_below?` looks for a `"""` line anywhere below, a file with an unclosed `@doc """` on `def a` followed by a correctly closed `@doc """` on `def b` is never repaired: the second doc's closer vetoes the first doc's fix. The comment presents this guard as protecting correctly-closed docs; it also declines a whole class of genuinely broken files. Worth stating in the moduledoc as a known limitation.
- experiment: settles the blocker — prints the emitted source and its parse status; expect the `"""` above `1 + 1` and `:ok` (a parseable, silently-emptied doc that no guard reverts): mix run -e 'out = Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix("defmodule Sample do\n  @doc \"\"\"\n  1 + 1\n\n  def get(m, k), do: Map.get(m, k)\nend\n"); IO.puts(out); IO.inspect(elem(Code.string_to_quoted(out), 0))'

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule over the reviewer's input (`defmodule Sample do` / `@doc """` / `1 + 1` / blank / `def get(m, k), do: Map.get(m, k)` / `end`). The emitted source put the closing `"""` on the line directly under the opener: the doc came out empty, `1 + 1` became live module-body code, and `Code.string_to_quoted` on that output returned `:ok`, so nothing downstream would ever revert it. The cause was two different lines doing two jobs — the admission test asked "is there a definition anywhere below?", while the insertion point was "the first non-blank line below", and those coincide only when the doc has no text in it. Pinned with two firing tests in `test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs`: one with prose before the `def`, one with the reviewer's parseable `1 + 1`; both compare the whole emitted string and both were watched failing on the old rule with the terminator exactly one line too high. Fix: a single `def_offset/1` now answers both questions — it returns the index of the first line that matches `@def_line`, `nil` when there is none — so the terminator lands immediately above the definition and the admission test cannot drift from the edit again. `def_below?/1` and `find_next_nonblank_offset/1` are gone. Verified on the string the rule actually emitted: it parses, the `@doc` attribute still holds `"1 + 1\n\n"`, `def get/2` is still a real function, re-running the fix on that output changes nothing, and running the rule's own fix over its own source file still returns it byte-identical (the self-corruption check). Committed 4f8d003.
- [2 concern] fixed — This is the missing-coverage half of [1]: every firing fixture had `def` as the literal first non-blank line, so the bug could not show. The two new firing tests are exactly the case the reviewer asked for — an unclosed `@doc """` with text before the `def`, asserting the closer lands *under* the text — plus a check that the repaired source parses and no longer flags. Committed with [1] in 4f8d003.
- [3 nit] fixed — Confirmed dead by execution, not by reading: I ran two copies of `closing_quotes_below?`, one with the `@def_line -> false` clause and one without, over all 1464 line-lists of length 0–3 built from a pool of representative lines (blank, `def`, `defp`, `defmacrop`, `"""`, `@doc """`, prose, `end`, an assignment). Zero disagreements — `false` and the `true -> false` catch-all are the same thing to `Enum.find_value/3`, so the clause never changed an answer. Deleted it. The behaviour it *looked* like it implemented (stop scanning at the next definition) is now pinned by a test showing the scan does not stop there: a broken doc above `def a` followed by a correctly closed doc above `def b` is declined. Committed with [1] in 4f8d003.
- [4 nit] fixed — Confirmed the comment's claim is false by running the regex: with the alternation as written (shortest alternative `p` first) `defmacrop a` matches and the matched text is the whole `  defmacrop`, not `defmacro`; and a longest-first rewrite of the same alternation agrees with the shipped one on all 17 probe lines, including all 13 real `def…` forms and the four "def"-prefixed English/assignment lines it must reject. So ordering is irrelevant and the stated rationale would mislead an editor. The comment now says what actually keeps `defmacrop` whole: `\b` rejects any alternative that stops mid-word, and the engine backtracks to the spelling that ends on a word boundary. Committed with [1] in 4f8d003.
- [5 nit] fixed — Stated in the moduledoc under "Known limitation": because the `"""` scan runs to the end of the file, a file with a broken doc above `def a` and a correctly closed doc above `def b` is never repaired — the second doc's closing quotes veto the first doc's fix — and such a file is left to the next round. Also pinned with a test asserting that exact file is declined (`analyze == []`, `fix` returns it unchanged), so the trade-off is visible in the battery and not only in prose. Committed with [1] in 4f8d003.
- note: the moduledoc's Bad/Good example is a doc with *no* text (`@doc """` immediately followed by `def find_min_max`), which is the one shape where the old and new insertion points agreed. It is still correct, but it no longer illustrates the rule's stated purpose ("closes a doc before the next definition") as sharply as an example with doc text would. Not changed — the moduledoc examples are oracle inputs for the self-corruption check, so editing their bytes is the maintainer's call.
- commits: 4f8d00335195bcde5a798a0a7220b90cf742c82e
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — 2026-08-19 (modified, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:55 — `@def_line` is `^\s*def…\b`, so an *indented example* inside the doc text counts as "the next definition". On an unclosed doc whose body shows a `def` example — `defmodule S do` / `  @doc """` / `  Example:` / blank / `      def add(a, b), do: a + b` / `  def add(a, b), do: a + b` / `end` — `def_offset/1` returns the example line, and the closer is inserted above it: the doc is truncated to `"Example:\n\n"` and the documented example becomes a live second `add/2` clause. The output *parses*, so `ProgressGuard.verdict/4` keeps it and `commit_or_roll_back/4` commits it — the same silent, unrevertable damage class as the two blockers fixed in rounds 1 and 2, just entered through the insertion point instead of the admission test. No test in either test file feeds an *unclosed* doc that contains a `def`-shaped line in its body; the three "def example" fixtures (fix_test.exs:85, :106, :124) are all correctly closed docs, which the `"""` veto declines before the boundary is ever consulted. Anchoring the def scan to the indent already captured from the `@doc` line would exclude a deeper-indented example while still matching every fixture in the suite.
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:134 — because the scan stops only at a `def…` line, every module attribute between the doc text and the definition is swallowed into the doc. On `defmodule S do` / `  @doc """` / `  Adds.` / blank / `  @spec add(integer, integer) :: integer` / `  def add(a, b), do: a + b` / `end` the closer lands under the `@spec`, so the emitted module's `@doc` string ends with the spec text and the `@spec` attribute itself is gone. The result parses and compiles without a warning, so neither the per-rule guard nor the round's all-or-nothing gate can see the deleted contract. `@doc` → `@spec`/`@impl` → `def` is the ordering LLMs emit most often, and it is exactly the shape this rule exists to repair. Note this is a behaviour change from the base version, which declined this input outright (`next_nonblank_is_def?` saw the prose line and returned `false`): the PR trades "repairs nothing" for "repairs it wrongly, and the wrong answer ships".
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:106 — two `@doc """` openers with no `"""` anywhere below them can resolve to the *same* def line, and the reduce then inserts two closers at that one index. On `defmodule S do` / `  @doc """` / `  Docs for a.` / blank / `  @doc """` / `  More docs.` / `  def a, do: 1` / `end` both openers compute `insert_at = 6`, and the output has `"""` twice in a row: the first terminates the doc, the second opens a heredoc that runs unterminated to EOF. That output can never parse, so the phase-level gate discards the whole round — including repairs other syntax rules made to the same file. A single closer at that index would have produced a source that parses. Nothing in the suite covers two openers that share a def (fix_test.exs:252 gives each opener its own).
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:149 — the `String.trim(next_line) == "" -> nil` clause in `closing_quotes_below?` is inert for the same reason round 2 deleted the `@def_line -> false` clause beside it: `Enum.find_value/3` treats `nil` and `false` identically, so the blank-line branch and the `true -> false` catch-all cannot produce different answers. Its survival still suggests blank lines are handled specially. The function is `Enum.any?(lines, &Regex.match?(~r/^\s*"""/, &1))`, and saying that directly would retire the explanatory comment above it too.
- experiment: settles blocker 1 — prints the emitted source and its parse status; expect the `"""` above the indented example and `:ok` (doc truncated, example promoted to a real clause, nothing reverts it): mix run -e 'out = Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix(~s|defmodule S do\n  @doc """\n  Example:\n\n      def add(a, b), do: a + b\n  def add(a, b), do: a + b\nend\n|); IO.puts(out); IO.inspect(elem(Code.string_to_quoted(out), 0))'
- experiment: settles blocker 2 — expect the `@spec` line to sit inside the doc string and `:ok` for the parse: mix run -e 'out = Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix(~s|defmodule S do\n  @doc """\n  Adds.\n\n  @spec add(integer, integer) :: integer\n  def add(a, b), do: a + b\nend\n|); IO.puts(out); IO.inspect(elem(Code.string_to_quoted(out), 0))'
- experiment: settles the double-insertion concern — expect two consecutive `"""` lines above `def a` and an `:error` parse status: mix run -e 'out = Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix(~s|defmodule S do\n  @doc """\n  Docs for a.\n\n  @doc """\n  More docs.\n  def a, do: 1\nend\n|); IO.puts(out); IO.inspect(elem(Code.string_to_quoted(out), 0))'

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — fix round 3 (2026-08-19)
- [1 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule over the reviewer's input and printing the string it emitted: on `defmodule S do` / `  @doc """` / `  Example:` / blank / `      def add(a, b), do: a + b` / `  def add(a, b), do: a + b` / `end` the closing `"""` landed above the indented example. Parsing that emitted string back showed the damage exactly: the `@doc` held only `"Example:\n\n"` and the module had two `add/2` clauses instead of one — and it parses, so nothing downstream would revert it. Pinned with "does not close the doc above a def example indented inside the doc text" in the rule's fix battery, watched failing on the old code with the closer one line too high. Fix: the scan for the next definition is now anchored to the indentation already captured from the `@doc` line, so a `def` indented deeper than the `@doc` is doc text (an example), not the definition being documented. The emitted output now keeps the example inside the doc (`"Example:\n\n    def add(a, b), do: a + b\n"`) and leaves one `add/2` clause. Committed e626a92.
- [2 blocker] fixed — Confirmed the same way: on `@doc """` / `  Adds.` / blank / `  @spec add(integer, integer) :: integer` / `  def add(a, b), do: a + b` the closer landed *below* the `@spec`, and reading the emitted string back showed the doc string ending with the spec's text and no `@spec` attribute left in the module. That output parses and compiles without a warning, so the deleted contract is invisible to both the per-rule guard and the round's gate. Pinned with "closes the doc above an @spec that sits between the doc and its def", watched failing on the old code. Fix (same commit, same function): the doc now ends at the first module attribute at the `@doc`'s own indentation above the definition, if there is one. The emitted output now has the doc as `"Adds the two numbers.\n\n"`, the `@spec` intact and outside it, and the `def` untouched. Committed e626a92.
- [3 concern] fixed — Confirmed: two unclosed `@doc """` openers sharing one `def` both resolved to the same insertion index, and the emitted source had two `"""` lines stacked; `Code.string_to_quoted` on it returned `:error`, so the whole round — including any other rule's repairs to that file — would have been thrown away. Pinned with "gives each unclosed doc its own closer when both share the next def", watched failing on the old code with the two stacked closers. The fix for [1]/[2] resolves this without extra machinery: the second `@doc """` is itself a module attribute at that indentation, so it ends the first doc, and the two openers now get two different insertion points. The emitted output parses, each doc holds its own text (`"Docs for a.\n\n"` and `"More docs.\n"`), and `def a/0` survives. Committed e626a92.
- [4 nit] fixed — Confirmed the clause is inert by executing both versions side by side rather than by reading: the shipped three-branch `closing_quotes_below?/1` and the plain `Enum.any?(lines, &Regex.match?(~r/^\s*"""/, &1))` were run over 7,381 generated line lists built from blanks, whitespace-only lines, `"""` lines, `def` lines, prose and attribute lines — 0 disagreements, i.e. the blank-line `-> nil` branch cannot change any answer. The function is now that one line, and the comment above it no longer explains a `find_value` mechanism it no longer uses. Behaviour-preserving by construction, so no test can go red on it; the answer it must keep giving is pinned by a new test, "declines the repair when a blank line precedes the closing quotes below" (a doc whose first line is blank and whose closing quotes appear further down must still be declined), which passes before and after. Committed e626a92.
- note: the new attribute boundary is any line matching `@word` at the `@doc`'s own indentation. Doc *prose* that starts a line with a bare `@spec`/`@doc` at exactly the module's indentation would therefore be read as the end of the doc and truncate it. Prose normally writes such a mention inline or in backticks (a backtick is not an `@`), and the alternative — scanning past every attribute to the `def` — is finding [2], which deletes contracts. Flagged for the maintainer rather than narrowed to a whitelist of known attribute names, since a whitelist would not change this case and would miss real custom attributes.
- note: `test/support/rule_case.ex` gained one verb, `module_shape/1`, returning the `@doc` strings, `@spec`s and `def` clauses a source carries. It exists because the damage in findings [1] and [2] parses and compiles cleanly — only reading the emitted string's meaning back shows the truncated doc and the deleted spec — and rule tests are forbidden from touching `Code.*`/`Sourceror.*` directly by `test/no_parser_calls_in_rule_tests_test.exs`, which caught the first version of these tests doing exactly that.
- commits: e626a9239001fffa373e90fb89fb365ac72e0e0c
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — 2026-08-19 (modified, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:153 — `doc_end_offset/2` searches for the next definition all the way to end-of-file with nothing stopping it at the enclosing module's `end`, so on a two-module file whose first module has an unclosed `@doc """` and which contains no `"""` anywhere below (e.g. `defmodule Helpers do` / `  @doc """` / `  Shared helpers.` / `end` / blank / `defmodule Main do` / `  def run, do: :ok` / `end`) the closer is inserted before `def run` — inside the *second* module. The result is `defmodule Helpers do` with `@doc "Shared helpers.\nend\n\ndefmodule Main do\n"` and `def run/0` as its own function: module `Main` has been swallowed into a docstring and silently deleted. This passes every downstream gate — the two outdented lines inside the heredoc are only a *warning* in this Elixir (the repo's own `test/semantic/outdented_heredoc_check_test.exs` pins `severity: :warning`), the output parses under Sourceror, and `compiles_no_worse?/2` compares error sets, not warnings. This is new in this delta: the pre-PR `next_nonblank_is_def?` required the *first non-blank* line below the opener to be a `def`, so the old code declined this input outright and never spanned an outdented line.
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:35 — the moduledoc's "Known limitation" section records that `closing_quotes_below?/1` scans to EOF, but says nothing about `doc_end_offset/2` doing the same. A maintainer reading the moduledoc would reasonably believe the definition search is bounded by the module it started in; the `## Known limitation` text is the natural place to record the blocker above once its scope is decided.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:83 — the issue message still says the closing `"""` is "missing before the next `def`", but after this change the fix may insert before a module attribute (`@spec`, `@impl`, a second `@doc`) rather than before a `def`. The message no longer describes where the rule actually acts.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:280 — the `@spec` case is the only attribute stop that is tested, and only through `fix`. There is no test pinning the offset-0 attribute case (an attribute on the line immediately below the opener, which yields an empty `@doc ""`), and no analyze-side assertion that the attribute-stop shape is reported at all.
- experiment: settles the blocker — `mix run -e 'src = "defmodule Helpers do\n  @doc \"\"\"\n  Shared helpers.\nend\n\ndefmodule Main do\n  def run, do: :ok\nend\n"; out = Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix(src); IO.puts(out); IO.inspect(match?({:ok, _}, Sourceror.parse_string(out)), label: "parses")'` — if it prints the closer inside `defmodule Main do` and `parses: true`, the corruption is confirmed silent.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (modified, test_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:190 — the new "doc text must not become module-body code" battery only covers words that *begin with* the letters "def" (`defaults …`, `defaults = …`), which the rule's `\b` correctly rejects. It has no case for a doc line that begins with a *real* definition keyword. On `defmodule Sample do\n  @doc """\n  defstruct fields\n\n  def add(a, b), do: a + b\nend`, `doc_end_offset/2` returns 0 (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:153 — `defstruct` is in `@def_word` and `fields` gives it its word boundary), so the closer is inserted directly under the opener: the doc is emptied and `defstruct fields` becomes live module-body code. That output *parses*, so the per-rule progress guard keeps it and the round's all-or-nothing gate (lib/syntax.ex:151) commits it — exactly the silent corruption the new tests were added to prevent, and exactly the case the rule's own comment claims the `\b` rules out ("The trailing `\b` is what keeps English prose out", lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:53-57).
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:280 — the `@spec` test pins the *wanted* half of `@attribute_word`, but the cost the rule's own comment acknowledges (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:66-68: doc prose whose line starts with a bare `@word` at the doc's indent is read as the end of the doc) is unpinned. A doc line such as `  @timeout - how long to wait` truncates the doc above it and the emitted source still parses, so nothing downstream reverts it; a test would keep that cost visible if the regex is ever widened.
- experiment: settles the concern above — `mix run -e 'IO.puts Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix("defmodule Sample do\n  @doc \"\"\"\n  defstruct fields\n\n  def add(a, b), do: a + b\nend\n")'`

Notes on what was checked and found sound (not findings): every new expected string was re-derived by hand from the rule and matches byte-for-byte, including the `@spec`, indented-`def`-example, and two-openers cases; assertions use exact `==` via `confirm_fix/2` and `module_shape/1` rather than `=~`/`String.contains?`; each decline test is discriminating (removing the guard it names changes the outcome, so none are vacuous); no fixture is compiled or defines a top-level module, so failure classes 5 and 6 do not apply; end-to-end firing through the real round is proved in test/syntax_round_safety_test.exs:305; and running the rule's `fix/1` over its own source and over this test file is a no-op (no line matches `@doc_heredoc_open`), so the self-corruption oracle is clean.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] fixed — Confirmed by executing the reviewer's experiment: on a module whose doc reads `defstruct fields`, then a blank line, then `def add(a, b)`, the rule emitted the closing quotes directly under the `@doc """` opener — the doc came out empty and `defstruct fields` became live module-body code. That output parses, so nothing downstream can revert it. Pinned it with a new test ("declines when the doc's first line is a definition form @doc cannot document") in the file's own battery; it failed on the old code for exactly that reason (the rule reported an issue on line 2 where it should report none). The fix: `@doc` only ever attaches to a function-like definition — never to `defmodule`, `defstruct`, `defimpl`, `defprotocol`, `defexception` or `defoverridable` — so a line spelling one of those at the doc's own indentation is as plausibly doc prose ("defstruct fields are validated on build") as it is code, and nothing in the line tells them apart. Rather than guess, the rule now declines, which is the same answer it already gives a stray `"""` below it cannot place. Concretely: a new `@documented_def_word` regex (the documentable subset) and one guard in `doc_end_offset/2`; the broad `@def_word` still locates the definition, so the guard only fires on the *first* definition-form line below the opener. Re-ran the emission afterwards: the ambiguous module is now returned unchanged and the canonical broken doc (`@doc """` straight into `def find_min_max`) is still repaired exactly as before. Added a second test proving the narrowing did not over-reach — a `defstruct` sitting *below* the documented `def` still does not veto the repair. Also added a sentence to the rule's "Known limitation" section recording the new decline. Committed with the fix.
- [2 nit] fixed — Wrote the missing test. Confirmed the cost by executing the rule on a doc containing the prose line `  @timeout - how long to wait` at the doc's own indentation: the rule closes the doc *above* that line, so the doc is truncated to "Adds the two numbers." and the prose becomes module-body code — and the emitted source parses, so nothing downstream reverts it. The new test ("truncates the doc at a prose line that starts with a bare @word") asserts the exact string the rule emits and reads the doc and def list back off that string, so the cost is now visible and any widening of the attribute scan trips it. This is pinned rather than repaired: that same attribute scan is what buys the `@doc` → `@spec` → `def` repair, which is the ordering the rule exists for. Committed with finding [1].
- commits: 4d9ebd11f46ece21e1b86dc6a4b23971dbb205a9
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — 2026-08-19 (modified, rule_syntax)
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:169 — the search for "the next definition" runs to the end of the file and is stopped by nothing, so it will cross the enclosing module's own `end`. On `defmodule Solution do / @doc """ / Solves it. / end / (blank) / defmodule Helper do / def helper, do: :ok / end` the first line at the doc's indent that looks like a definition is `def helper` inside the *second* module, so the closer is inserted there: `end`, the blank line and `defmodule Helper do` are all absorbed into the doc string, module `Helper` ceases to exist, and `helper/0` is silently re-homed into `Solution`. The pre-PR code required the *next non-blank* line to be a `def` and therefore declined this input; the new scan admits it. Whether the damage ships or is merely wasted work turns on one thing I cannot settle by reading: the swallowed lines sit at column 0 while the inserted terminator sits at column 2, so the result is an outdented heredoc — if Elixir treats that as a hard error the round's all-or-nothing gate discards everything, if it is only a warning the corrupted source parses and is committed. A line indented *less* than the `@doc` proves the doc's enclosing block has already closed and the scan can never legitimately continue past it; the same input with the intervening code at the doc's own indent (e.g. a stray `alias`/`import` between the broken doc and its `def`) has no outdent to save it.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:99 — the issue message still says the closing quotes are "missing before the next `def`", but after this change the closer is inserted before an `@spec`/`@impl`/second `@doc` whenever one sits between the doc and its `def`. The message no longer describes where the repair actually goes.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:35 — the new "Known limitation" section lists the `"""`-below veto and the undocumentable-definition decline, but not the two limits a reader is most likely to trip over: the rule also declines outright when no definition appears below at the `@doc`'s own indentation, and the definition search is unbounded downward (the finding above). Both are stated in inline comments; neither reaches the moduledoc a maintainer reads first.
- experiment: settles the first finding — `mix run -e 'src = ~s(defmodule Solution do\n  @doc """\n  Solves it.\nend\n\ndefmodule Helper do\n  def helper, do: :ok\nend\n); out = Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix(src); IO.puts(out); IO.inspect(match?({:ok, _}, Sourceror.parse_string(out)), label: "parses?")'` — if it prints `parses?: true`, the rule ships a source in which module `Helper` has been deleted.

Checked and clean, for the record: check and fix share one predicate (`doc_end_offset/2` + `closing_quotes_below?/1`), so there is no second copy to drift; the fix is idempotent (its own output has `"""` below, which the veto then catches, and `analyze(fix(x)) == []` is asserted); running `fix/1` over this rule's own source is a no-op, because every `@doc """` and `"""` in the moduledoc is backslash-escaped and so matches neither anchored regex; the `\b` reasoning in the `@def_word`/`@documented_def_word` comments is correct, including the `defmacrop`/`defmacro` and `defguardp`/`defguard` orderings; the two-openers-share-one-def case really does resolve to two distinct insertion points (traced by hand against the fixture at test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:412); the tests assert meaning rather than text (exact `==` via `confirm_fix`, plus `module_shape` reading docs/specs/defs back off the emitted bytes), define no top-level `defmodule Example`-style modules, and hand nothing to `Code.compile_string`; and test/syntax_round_safety_test.exs:305 pins the real rule firing end-to-end through `Credence.Syntax.fix_with_trace/2`, so this is not a rule that ships inert.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (modified, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:336 — the delta pins check/fix agreement in one direction only. Seven of the nine new decline cases assert `analyze(code) == []` alongside `confirm_fix(fix(code), code)`, but none of the six new repair cases (@spec boundary, doc-text boundary, indented `def` example, two stacked openers, defstruct-below) asserts that `analyze/1` flags the same input. The analyze test file covers only the two original shapes (`@doc """` immediately above a `def`, and with one blank line), and there is no `_equivalence_test.exs` for this rule. So if `unclosed_doc_heredoc?/3` stopped sharing `doc_end_offset/2` — e.g. reverted to a first-non-blank-line probe — the rule would silently stop reporting the @spec-bounded and attribute-bounded docs it still repairs, and every test in both files would stay green. Adding `assert analyze(input) != []` (or the issue's `meta.line`) beside each `confirm_fix` in the repair tests closes it. Low severity because the round calls `fix/1` unconditionally, so a divergence degrades the issue report, not the rewrite.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — The repair tests only checked the string the fix emits, never that the check reports the same input, so the two halves of the rule could drift apart unnoticed. Confirmed by executing the drift: I edited the rule so its check went back to the old "is the next non-blank line a def?" probe (the fix untouched), and ran both rule test files — 26 tests, 0 failures, nothing noticed. On the @spec fixture that same build printed `analyze: []` while still rewriting the file, i.e. it repairs docs it no longer reports. Pinned it with a `reported_lines/1` helper (the opener lines analyze flags) and an exact `==` assertion on those lines beside `confirm_fix` in all seven new repair cases: the @spec boundary, the two doc-text-boundary cases, the indented `def` example, the bare-`@word` prose truncation, the defstruct-below repair, and the two stacked openers (which must report both openers, lines 2 and 5). Watched all seven fail against that same drifted check, each with `left: []` — the defect itself, not a setup error. No change to the rule: the shipped code already agrees, and this only makes the agreement testable. Gate green (format, compile --warnings-as-errors, mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency: 9057 tests, 0 failures) with the rule restored byte-for-byte. Committed 8b38786.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (modified, test_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:1 — the file pins every boundary of the rule's downward scan except one: what happens when the scan crosses a line indented *less* than the `@doc` (the enclosing module's own `end`). Every fixture here is a single module, so no test constrains `defmodule A do / @doc """ / Shared helpers. / end / (blank) / defmodule B do / def run, do: :ok / end`, where the closer lands inside module B, module B's header is swallowed into the docstring and `run/0` is silently re-homed into A. This is the test-side of the already-reported rule finding (findings.md: blocker at lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:153, restated as a concern at :169) and is still open at head — flagged here only so the pin lands in this file when the rule decision is made. It must NOT be closed by asserting the rule's current output as expected.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:11 — the new `reported_lines/1` header comment claims "Every repair case below asserts this beside the string the fix emits". Five repair cases below it do not: :20, :45, :483, :496 and :531. The claim is true of the seven cases the delta added, not of the file. A maintainer adding a repair test and trusting the stated convention would leave the check/fix agreement unpinned for it — which is exactly the drift the helper was introduced to catch.
- experiment: settles the concern — `mix run -e 'src = ~s(defmodule Helpers do\n  @doc """\n  Shared helpers.\nend\n\ndefmodule Main do\n  def run, do: :ok\nend\n); out = Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix(src); IO.puts(out); IO.inspect(match?({:ok, _}, Sourceror.parse_string(out)), label: "parses?")'` — if the closer prints inside `defmodule Main do` and `parses?: true`, the untested path corrupts silently and the missing test is load-bearing.

Checked and sound, for the record (not findings): all seven new expected strings were re-derived by hand from `doc_end_offset/2` + `attribute_end_offset/3` + `closing_quotes_below?/1` and match byte-for-byte, including the two-stacked-openers case (insertions are computed against the original line list and applied descending, so both openers really do resolve to distinct points — 4 and 6 — rather than the second being vetoed by the first's inserted `"""`); every `reported_lines/1` assertion is correct against `analyze/1`, which drops exactly the same prefix as `fix/1` (`Enum.drop(lines, line_no)` vs `Enum.drop(lines, idx + 1)`), so the two halves agree; assertions use exact `==` through `confirm_fix/2` and `module_shape/1` (which reads docs/specs/defs back off the emitted bytes via `Code.string_to_quoted`, not `=~` or `String.contains?`); the heredoc de-indentation in each `expected` (4-space base, `#{close}` at column 4 yielding `  """`) is correct; every decline test is discriminating — removing the guard it names flips the outcome, so none is vacuous, and each also asserts `analyze(code) == []` so a decline cannot be mistaken for a silent no-op fix; no fixture defines a top-level module or reaches `Code.compile_string`, so failure classes 5 and 6 do not apply; and running the rule's own `fix/1` over this file is a no-op, since every `@doc """` in it is written `@doc \"""` and so matches neither anchored regex.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs — fix round 3 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] fixed — The file had no fixture where the rule's search for "the next definition" crosses a line indented *less* than the `@doc` (the module's own `end`). Confirmed the damage by running the rule on the two-module file the reviewer named (`defmodule Helpers do` / `  @doc """` / `  Shared helpers.` / `end` / blank / `defmodule Main do` / `  def run, do: :ok` / `end`) and parsing the string it emitted back: the closing quotes landed inside `Main`, and the result had exactly one module left — `Helpers`, whose doc string was `"Shared helpers.\nend\n\ndefmodule Main do\n"`. Module `Main` was gone and `run/0` had been re-homed. That output parses (an outdented heredoc is only a warning here), so nothing downstream would revert it. Per the reviewer's instruction I did not pin the current output as expected; I pinned the correct answer instead — a decline test asserting the rule reports nothing and returns the file unchanged — and watched it fail on the old code with `analyze` reporting line 2. Fix, in the rule: the definition scan now runs only while each line is blank or starts with the `@doc`'s own indentation, so the first non-blank outdent bounds it and the rule declines rather than reaching into a later module (the same answer it already gives a `"""` it cannot place). Recorded the new decline in the moduledoc's "Known limitation" section. A second test pins that the bound is the outdent and not "stop at the first `end`": an `end` indented deeper than the `@doc` is the last line of a doc example, and the repair still lands below it, with the doc read back off the emitted bytes to show the example stayed inside it. Re-ran the reproduction afterwards (rule now returns the file unchanged), confirmed the canonical broken doc is still repaired and still parses, and confirmed running the rule's own `fix/1` over its source and over this test file is still a no-op. Committed aec6c9c.
- [2 nit] fixed — The `reported_lines/1` header comment claims every repair case asserts the lines the check reports beside what the fix does; five did not. Confirmed by scanning the file programmatically for test blocks that run `fix/1` on an input the rule rewrites and checking each for the assertion: five printed UNPINNED, exactly the ones named ("fixes unclosed @doc heredoc by inserting closing quotes", "… with blank lines before def", "fixed output no longer flags", "closes every unclosed @doc heredoc in a module", "fixed output is well-formed (parses)"). Showed the gap was load-bearing by executing the drift the helper exists to catch: I changed the check so it stops reporting a doc that has no text in it, leaving the fix alone. This file stayed entirely green — 22 tests, 0 failures — while the rule went on rewriting all five of those docs without reporting any of them. Added the missing assertion to each (the two-openers case must report both openers, lines 2 and 7), watched all five go red under that same drift with `left: []` — the divergence itself, not a setup error — and restored the rule byte-for-byte before the gate. The last two tests now bind their input instead of inlining it so the assertion has something to name; their existing assertions are untouched. Reworded the header comment to state the convention with no exception. Committed 63bee48.
- note: the separate analyze battery (test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_analyze_test.exs) independently caught two of the five shapes above under that drift, but not the two-stacked-openers shape — worth knowing that the two files' coverage overlaps only on the two original fixtures.
- note: for finding [1] the alternative to declining was to close the doc *at* the outdented line, i.e. insert the terminator immediately before the module's own `end`. That would repair the file rather than leave it, but it produces a module carrying a doc with no definition under it (a compiler warning), and it guesses that the documented `def` was never written. I chose the decline because it matches every other "cannot place this" answer the rule already gives; if the maintainer prefers the repair, the change is one clause in `doc_end_offset/2` and the new decline test becomes a repair test.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — 2026-08-19 (modified, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:173 — `doc_end_offset/2` now ends the doc at the first *def-or-attribute* line at the doc's indent, so every other module-body line between the opener and that def is swallowed into the doc string and deleted. On `defmodule Server do\n  @doc """\n  Starts the server.\n  use GenServer\n\n  def start_link(opts), do: ...\nend` the closer is inserted above `def start_link`, `use GenServer` becomes doc text, and the module loses the GenServer behaviour, its default callbacks and `child_spec/1`. The output parses and compiles (a stale `@impl` only warns), so `commit_or_roll_back/4` keeps it and nothing downstream reverts it. The same absorption eats an Ecto `schema "users" do ... end` block, an Nx `defn`, or `import`/`alias`/`require` lines sitting between a doc and its def — none of which `@def_word` or `@attribute_word` recognise. This is new in this delta: the base version required the *first non-blank* line below the opener to be a `def` and inserted immediately above it, so it could never absorb an intervening line. The rule already declines when the first definition-form line is one `@doc` cannot document (`defstruct`, `defmodule`, …); the gap is that non-`def…` module-level constructs get no such treatment.
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:70 — the `@documented_def_word` decline is scoped to the *first* `@def_word` hit only, which is what makes the blocker above reachable: any line the two regexes do not recognise is silently classified as doc prose rather than as "I cannot tell, decline". A maintainer should decide whether the conservative answer (decline when a line at the doc's own indent is neither prose-shaped nor a recognised attribute/def) is affordable, given the rule's existing appetite for declining.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:104 — the issue message still reads "the closing `\"\"\"` is missing before the next `def`", but after this change the closer can land above an `@spec`/`@impl`/second `@doc` instead of above a `def`. The moduledoc was updated for this; the message was not.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:41 — the Known-limitation text says a file whose earlier doc is broken and whose later doc is closed is "left to the next round", but `closing_quotes_below?/1` is a pure function of the same unchanged source, so the next round returns the same decline. Nothing in this rule will ever repair such a file; the sentence reads as if a retry would.
- experiment: settles the blocker — mix run -e 'IO.puts(Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedDocHeredoc.fix("defmodule Server do\n  @doc \"\"\"\n  Starts the server.\n  use GenServer\n\n  def start_link(opts), do: GenServer.start_link(__MODULE__, opts)\nend\n"))'

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (modified, test_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:459 — this test pins as *correct* an output that deletes a line of the user's documentation and turns it into live code: input whose doc prose reads `@timeout - how long to wait` at the doc's own indent comes back with the doc truncated above that line and the prose promoted to a module-body expression, and because the result parses nothing downstream reverts it. The boundary that does this is `@attribute_word` (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:90), a bare `^@\w+\b`, introduced by this PR in e626a92. The same PR (4d9ebd1, lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:79 and the test at :176) established the opposite policy for the identical kind of ambiguity — a `defstruct` line at the doc's indent is "as plausibly doc prose as it is code", so the rule declines rather than guess. A bare `@word` line is exactly as ambiguous, but here the rule guesses "code" and corrupts. Unlike the `defstruct` case there *is* something to tell them apart with: the repair this boundary exists to buy (`@doc` → `@spec` → `def`, test at :420) only needs the doc-adjacent attributes, so restricting `@attribute_word` to a known set (`@doc`, `@spec`, `@impl`, `@deprecated`, `@since`, `@doc`-family) — or declining, as the `defstruct` branch does — would keep every repair this file asserts while dropping the corruption. A human should decide whether the cost is still worth pinning; the moduledoc documents it (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:87-89), which is why this is a concern and not a blocker.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc_fix_test.exs:21 — every fixture in the file puts `@doc` at exactly two-space indentation, so the whole file stays green if the rule's captured `indent` were replaced by a literal `"  "` in the emitted closer (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex:145) and in the indent-sensitive scans (`at_indent?`, `inside_doc_block?`). Those three indent-driven decisions are the heart of the delta (`aec6c9c` added the outdent bound; `e626a92` added the at-the-doc's-own-indent rule) and none of them is pinned against a second indentation. One fixture with the broken `@doc` inside a nested `defmodule` (four-space indent), and one at column 0 — where `inside_doc_block?`'s `String.starts_with?(line, "")` is unconditionally true and the outdent bound therefore never fires at all — would close the gap.

Reviewed and found sound (no finding raised): the check and the fix share one predicate (`doc_end_offset/2` + `closing_quotes_below?/1`) rather than keeping two copies, and the new `reported_lines/1` assertion is present on every repair case in the file, so the comment at :11-18 stating that convention is true as written; the fixtures are only ever parsed (`Sourceror.parse_string`, `Code.string_to_quoted` via `module_shape/1`), never compiled, so nothing executes fixture code and no fixture module name can collide across the suite; assertions are exact `==` (byte-exact via `confirm_fix/2`, meaning-level via `module_shape/1`) with no `=~`/`String.contains?` anywhere; the local `analyze/1` and `fix/1` helpers call the same arity-1 entry points the pipeline calls at lib/syntax.ex:116; and I traced each `expected` string and each `module_shape` assertion against the implementation by hand — the two-opener case (:498), the `@spec` case (:420), the deeper-indented `end` (:339) and the in-doc `def` example (:384) all match what the code emits.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex — 2026-08-19 (unchanged_rule, rule_syntax)
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:167 — the repair is committed only if deleting the stray `end` makes the WHOLE file parse, so the rule silently declines on any file that carries a second syntax error anywhere — including a second instance of this very bug. Two `fn … end)` + stray-`end` mistakes in one file are repaired by neither this rule nor `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter` (whose `repair/1` likewise requires `parses?(fixed)`): the first rule's candidate deletions all still fail to parse because of bug #2, and the sibling's insert-only pass never parses either. Output: source returned byte-identical, `analyze` returns `[]`, no trace entry. This is the opposite of what the round is built for — `lib/syntax.ex:11-30` states that unparseable intermediate states are expected and that gating on "must parse now" would disable the round — and the moduledoc's "a file unparseable for an unrelated reason is left untouched" (line 26) does not tell a maintainer that a *second copy of the same bug* is such a reason.
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:106 — this is the file's only test aimed at the rule's one line-editing step (`remove_stray_end/2` deletes a whole line matching `^\s*end\s*$`), and it never reaches it. The fixture's first parse error is `def broken(, do: 1`, not an `fn`/`)` mismatch, so `detect/1` returns `:none` and the rule declines before any line is considered; deleting the `@moduledoc` block from the fixture entirely would not change the result. So nothing in the suite exercises the line-delete against a source whose only error IS the fn bug and which also contains a bare `end` line inside a heredoc, a comment, or a second block below the insertion point — the exact input the code comment at lines 154-157 claims to be safe on.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:154 — the comment credits the wrong mechanism. The downward scan only keeps the rule out of text *above* the insertion line; it does nothing about a bare `end` line inside a heredoc *below* it. What actually rules those out is the `parses?(candidate)` check: deleting a line inside a string/heredoc removes no `end` token, so the extra `end` is still unbalanced and the candidate never parses. Worth rewording, so that a future relaxation of the parse gate is not made on the belief that the scan direction is carrying the safety.
- experiment: settles the first finding — `mix run -e 'one = ~s|defmodule TB do\n  def a(m) do\n    Enum.map(m, fn r ->\n      Enum.map(r, fn e ->\n        if e == 0 do 1 else e end)\n      end\n    end)\n  end\nend\n|; IO.inspect({Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedFnDelimiter.analyze(one), Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedFnDelimiter.analyze(one <> one)})'` — expect `{[%Credence.Issue{...}], []}`: one occurrence is flagged and repaired, two identical occurrences in one file are flagged and repaired not at all.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] fixed — Confirmed by running the reviewer's experiment: one copy of the bug in a file is flagged and repaired, but two copies in one file are repaired not at all (`analyze` returned `[]`, `fix` returned the source byte-identical). The cause is that the rule deleted the stray `end` only if that deletion made the *whole file* parse, so any second syntax error anywhere vetoed the repair — including a second copy of this same bug. That contradicts `lib/syntax.ex`, which says the round is built to walk through source that still does not parse. Pinned with three failing tests in the file's own battery: two copies of the bug are both repaired to an exact expected string; both occurrences are reported at their line numbers in the *input* (5 and 14); and the bug is repaired on a file that is also broken for an unrelated reason. All three failed first, showing exactly the byte-identical no-op and the empty issue list. The fix replaces the whole-file gate with a local one: a candidate line deletion is committed when the extra-`end` error *at that same `(`* is gone **and** the parser now gets at least as far as the line that was deleted. Identifying the error by the position of its `(` rather than by its shape turned out to be load-bearing: an unrelated `def f(, do: 1` in the same file produces an identically-shaped "`(` closed by `end`" error, and a first version of the change deleted the module's own final `end` because of it — the pinning test caught that, not reading. The repair then runs to exhaustion so every occurrence is repaired, and `analyze` reports each one; since each pass deletes one line, later passes' line numbers are translated back into the input's numbering. Proof: all 17 tests in the two test files for this rule pass; `mix format`, `mix compile --warnings-as-errors`, and `mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency` (9065 tests, 0 failures) are green. The output was verified by executing the string the rule *actually emitted* — it compiles (`:ok` via `RuleHelpers.compile_and_capture/1`), equals a hand-written control byte-for-byte, and both repaired modules compute the right answer. Committed as 3396ee9.
- [2 concern] fixed — Confirmed by parsing the fixture at `close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:106`: its first parse error is at line 7, `def broken(, do: 1` (an `(` closed by `end`), not an `fn`/`)` mismatch — so `detect/1` returns `:none` and the rule declines before any line is examined. Deleting the `@moduledoc` block from that fixture gave the same no-op, exactly as the reviewer predicted, so the docstring was never doing any work. Wrote the test that was missing: a source whose *only* fault is this bug and whose first bare `end` line at or below the insertion point is heredoc content (`IO.puts("""` / `end` / `"""`)`). The line-delete really does reach that heredoc line, and the assertion is an exact whole-string comparison showing the heredoc preserved byte-for-byte and the real stray `end` deleted; the emitted string was also compiled to `:ok`. Added alongside it: a source with a later function whose own `end` must not be touched when there is no stray `end` to delete, and a re-fix-your-own-output check. The original docstring test is kept unchanged. Committed as 3396ee9.
- [3 nit] fixed — Confirmed the comment at the old line 154 credits the wrong mechanism. On the new heredoc fixture the downward scan *does* reach the bare `end` line that is heredoc content (it sits below the insertion line), so the scan direction excludes nothing there; deleting that line leaves the identical mismatched-delimiter error, because no `end` *token* was removed — printed both errors side by side and they name the same `(` at 3:13. So the parse check, not the scan direction, is what rules heredoc content out. The comment is reworded to say so, and the moduledoc says it too, which matters now that the parse gate has been relaxed for finding [1] — the replacement check preserves precisely that property and the comment now states which check is carrying it. Committed as 3396ee9.
- note: `Credence.Syntax.NoUnclosedFnDelimiter` (the sibling rule named in finding [1]) still gates its `repair/1` on `parses?(fixed)`, so it has the same "declines on any file with a second fault" behaviour that finding [1] describes. Out of scope for this file; left untouched.
- commits: 3396ee93a04eac73b607cb76ebf19421093fb8b8
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex — 2026-08-19 (modified, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:162 — `stray_end_error/1` accepts ANY "`(` closed by `end`" mismatch anywhere in the file, not only one caused by the `end` this rule just inserted; with the whole-file parse gate removed by this PR, a file whose *second, unrelated* fault has that shape makes the rule invent a stray `end` that does not exist and delete a real one. Input `defmodule Mixed do\n  def a(list) do\n    Enum.max_by(list, fn {_, s} -> s)\n  end\n\n  def broken(, do: 1\nend` — the parser's first error is the `fn`/`)` mismatch on line 3, so `detect/1` fires; inserting `end` there makes lines 1-4 perfectly balanced (this is NoUnclosedFnDelimiter's plain-unclosed-`fn` shape, which this rule's moduledoc:41-44 promises it never touches), but the tokenizer then reports the unrelated `def broken(` on line 6 as a `(` closed by the `end` on line 7, so `stray_end_error/1` returns `{:ok, {6, 13}}` and the rule proceeds to delete a line. Output: `def a`'s own `  end` (line 4) is deleted. Under the base version this was rejected because the candidate did not parse.
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:259 — the identity guard `same_stray_end?/2` compares the `(` position captured *before* the deletion against the one reported *after* it, relying on the invariant stated at :159-161 and :236-240 that "the `(` is always above the line being deleted". Nothing checks that invariant. When `stray_end_error/1` picked up a `(` that is *below* the insertion point (the blocker above), deleting any bare `end` line shifts that `(` up by one line, the two positions differ, and the guard reads the unchanged error as "the stray `end` is gone" — so the very first bare `end` line at or below the insertion point is accepted no matter what it closes. The same positional-not-semantic weakness lets a deletion be accepted whenever the identical structural error is merely re-reported at a *different* enclosing `(`.
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:262 — `stopped_at_or_below?/2` does not do what the comment at :241-245 says it does. That comment claims it rejects a deletion that removed a real enclosing `end`, because the resulting unterminated block "drags the error back above the repair". But this repo's own `Credence.Syntax.ProgressGuard` moduledoc (lib/syntax/progress_guard.ex:43-47) records the opposite: `meta[:end_line]` is **EOF** for an unclosed delimiter. A deletion that leaves a block unterminated therefore reports `end_line` at EOF, which always satisfies `line > deleted_idx`, so this guard passes exactly the case it is documented to catch. It is the only backstop left after the whole-file parse gate was removed, and it is doing far less than the comment asserts. (Related: the rule hand-rolls a progress measure that `ProgressGuard` already provides for this round.)
- concern: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:176 — the test "repairs its own occurrence on a file that is also broken for an unrelated reason" is the test that pins the unsound path. In its fixture, inserting `end` before the `)` leaves lines 1-6 fully balanced with no stray `end` at all; the only `(`-closed-by-`end` error in the file belongs to `def broken(, do: 1` on line 8, and the deletion of line 5 is accepted only because that unrelated `(` shifted from line 8 to line 7. The expected output happens to be defensible on its own, but it was reached from a signal that says nothing about line 5. A maintainer fixing the blockers must re-derive this expectation rather than preserve it — a natural guard (require the reported `(` to be at or above the insertion line) makes this test fail.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:86 — the multi-pass loop has no pass bound, unlike the sibling `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter` (`@max_passes 100`, lib/syntax/no_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:32). Termination is genuinely guaranteed (one line removed per pass), but the work is not: each pass tokenizes once per bare `end` line at or below the insertion point, so a pathological file is O(n²) full tokenizations. Cheap to bound.
- experiment: settles the first two blockers — `cd /home/kamil/projects/credence && mix run -e 'IO.puts(Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedFnDelimiter.fix("defmodule Mixed do\n  def a(list) do\n    Enum.max_by(list, fn {_, s} -> s)\n  end\n\n  def broken(, do: 1\nend\n"))'` — a sound rule prints the input unchanged (this is NoUnclosedFnDelimiter's shape and the file has no stray `end`); the defect prints it with `def a`'s `  end` line deleted.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by running the reviewer's input through the rule: `defmodule Mixed do / def a(list) do / Enum.max_by(list, fn {_, s} -> s) / end / / def broken(, do: 1 / end` came back with `def a`'s own `  end` line deleted, and `analyze` reported an issue on line 3. The cause: after inserting `end` before the `)`, the rule asked the parser "is there now an extra `end` where a call's `)` should close?" and accepted that answer from anywhere in the file. On this input the insertion balances lines 1-4 outright — there is no stray `end` at all, this is the sibling rule's shape — and the complaint the parser actually returns belongs to the unrelated `def broken(` three lines below the repair. Measured it directly: the reported `(` is at line 6, the repair at line 3. On every fixture where the rule is genuinely right, the reported `(` is *above* the repair (line 3 vs repair at line 10, 5, 5). So the rule now requires the complaint to name a `(` at or above the line it just repaired; below it, that is somebody else's fault and says nothing about this repair. Pinned by two new tests that both failed first showing the exact corrupted output — the reviewer's input, and the same defect wrapped in an enclosing call. Both now assert the source comes back byte-identical with no issues reported.
- [2 blocker] fixed — Same reproduction as [1]; this is the second half of why that corruption was accepted. The rule remembered the `(`'s position before deleting a line and compared it against the position reported afterwards, on the unchecked assumption that the `(` always sits above the deleted line. With the `(` actually below (finding [1]), deleting a line shifted it up by one, the two positions differed, and the rule read "different position" as "the stray `end` is gone" — measured: reported `(` moves from line 6 to line 5 across the deletion, and the file is unchanged in every other respect. Two changes. First, [1]'s guard makes the assumption true by construction: the deleted line is always strictly below the repaired line, which is at or below the `(`, so the `(` can no longer move. Second, the before/after position comparison is gone entirely — the check after the deletion is now literally the negation of the check before it ("no extra-`end` complaint at or above the repair"), one predicate used twice instead of two positions compared. That also closes the reviewer's second case: the same fault merely re-blamed on a *different* enclosing `(` above the repair used to read as success and now does not. Verified against every fixture in the battery that the new predicate reproduces the old accept/reject decision on all of them except the corrupting ones.
- [3 concern] fixed — Confirmed the guard does not do what its comment claims. The comment said it rejects a deletion that removed a real enclosing `end`, because the resulting unterminated block drags the error back above the repair. Executed: deleting `def a`'s `end` from a repaired fixture leaves the module unterminated and the parser reports its stop at line 9 of a 9-line file — EOF — which trivially satisfies `line > deleted_line`, so the guard passes. (This repo's own `ProgressGuard` moduledoc records the same thing and refuses to compare EOF stops at all.) Measured across every fixture in the battery: the guard never once changed an outcome the extra-`end` check had not already decided. Then found the case it was supposed to catch, live on this tree: a file whose stray `end` carries a trailing comment, so the old search walked past it to the next bare `end` line and deleted the enclosing `case`'s own terminator. That deletion *rebalances* the file — the stray `end` closes the `case` instead — so the result parses and no gate could object; the rule silently emitted a differently-meaning module. The real fix is to stop searching: the stray `end` is, by the shape of the bug, the first `end` **token** below the repaired `)`, so the rule now goes straight to it. The search runs over `SourceMask`'s shadow, where a bare `end` inside a heredoc, string or comment is not a token — so literal content neither stops the search nor gets deleted, which is a stronger version of the masking the old parse-check was doing by accident. If that first `end` is not alone on its line, the rule declines rather than editing around it. Pinned by two tests (the comment-carrying stray inside a `case`, which failed first showing the deleted `case` terminator; and a nested `if` block below a comment-carrying stray, which the old code declined only by accident and must still decline). The old guard is kept — removing it could only widen what is accepted — but its comment now states only what it actually checks and says explicitly that it does not catch this case.
- [4 concern] fixed — Confirmed the reviewer's reading by measuring the fixture: inserting `end` before the `)` on line 4 leaves lines 1-6 perfectly balanced, and the only "`(` closed by `end`" complaint in the file is the unrelated `def broken(` on line 8, whose `(` shifted from line 8 to 7 across the deletion. So the test's expected output was reached from a signal that says nothing about line 5, and once [1]'s guard is in place the rule correctly declines that input. Re-derived rather than preserved: that fixture is now a test asserting the rule leaves it untouched and reports nothing, with a comment explaining it is the plain unclosed-`fn` shape the moduledoc promises to leave to `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter`. What the old test was *meant* to pin — that a second, unrelated fault elsewhere must not veto a real repair — is pinned by a new fixture that really is this rule's shape: a genuine stray `end` on line 6 whose enclosing `(` is on line 3, above the repair, in a file that also carries `def broken(, do: 1` below. That one is repaired to an exact expected string, and its `def broken(` line is left alone.
- [5 nit] fixed — Added `@max_passes 100`, matching `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter`. Termination was already guaranteed, so the bound is on work, not correctness; the larger win came with [3], since each pass now does one shadow scan to find the stray `end` instead of one full tokenization per bare `end` line at or below the repair. Pinned that the bound does not cut a real file short with a new test: three copies of the bug in one file are all repaired to an exact expected string and all three reported at their input line numbers (5, 14, 23).
- note: the corruption found while confirming [3] — a whole-line delete walking past a stray `end` that shares its line with a comment — is worth checking for in the sibling `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter` too; it was not examined here. Also still open from fix round 1: that sibling gates its `repair/1` on `parses?(fixed)`, so it declines on any file carrying a second fault, the behaviour round 1's finding [1] removed from this rule.
- commits: 8cfa78e54bf3905ffaad801567d15a865298ac38
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex — 2026-08-19 (modified, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:210 — an unclosed `(` sitting ABOVE the repaired line produces the very same "`(` closed by `end`" complaint the rule reads as "there is a stray `end` of mine to delete", and `opening_line(meta) <= repair_line` admits it, so the rule deletes a real block terminator. On the input `defmodule M do` / `  def broken(, do: 1` / `` / `  def go(list) do` / `    Enum.max_by(list, fn {_, s} -> s)` / `  end` / `end` the first parse error is the `fn`/`)` mismatch on line 5, so `end` is inserted there; the leftover error is then the `(` opened on line 2 (above line 5) being closed by the `end` on line 7, which `stray_end_above?` accepts. `delete_stray_end` deletes the first `end` token below line 5 — line 6, which is `def go`'s own terminator, not a stray one — and `clears_stray_end?` (line 318) accepts the result because the remaining error is now an EOF "missing terminator: )" whose `closing_delimiter` is nil (so `stray_end_meta?` is false) and whose `end_line` is past the deleted line. Output is the file with `def go`'s `end` gone, plus a bogus issue reported at line 5. This is exactly the plain-unclosed-fn shape the moduledoc promises to leave to `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter`, and it is a regression introduced by this PR: the base version's whole-file parse gate declined here. Every test added for the unrelated-fault case ("declines the plain-unclosed-fn shape even when an unrelated ( is closed by an end below", "does not delete a real end on the strength of an unrelated ( closed by an end below") places the unrelated `(` BELOW the repair; the above-the-repair direction the new guard does not cover is untested. The Syntax round's all-or-nothing rollback masks this only while the file still fails to parse at the end of the round — it does not apply under `syntax_partial_repairs: true`, nor when a later rule repairs the remaining `(`.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:30 — the moduledoc's locality claim ("Each occurrence is repaired on its own", only "a fault the rule cannot see past" is declined) overstates what the code does. `repair_once` only ever looks at the parser's FIRST error; if that first error is a `fn`-closed-by-`)` of the OTHER shape (a plain unclosed `fn` higher in the file), inserting `end` there leaves the genuine stray-`end` occurrence below as the next error, `stray_end_above?` is false, and the rule declines the whole file — while `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter` also declines, because its whole-file gate is blocked by the second fault. Neither rule repairs anything. Pre-existing behaviour (the base version declined too); only the doc claim is new.
- experiment: settles the blocker — `cd /home/kamil/projects/credence && mix run -e 'IO.puts(Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedFnDelimiter.fix("defmodule M do\n  def broken(, do: 1\n\n  def go(list) do\n    Enum.max_by(list, fn {_, s} -> s)\n  end\nend\n"))'` — if the printed source is missing the `  end` that closed `def go`, the rule deleted a real terminator; if it is byte-identical to the input, the rule declined and the finding is wrong.

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex — fix round 3 (2026-08-19)
- [1 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by running the reviewer's exact input through `fix/1`: `defmodule M do` / `  def broken(, do: 1` / `` / `  def go(list) do` / `    Enum.max_by(list, fn {_, s} -> s)` / `  end` / `end` came back with `def go`'s own `  end` line deleted, plus an issue reported at line 5. What goes wrong: after inserting `end` before the `)`, the rule asks the parser "is a call's `)` now closed by an extra `end`?" and accepts the answer if the `(` named sits at or above the repaired line. An unclosed `(` *above* the repair produces exactly that complaint — the parser blames some later real `end` on it — and the guard added in the previous round only ruled out the same complaint coming from *below*. Measured the two side by side and they are structurally identical from the parser's view: same error type, same delimiters, `(` above the repair in both, and in both the offending `end` is the second `end` token below the repair. The only thing that separates them is whether that `(` is ever closed at all: a real occurrence's enclosing call has its `)`, `def broken(` never does. So the discriminator used is what the deletion does rather than what the complaint looks like — deleting a genuinely stray `end` cannot leave anything unterminated, because a stray `end` closes nothing. The post-deletion check now also refuses the candidate when the parser reports an opener at or above the repair as *missing its terminator* (an opener named with no closing delimiter, which is how a delimiter left hanging at end-of-file is described). Measured that signature on every fixture in the battery: it appears only on the corrupting inputs; genuine repairs either parse outright or leave a mismatched-delimiter complaint elsewhere, never a missing terminator above the repair. Pinned with three new tests: the reviewer's input, and the same shape with a further function below it so the terminator at risk is unmistakably real — both failed first, printing the file with `def go`'s `end` gone — and a third for a genuine occurrence of this rule's own shape sitting below an unclosed `(`, which must be declined (its only available evidence belongs to the broken `(`, not to the repair; that one already declined and is now pinned so it stays that way). The moduledoc's list of what is checked before a line is deleted goes from two items to three and says which one carries this. Proof: the reviewer's input now comes back byte-identical with no issues; the emitted string for a genuine repair was executed through `RuleHelpers.compile_and_capture/1` and returned `{:ok, []}`; all 26 tests across the rule's two test files pass; `mix format`, `mix compile --warnings-as-errors` and `mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency` (9074 tests, 0 failures) are green.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed by executing the shape the reviewer describes: a file whose line 3 is a plain unclosed `fn` (the sibling rule's shape) and whose lines 7-11 are a genuine occurrence of this rule's shape. Printed the parser's first complaint — it is line 3's `fn`/`)` mismatch — and ran both rules over the file: `CloseUnclosedFnDelimiter` returned the source byte-identical with `analyze` empty, and so did `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter`. Neither rule repairs anything, exactly as the finding says, so the moduledoc's "each occurrence is repaired on its own" was promising more than the code delivers. Reworded: the moduledoc now says only that a second fault elsewhere does not *by itself* veto a repair, and lists the three second faults that do stop the rule — a first complaint of some other kind, a first complaint that is the sibling's shape (naming the sibling, and saying that neither rule repairs anything until one of the two faults is gone), and an unclosed `(` above the repair. Added a test that pins the behaviour the reworded text describes: both rules leave that input untouched and report nothing. That test passes on the pre-fix code as well — a documentation claim cannot be made to fail a test — so it is a characterisation test, not the pinning test for a defect; the executed evidence above is what confirms the finding.
- note: the rule deletes the first `end` *token* below the repair, and nothing checks that no block opens between the repair and that line. Constructed `if r > 0 do` / `:big` / `end` sitting between the repaired `)` and the stray `end` and ran it: the rule got the right answer there only because the two candidate lines were byte-identical (`      end`), so deleting either produced the same file. An input where the intervening block's terminator is indented differently from the stray, or carries different whitespace, was not tried. Out of scope for these findings; left unfixed.
- commits: 1ea542d175ee836677510938b53a22a0a7bc3af9
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex — 2026-08-19 (modified, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:305 — "first `end` token below the repair is the stray one" is an assumption, not a property of the bug, and when it is false the rule deletes a real block terminator and the result parses, so nothing catches it. Input: the documented bug shape where the outer `fn` body continues with a complete `do…end` block before the dangling `end` — `defmodule M do / def go(m) do / Enum.map(m, fn r -> / Enum.map(r, fn e -> / if e == 0 do 1 else e end) / if r > 0 do / IO.puts("big") / end / r * 2 / end / end) / end / end`. After inserting `end` on line 5 the leftover complaint is `(` (line 3) closed by `end` (line 11), 3 <= 5, so `stray_end_above?` passes; `first_end_token_line/2` then stops at line 8 — the `if`'s own terminator — not at the real stray on line 10. Deleting line 8 yields `if r > 0 do IO.puts("big"); r * 2 end`, which parses and compiles cleanly, so `clears_stray_end?/3`'s `{:ok, _ast} -> true` arm accepts it unconditionally. The function now returns `nil` for `r <= 0` instead of always returning `r * 2` — a silent behaviour change, and `analyze/1` reports line 5 with "a stray `end` follows", which is a false diagnosis of that line. Pre-existing: the base version's "first bare `end` line whose removal makes the whole file parse" scan picks the same line 8 and commits the same output. What the PR changes is that the moduledoc (lines 79-83) and the `clears_stray_end?` comment (lines 339-346) now assert this deletion is ruled out "by the shape of the bug", so the hazard is now documented as handled. Note the guard is one-sided: it bounds walking *past* the stray but not stopping *short* of it.
- concern: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:347 — no test exercises `stopped_at_or_below?/2` firing (the third bullet of the `clears_stray_end?` contract). Every declining test in `close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs` is turned away earlier, by `stray_end_above?/2`, `bare_end?/1` or `unterminated_above_meta?/2`; the arithmetic in `stopped_at_or_below?/2` mixes a post-deletion `end_line` with a pre-deletion 0-based index, and reading alone I can only confirm the off-by-one is right, not that the branch is reachable. An unreachable guard is one that silently rots.
- nit: lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:316 — the comment says "Two things must hold" and then lists three bullets.
- experiment: settles the blocker — `mix run -e 'IO.puts Credence.Syntax.CloseUnclosedFnDelimiter.fix(~s|defmodule M do\n  def go(m) do\n    Enum.map(m, fn r ->\n      Enum.map(r, fn e ->\n        if e == 0 do 1 else e end)\n      if r > 0 do\n        IO.puts("big")\n      end\n      r * 2\n      end\n    end)\n  end\nend\n|)'` — if the printed source has `r * 2` sitting inside the `if` block, the rule deleted the `if`'s terminator instead of the stray `end`.
