- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:495 — "fixing twice changes nothing the second time" cannot catch an idempotency defect. Its input is the two-copy `TwoBugs` file, whose repaired output parses, so the second `fix/1` call stops at `detect/1`'s `{:ok, _ast}` branch and returns the source untouched without ever reaching the insert/delete path. That is the same short-circuit already covered by "does not modify already-valid code" (line 96). The only fix output in this file that still does not parse is the `Local` fixture (line 182), whose repair leaves `def broken(, do: 1` behind — re-running the fix on *that* output is the case where a non-idempotent rule could actually delete a second line, and nothing asserts it.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — Confirmed by execution. I copied the rule into a scratch file with counters added at two points (the moment the parser confirms the "`fn` closed by `)`" shape, and the moment the rule commits to deleting a line) and then ran `fix` twice on each candidate source. On the fixture the old idempotency test used (two copies of `TwoBugs`), the *second* `fix` call matched the shape check 0 times and reached the deletion 0 times: its once-repaired output parses, so the rule handed the source straight back at the parser — the very same short-circuit the test on line 96, "does not modify already-valid code", already covers. So the test could not have caught an idempotency defect. I pinned that by adding `refute valid_syntax?(once)` to the existing test with its existing fixture and running it: it failed with "Expected false or nil, got true", i.e. the once-fixed source parses. The fix changes the fixtures, not the rule. The test is now two tests, both keeping that `refute` so the fixture can never silently drift back to a parseable one: (a) a source whose leftover fault after repair is a plain unclosed `fn` — the same instrumentation shows the second `fix` call here does match the shape check, does insert `end`, and is only then turned back by the rule's "is that leftover `end` really a stray one of mine?" guard, which is as deep as a second call can reach; and (b) the reviewer's suggested `Local` source, whose leftover fault is the unrelated `def broken(, do: 1` and where the second call is declined one step earlier, on the shape of the parser's complaint rather than on "it already parses". Both pass; `mix format`, `mix compile --warnings-as-errors` and `mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency` (9075 tests) are green. Committed as c317724.
- note: while measuring the above I found that no input can make the second `fix` call reach the line-deletion at all, and that is structural rather than accidental: `fix/1` loops its single-occurrence repair to a fixpoint internally, so `fix(fix(x))` re-enters with a source the loop has already declared finished. The one exception is the 100-pass work bound — a file with more than 100 occurrences comes back from `fix/1` with occurrences still unrepaired, and fixing it again does change it. That is documented behaviour of the bound ("a bound on *work*, not a correctness guard"), not a defect, so I did not assert anything about it; noting it only because it means an idempotency test on this rule can pin how far the second call travels, but cannot by itself catch a bad deletion.
- commits: c3177243a986375fa743f63bcca043e18f3df510
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (modified, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:108 — "leaves the bug shape inside a docstring untouched when the file is broken elsewhere" never reaches the masking code it appears to test. On that fixture the tokenizer's first complaint is the unrelated `def broken(` on line 7 being closed by the module's `end` (opening delimiter `(`, not `fn`), so `detect/1` returns `:none` and the rule hands the source straight back without looking at a single line. The test would pass byte-identically against a rule with no `SourceMask` use at all. Masking is genuinely covered — but only by the Heredoc test at :413, which is the one place the downward `end`-token scan actually runs over a shadow. (Pre-existing; unchanged by this PR.)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:372 — the comment "The multi-pass loop is bounded now; the bound must not cut a real file short" overclaims what the test below it proves: `@max_passes` is 100 (lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:92) and the fixture drives 3 passes, so the bound is never approached. Nothing in the suite covers what happens when it *is* hit: `repair/4` at lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:130 returns `done(source, reported)`, i.e. a file with more than 100 occurrences comes back partially repaired and is reported as `{:fixed, ...}` with issues only for the occurrences that got rewritten — silently partial rather than declined. Either drop the bound claim from the comment or add a fixture that exercises the cut-off.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:539 — the `Local` fixture is duplicated verbatim from :183. The comment at :532 asserts "this is the once-fixed `Local` source above", but nothing ties the two copies together: editing one leaves the other's comment quietly false and the idempotency test still green on a source that is no longer the one it claims to be. Bind it once (a module attribute or a helper) so the two tests cannot drift apart.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:403 — the three-copy test's `%Issue{meta: %{line: 5}}` pattern drops the `rule:` field that the otherwise-identical two-copy assertion at :172 pins. Harmless today, but the assertion no longer proves the issues came from this rule.
- experiment: settles the :108 nit — `elixir -e 'IO.inspect(Code.string_to_quoted(~s|defmodule M do\n  @moduledoc """\n      Enum.map(row, fn element ->\n        if element == 0 do a else element end)\n      end\n  """\n  def broken(, do: 1\nend\n|, columns: true))'` — if the reported meta carries `opening_delimiter: :"("` rather than `:fn`, the rule short-circuits in `detect/1` and the docstring is never consulted.

Not findings, checked and clear: every assertion in the file compares meaning (`confirm_fix`'s exact `==`, `valid_syntax?`, exact-length `%Issue{}` list patterns) — no `=~`, `String.contains?` or `Regex.match?` assertions. No fixture is ever compiled (the rule uses `Code.string_to_quoted`, `valid_syntax?` uses `Sourceror.parse_string`), so the duplicate `defmodule TwoBugs`/`Local`/`Solution` names race nothing and no arbitrary fixture reaches the compiler. The repair is proven to fire end-to-end on realistic input (:13, :127, :182, :374, :413) with byte-exact expected output. The reported line numbers at :172 and :402 (5/14, 5/14/23) match `input_line/2`'s shift-back arithmetic over the accumulated deletions. The new negative tests each reach and exercise the specific guard they name: :219 and :240 are turned back by the "`(` at or above the repair" test in `stray_end_above?`, :262 and :280 by the new unterminated-opener refusal in `clears_stray_end?`, :328 and :352 by `bare_end?`. The two idempotency tests at :510 and :538 now genuinely re-enter the repair — `refute valid_syntax?(once)` is what stops them decaying back into the "already parses" short-circuit — and the sibling-ownership test at :471 correctly pins that both rules decline the two-fault file, matching `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter`'s parse-or-nothing gate.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — Confirmed the reviewer's reading by running their experiment: parsing the fixture reports `opening_delimiter: :"("` (the unrelated `def broken(` on line 7 closed by the module's `end`), not `:fn`, so `detect/1` returns `:none` and the rule hands the source back without looking at a line. Went further and ran the rule over three variants of the fixture — the original, one with the docstring's contents replaced by a harmless sentence, and one with the `@moduledoc` deleted outright: all three came back byte-identical with `analyze/1` empty, so the docstring was doing no work at all. The test still pins a real guarantee, just not the one its name claimed, so I renamed it to "declines outright when the parser's first complaint is not an fn closed by )", replaced the comment with an explanation of why the docstring is inert here, pointed it at the `Heredoc` test as the file's one genuine masking test, and added an `analyze(input) == []` assertion alongside the existing exact-output check. No fixture bytes changed.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed both halves. The bound is never approached by the three-copy fixture, and nothing covered it being hit: running the rule over 101 copies of the bug returned the first 100 repaired and the 101st handed back exactly as it arrived, output that does not parse, and `analyze/1` reporting only 100 issues (last at line 398, nothing for the occurrence at line 402) — silently partial, indistinguishable from a full repair by the return value. Rather than just dropping the claim I corrected the comment (the three-copy test says nothing about the bound) and wrote the two tests that were missing, at the boundary itself: exactly 100 occurrences repaired in full with an exact whole-string comparison, and 101 pinned to `100 repaired copies <> 1 untouched copy` with `refute valid_syntax?` and the 100-issue count. Watched them fail for the right reason by temporarily moving `@max_passes` from 100 to 50 in the rule: exactly those two tests failed and no others, so they really do exercise the bound; the rule was restored byte-identical (`git diff` over `lib/` empty). To keep the cost down the two tests stack a four-line occurrence rather than the nine-line module fixture — the file's runtime goes from 0.05s to 0.8s. Also bound that occurrence and its repair as `@one_occurrence`/`@one_occurrence_repaired` so the two tests share one fixture.
- [3 nit] fixed — Confirmed the drift is real and silent: I renamed the module in the first `Local` fixture only (`defmodule Local` → `defmodule LocalDrifted`, in both its input and its expected output) and ran the file — 22 tests, 0 failures, with the second test's comment "this is the once-fixed `Local` source above" now plainly false and nothing complaining. Bound the fixture once as a module attribute `@local_source` and pointed both tests at it, so the source cannot differ between them by construction; reworded the second test's comment to say they are literally the same bytes. Re-ran the drift experiment against the fixed file: editing the shared attribute is now noticed immediately (the repair test fails on its expected output), which is the whole point — there is only one copy left to edit.
- [4 nit] fixed — Confirmed the assertion does not prove provenance: `match?([%Issue{meta: %{line: 5}}, %Issue{meta: %{line: 14}}, %Issue{meta: %{line: 23}}], issues)` returns `true` against a hand-built list of issues carrying `rule: :some_other_rule`, while the two-copy test's pattern (with `rule:`) returns `false` on the same list. Added `rule: :close_unclosed_fn_delimiter` to all three elements of the three-copy pattern, matching the assertion at the two-copy test.
- note: the behaviour test [2] now pins is worth a maintainer's eye on its own merits. Past `@max_passes` the rule returns a partially repaired source through the same `{:fixed, ...}` channel as a complete one, so a caller has no way to tell "I fixed everything" from "I fixed the first hundred and gave up" except by re-parsing the result. The sibling `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter` carries the same bound. Declining outright at the bound, or signalling the cut-off, would be a behaviour change and is not something I made on my own; the test is written so that changing it is deliberate rather than a surprise.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (modified, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:48 — the same 15-line `Solution` fixture is inlined byte-identically three times (lines 48-64, 87-103, 109-125), and the near-identical 9-line `TwoBugs` fixture twice more (lines 172-182, 203-213). This PR extracted `@local_source` and `@one_occurrence` precisely so shared fixtures "cannot drift apart"; the same treatment was not applied to the most-duplicated ones, so editing the input of "inserts missing end before ) and removes stray end on next line" without editing the other two silently leaves "fixed output no longer flags" and "fixed output is well-formed (parses)" asserting against a different program than the test they were written to accompany.

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs — fix round 3 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — Confirmed by execution, twice. First I checked the copies really are the same bytes: the three `Solution` heredocs (the repair test's input, and the inputs of "fixed output no longer flags" and "fixed output is well-formed") differ only in the variable name on the assignment line, and the two `TwoBugs` heredocs are byte-identical. Then I made each drift happen. (a) I renamed the module and the function in the *first* test's `Solution` fixture only — the edit the finding describes — and ran the file: 24 tests, 0 failures. Nothing noticed that the other two tests were now checking the repaired output of a program no test feeds them any more. (b) I added one blank line to the *first* `TwoBugs` copy, which shifts every occurrence in that fixture down a line, and ran the file: again 24 tests, 0 failures — while the line-number test kept asserting lines 5 and 14 against its own untouched second copy, arithmetic that no longer describes the fixture the repair test uses. The fix binds each fixture once as a module attribute, exactly as this PR already did for `@local_source` and `@one_occurrence`: `@solution_source` / `@solution_repaired` for the three tests, `@two_bugs` / `@two_bugs_repaired` for the two. No fixture byte changed — the attributes hold the same programs the tests were running, and all 24 tests pass unchanged. The pinning is the drift experiment re-run against the bound file, since "these two tests use the same input" is a property of the file, not something a test can assert about itself: (a) renaming the module in `@solution_source` alone now fails the repair test instead of passing silently; (b) editing `@solution_source` and `@solution_repaired` together so the repair leaves an unparseable leftover behind now fails "fixed output is well-formed (parses)" — proof that test really does follow the shared input rather than a stale copy of its own; (c) the blank-line edit to `@two_bugs` now fails the line-number test, which is the drift that was silent before. Gate: `mix format` on the touched file, `mix compile --warnings-as-errors` clean, `mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency` — 6 properties, 9077 tests, 0 failures — and `git status` clean apart from the wrapper's own ledgers. Committed as 5a38bf2.
- note: a third near-identical copy of the same nine-line module remains — the `ThreeBugs` fixture in "repairs every occurrence when the bug appears three times in one file". It is the `TwoBugs` program with the module renamed, so it is not one of the byte-identical copies the finding names, and folding it in would mean changing that test's input bytes. Left alone as out of scope; a maintainer may prefer one shared fixture for all three of those tests.
- commits: 5a38bf235673c78d01c68e5f49ec147be1491d24
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (modified, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:211 — four decline tests (PlainShape here, plus Reduced at :232, AboveReduced at :254, AboveWithTail at :272) name `NoUnclosedFnDelimiter` as the owner of the shape they hand off, but each fixture also carries `def broken(, do: 1`, and the sibling commits its insertion only when the *whole file* parses afterwards (lib/syntax/no_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex:66-77). So on these four inputs the sibling declines too and no rule repairs anything — the tests assert one half of a handoff that does not happen. Only the `Both` test at :495 actually calls the sibling, and it correctly asserts "neither fires". Adding the same two sibling assertions to these four would turn a misleading comment into a pinned fact.
- nit: test/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter_fix_test.exs:445 — `String.duplicate(@one_occurrence, 100)` is built twice in one test (once for `fix`, again for `analyze` on :449), so the `fix` result and the `analyze` result are compared against two separately constructed 400-line inputs. The very next test (:460) binds `input` once; this is a leftover of the same fixture-binding cleanup and also doubles the cost of a test that runs 100 repair passes.
- experiment: settles the first nit — shows the sibling leaves the PlainShape fixture byte-identical, i.e. nothing repairs it: mix run -e 'src = "defmodule PlainShape do\n  def a(m) do\n    Enum.map(m, fn r ->\n      if r == 0 do 1 else r end)\n    end\n  end\n\n  def broken(, do: 1\nend\n"; IO.inspect(Credence.Syntax.NoUnclosedFnDelimiter.fix(src) == src, label: "sibling also declines")'

## lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:17 — the "Not flagged" list is incomplete about what the rule declines, so it reads as broader coverage than it has: a destructuring left-hand side (`{:ok, val} =.foo()`), a module attribute (`@attr =.foo()`), a second `=` on the line (`x = y =.foo()`) and more than one space before the dot (`x =  .foo()`) are all left untouched because `@bad_pattern` demands a bare identifier at line start and at most one space. All four are pinned as no-ops in the tests; only the moduledoc omits them.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:62 — `@bad_pattern` carries no `u` modifier, so `[a-zA-Z_]\w*` is ASCII-only and an assignment to a non-ASCII identifier (`café =.make_ref()`, `Größe =.foo()`) is silently declined by both `analyze/1` and `fix/1`. Elixir accepts such identifiers, so this is a real missed repair — safe direction, but neither documented nor tested.
- nit: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:219 — the one test that covers the mixed case (real code repaired in a file that also documents the broken form) asserts with two `=~` substring checks instead of the exact whole-string `==` that `confirm_fix/2` — already imported at line 4 — provides. `=~` passes on output that is correct at those two spots and wrong anywhere else in the file, which is exactly the assertion style docs/02 rules out; the sibling tests in the same file all use `confirm_fix`.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:9 — the moduledoc has no `## Bad` / `## Good` block, so this new rule lands directly on the `@bad_good_ledger` exception list in test/rule_card_test.exs:59 (Rule Standard v1 requirement 6, which that gate describes as a ratchet at 40 of 43 compliant). Substantively the rule is fine for the self-corruption oracle — its two `→` examples at lines 14-15 do sit at the start of their heredoc lines and are the exact bytes it rewrites, and test line 224 proves it no longer rewrites its own source — so this is a heading rename, not missing content.
- experiment: settles the second nit's offset question before anyone adds `/u` — the splice at lines 89-90 depends on `return: :index` yielding byte offsets that `binary_part/3` can use: `mix run -e 'IO.inspect({Regex.run(~r/^(\s*[a-zA-Z_]\w*\s*=)\s?\.(?=[a-zA-Z_])/u, "café =.foo()", return: :index), Regex.run(~r/^(\s*[a-zA-Z_]\w*\s*=)\s?\.(?=[a-zA-Z_])/, "café =.foo()", return: :index)})'`

## lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule over the four shapes the moduledoc's "Not flagged" list did not mention: `{:ok, val} =.foo()`, `@attr =.foo()`, `x = y =.foo()` and `x =  .foo()` all come back byte-identical from `fix/1` and unreported by `analyze/1`, so the rule really is narrower than its documentation reads. Pinned with one list in the fix battery (`@declined`) that drives both a no-op-and-not-reported test per shape — two of the four had no test at all — and a new test asserting the moduledoc's "Not flagged" section names each of them; that test failed listing all four before the doc was edited. Then extended the list with the three structural declines plus a sentence saying they are deliberate declines (the pattern wants a bare identifier at the start of the line and at most one space before the dot). No behaviour change. Follow-up landed in the next commit: the new test originally read `Code.fetch_docs/1` directly, which `no_parser_calls_in_rule_tests_test.exs` forbids in `test/syntax` and which went red on the full suite; the read now goes through a new `Credence.RuleDuplication.moduledoc_section/2` in `test/support`, alongside the existing `example/2`. Commits c4e75ef, 99f053d.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed: `analyze("café =.make_ref()")` returned `[]` and `fix/1` returned the line unchanged, while the repaired form `café = make_ref()` parses fine and the dotted form is rejected with "syntax error before: '.'" — a repair the rule should have made and didn't. Pinned with five tests that all failed first: analyze flags `café =.make_ref()` and `größe =.byte_size(x)`, fix repairs both to the exact expected string, the repaired line parses and no longer flags, and the string the rule *actually* emits compiles (`{:ok, []}`) while the line it replaced, through the same compile, does not (`{:error, [severity: :error]}`) — the control that stops the compile check passing on anything. The fix widens the name to `[a-zA-Z_\x80-\xff][\w\x80-\xff]*`, i.e. byte-wise, rather than adding the `u` modifier the reviewer's experiment suggested. `/u` does return usable byte offsets (I ran that experiment: `{0,8},{0,7}` against `binary_part`-compatible byte positions), but `Regex.run` with `/u` raises `ArgumentError` on a subject that is not valid UTF-8 — I reproduced that with a stray `0xFF` byte — and output truncated mid-character is exactly the broken input this phase exists to repair, so `/u` would turn a decline into a crash inside the fix pipeline. A test with such a byte pins that the line is still repaired rather than raising. One correction to the finding: its other example, `Größe`, is not repairable — Elixir rejects a non-ASCII alias outright ("invalid character \"ö\" … in alias"), so that line fails to parse before and after. Commit a6402d1.
- [3 nit] fixed — Confirmed by executing the rule on that fixture and then corrupting its output: replacing the function body's return value so `go/0` returns `:corrupted` still satisfied both `=~` checks *and* the `valid_syntax?` check (`{true, true, true}`), while differing from what the rule emitted. So the test accepted output that is wrong everywhere except the two spots it inspected. The fixture is now bound once as `@mixed_source`, the test compares the whole emitted file against the expected file with `confirm_fix`, and a CONTROL test keeps the corrupted variant in the battery to show the substring checks accept it and whole-string equality rejects it. Commit 99f053d.
- [4 nit] fixed — Confirmed by deleting this rule's entry from `@bad_good_ledger` in `test/rule_card_test.exs` and running the gate: "the ledger only grows by argument" went red naming exactly `Credence.Syntax.FixAssignmentDotSyntax`. Added the missing `## Bad` / `## Good` block — the two admitted shapes (`ref =.make_ref()`, `x = .some_function(a)`) and their repairs — and left the ledger entry deleted, which the sibling "ledger only shrinks" test now requires. Checked the block is load-bearing rather than decorative: matched against the raw source the pattern fires on both new Bad lines, and the real masked `fix/1` still returns its own file byte-for-byte (4404 bytes in, 4404 out, a single-`eq` Myers diff), so the self-corruption oracle now has something to find and does not find it. Commit de4df77.
- note: `test/equivalence_inputs_test.exs:128` ("every dimension named by mix credence.equiv exists") is a latent flake, untouched by this session and unrelated to this rule. It asks `function_exported?(Credence.EquivalenceInputs, dim, 0)` without `Code.ensure_loaded/1`, and that returns `false` for a module that has not been loaded yet — so the gate passes only when some earlier test happens to load the module. Run alone it fails on every seed I tried (1, 2, 3); it failed once in a full-suite run here (seed 775447, reporting `--dim unicode_strings`) and passed in the next full run. Reproduction: `mix test test/equivalence_inputs_test.exs:128`; `function_exported?` answers `false` before `Code.ensure_loaded/1` and `true` after. The registry itself is fine — the gate is what is broken, and in the direction that hides a missing dimension.
- commits: de4df777292126a02b3703727ffb7133fb799e63 c4e75ef65dc31ab8ac7c214981d60cac20cfd24c a6402d1cf06fb83cbbbecd88e98b8c217ef83888 99f053dea4f998803f361bc48eb4f6c4065375c6
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:213 — "a line that is not valid UTF-8 is repaired, not crashed on" hides the 0xFF byte inside a trailing `#` comment, and `SourceMask.mask/1` blanks a comment byte-for-byte to 0x01 before the regex ever runs. The subject the pattern actually matches is pure ASCII, so this test passes identically with or without a `/u` modifier on `@bad_pattern` — it does not defend the byte-wise `\x80-\xff` design that lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:86-93 and the test's own comment at :208-212 say it exists for. The input that would reach the pattern un-blanked is a truncated multi-byte character in *code* position (e.g. `caf<0xC3> =.make_ref()`), and nothing tests it; since `Credence.Syntax.run_rule/2` (lib/syntax.ex:113) calls `rule.fix/1` with no rescue, a later "tidy the regex" change to `/u` would raise out of the whole fix pipeline with the suite green.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:94 — the lookahead `(?=[a-zA-Z_])` is ASCII-only while the left-hand identifier class was deliberately widened to `\x80-\xff`, so a non-ASCII *callee* (`x =.größe_von(y)`) is declined even though it is the same syntax error and carries none of the digit ambiguity the lookahead exists to avoid. Missed repair, and the moduledoc's "Not flagged" list does not name it.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:29 — "A second `=` on the line (`x = y =.foo()`)" overstates the decline: only an `=` appearing *before* the dot causes it. `x =.foo(a = 1)` has a second `=` on the line and is repaired, so a maintainer reading the list gets a wider abstention than the rule makes.
- experiment: settles the first finding — `mix run -e 'IO.inspect Credence.Syntax.FixAssignmentDotSyntax.fix(<<"caf", 0xC3, " =.make_ref()">>)'` (byte-wise pattern: returns the repaired binary; a `/u` pattern would raise instead).

## lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 concern] fixed — Confirmed the test was vacuous by running both regexes over what the pattern actually sees. For the old fixture (`ref =.make_ref() # <0xFF>`) masking blanks the whole comment, so the shadow is `ref =.make_ref() ` followed by three 0x01 bytes: `String.valid?(shadow)` is true and a `/u` version of the pattern matched it happily (`[{0,6},{0,5}]`). For a truncated character in *code* position (`caf<0xC3> =.make_ref()`) the shadow is still invalid and the same `/u` pattern raised "argument error", while the shipped byte-wise pattern matched and `fix/1` returned the repaired binary. So the existing test defended nothing, exactly as the finding said. Pinned it: added a test that feeds the truncated character in code position and asserts the whole repaired string, guarded by `refute String.valid?(shadow)` so it cannot silently go vacuous the same way. Proof it is load-bearing: I temporarily switched `@bad_pattern` to `/u`, ran the battery, and got exactly one failure — the new test, with `** (ArgumentError)` out of `fix_line/2` — while the old comment-byte test passed; then restored the rule. Also retitled the comment-byte test to claim only what it pins (the mask survives the stray byte). Test-only change, no rule behaviour touched. Commit 728ad65.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed a missed repair: `x =.über(y)` came back byte-identical from `fix/1` with `analyze/1` returning `[]`, while `def über(x)` is valid Elixir (a module defining and calling it compiled `{:ok, []}`). So the rule declined a line with the same syntax error it exists to repair. One correction to the finding: its own example, `x =.größe_von(y)`, was already being repaired — only the first character after the dot is inspected and that one is ASCII `g`, so the decline needs a callee whose *leading* byte is non-ASCII. Pinned with two tests that both failed first: the whole-string repair (`x =.über(y)` → `x = über(y)`), and a compile of the string the rule actually emits inside a module that defines `über/1`, with the unrepaired line through the same compile as the control (it failed with "syntax error before: '.'" before the fix, which is what made the emitted-string test red). Fix: widened the lookahead after the dot from `[a-zA-Z_]` to `[a-zA-Z_\x80-\xff]`, matching the byte-wise widening the left-hand name class already had. A high byte is not a digit, so none of the `rate = .05` / `x =.5e3` value ambiguity that the lookahead exists to avoid applies. Updated the moduledoc's digit section, which had listed the admitted characters as `a-z`, `A-Z`, `_` only. Commit 837ca0f.
- [3 nit] fixed — Confirmed the doc overstates: `x =.foo(a = 1)` has a second `=` on the line and `fix/1` returned `x = foo(a = 1)` with `analyze/1` reporting the issue, while `x = y =.foo()` (the `=` before the dot) came back unchanged. Pinned with two tests — the whole-string repair of `x =.foo(a = 1)`, and a check that the moduledoc's "Not flagged" list names that counterexample; the doc test failed first, quoting the missing example. Changed the list entry to say a second `=` declines only when it comes *before* the dot, and to name `x =.foo(a = 1)` as repaired. Documentation only, no behaviour change; the rule's "does not rewrite its own source file" test still passes, so the moduledoc edit did not disturb the self-corruption oracle. Commit 6bb2306.
- note: unrelated to this rule and not touched — `mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency` prints `** (UndefinedFunctionError) function MyAppTestMUEC.__ex_unit__/0 is undefined ... ExUnit.Runner.run_module/5` from a raised process during the run. It does not fail the suite (0 failures both runs), but it means some test compiles a module named `MyAppTestMUEC` into the ExUnit server's module list and the module is then unavailable when the runner reaches it — a test that defines modules at runtime is leaking a name into the runner. Worth a maintainer look because a genuinely broken test module would be swallowed the same way.
- commits: 728ad65d8d0d41464036f80dbd61620061daf5f7 837ca0ff025fadbb2470b8a04c5428f87ecc5d10 6bb2306c512b5a7d6cc60f846acedec83240b54e
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:99 — the pattern is `^`-anchored, so only the FIRST `=.` on a line is repaired, and after that repair `analyze/1` reports nothing at all for the line. On `a =.foo(b =.bar())` the fix emits `a = foo(b =.bar())`, which still does not parse, yet the rule now says the line is clean — so the round's phase-level "still does not parse" rollback discards the partial repair and a re-run cannot converge. No test pins a second occurrence on one line, and the "round-trip / fixed output no longer flags" test (test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:301) reads as a self-consistency proof but would pass here for the wrong reason.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:97 — the comment justifies the byte-wise `\x80-\xff` class with "a high byte reaching the pattern is part of an identifier, never of a literal". The second half is true (masking blanks literals) but the conclusion is not: a non-identifier character in code position keeps its bytes in the shadow — `Credence.SourceMask`'s own moduledoc (lib/source_mask.ex:445-459) exists precisely because an em dash can sit in code position in source that does not parse. So `x =.—dash()` is flagged and rewritten to `x = —dash()`, which still does not parse; the rule reports and "repairs" a line it cannot actually help. The direction is benign (broken in, broken out) and `\p{L}` is not available without the `/u` modifier the moduledoc deliberately rejects, so this is a claim to soften rather than a behaviour to change — but nothing in the test battery pins it either way.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:126 — `x=.foo()` (no space before the `=`) is admitted by `\s*=` and repaired to `x= foo()`: the callback appends exactly one space after the `=` but never normalises the space before it, so the emitted line parses and means the right thing while being the one admitted shape whose output is not `mix format`-clean. No test covers a spaceless `=`, so this shape's output is unpinned.
- experiment: settles the first finding — `mix run -e 'r = Credence.Syntax.FixAssignmentDotSyntax; f = r.fix("a =.foo(b =.bar())"); IO.inspect(f); IO.inspect(r.analyze(f))'` (expect `"a = foo(b =.bar())"` and `[]`).
- experiment: settles the second finding — `mix run -e 'r = Credence.Syntax.FixAssignmentDotSyntax; IO.inspect(r.analyze("x =.—dash()")); IO.inspect(r.fix("x =.—dash()"))'` (expect one issue and `"x = —dash()"`, which still does not parse).

## lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex — fix round 3 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule: `fix("a =.foo(b =.bar())")` emitted `"a = foo(b =.bar())"`, and `analyze/1` on that emitted line returned `[]`. So the rule handed back a line that still does not parse and then called it clean — the leftover `=.` sits past the start of the line, where the `^`-anchored pattern cannot see it, so no later pass can find it and the phase's "still does not parse" rollback throws the half-repair away. Pinned it two ways, both red before the change: the shape went into the file's `@declined` list (which drives a no-op test, a not-reported test, and a check that the moduledoc names it — all three failed), plus a test that spells out the dead end by asserting that neither the input nor the half-repaired line parses and that nothing reports the half-repaired line afterwards. Fix: `analyze/1` and `fix/1` now share one `repair/2` predicate, so a line is reported exactly when it is repaired, and a line whose remainder still carries the same shape is declined whole rather than half-rewritten — a repair that cannot finish the line is worth less than none. The remainder is inspected in the mask's shadow, like the match itself, so a `=.` inside a string or a trailing comment cannot decline a line the rule can repair (`x =.foo("b =.bar()") # c =.d()` is still repaired; pinned). Every other admitted input is byte-identical: the whole battery, including the rule's own-source self-corruption test, passed unchanged. Moduledoc's "Not flagged" list gained the new decline and the reason for it. Commit 9ee0d76.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed the comment's justification is false, by execution: `analyze("x =.—dash()")` returns one issue and `fix/1` emits `"x = —dash()"`, which the parser still rejects (`unexpected token: "—" (column 5, code point U+2014)`). An em dash is a high byte in code position, so masking — which only blanks literals and comments — does not make it "part of an identifier". Corrected the comment to claim only what masking gives (the byte did not come from a literal), to name the counterexample and where it comes from (`Credence.SourceMask`'s own moduledoc exists because an em dash can sit in code position), and to state the trade: broken in, broken out, and telling a letter from an em dash would need `\p{L}`, which needs the `/u` modifier the byte-wise class deliberately avoids. No behaviour change — as the finding asked, a claim softened rather than a behaviour altered — and the behaviour is now pinned by a test that asserts the whole emitted string and that neither the input nor the output parses, so it cannot drift silently in either direction. Commit 808e33a.
- [3 nit] refuted (as a defect) — The behaviour reproduces exactly as described: `fix("x=.foo()")` returns `"x= foo()"`, and for the same reason `fix("x  =.foo()")` returns `"x  = foo()"` — the callback appends one space after the `=` and never touches the bytes in front of it. What I refute is that this is a fault in the rule. This repo's own rule-review process puts layout out of a rule's scope in as many words (docs/02:216, "Spacing and layout don't matter — `mix format` runs after all the rules"), the emitted line parses, and the string the rule *actually* emits compiles inside a module (`{:ok, []}`) while the line it replaced fails the same compile with a syntax error — so the output is right in meaning, only unconventional in spacing. Normalising it would also rewrite the spacing of `x  =.foo()`, which has nothing to do with the spurious dot. The part of the finding that was real is the missing coverage, and that is not policy, so I wrote it: two tests now pin the emitted bytes for both spacings and the compiled meaning of what the rule emits, with the unrepaired line as the control. Commit 81cc0b6.
- note: after [1], the rule still has one shape where it emits a rewrite that cannot make the line parse — the em-dash case from [2] (`x =.—dash()` → `x = —dash()`). The per-rule progress guard keeps it, because "still does not parse" is deliberately not a regression in this round, so the file-level outcome is unchanged; but a maintainer may want the same "decline when the repair cannot finish" discipline applied there. It cannot be done with the current byte-wise class, which cannot tell a letter from a dash without `/u`; it would need a small exclusion list of lead bytes that can never start an Elixir identifier. Left unfixed: out of scope, and it is a design call about how much Unicode knowledge belongs in a pattern that must survive invalid UTF-8.
- commits: 9ee0d7667791456a4503bd87f15e431a479731d4 808e33a5853160cb2978d91d5530b8d70715be3d 81cc0b69ebae63f1c079ff38028de09c913974f0
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:32 — the "Not flagged" entry "A second `=.` later on the same line" reads as unconditional, but `@second_occurrence` (line 121) requires the second `=` to be preceded by a *bare identifier*, so a second `=.` whose left-hand side ends in `)`, `]` or `}` slips past it: on `a =.foo(b[:k] =.bar())` the tail is scanned as `foo(b[:k] =.bar())`, the byte before ` =` is `]`, nothing matches, and the line is prefix-repaired to `a = foo(b[:k] =.bar())` — which still does not parse and which `analyze/1` then reports as clean. That is precisely the "dead end" the guard and lines 39-44 say the rule refuses to create. The inputs that reach it are contrived (a second broken assignment on one line, with a destructured or indexed left-hand side), and every one of them has a left-hand side this rule declines anyway, so no repair is lost — but the moduledoc bullet and the test comment at test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:210-215 both state the invariant more broadly than the code holds it, and no fixture pins the hole in either direction.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:108 — the comment justifying the em-dash trade cites `Credence.SourceMask`'s moduledoc: "Punctuation sits in code position too: `Credence.SourceMask`'s own moduledoc exists because an em dash can, in source that does not parse." That moduledoc (lib/source_mask.ex:2-70) is entirely about rules rewriting the *inside of string literals* — its worked example is the four rules that corrupted `IO.puts("100% done")` — and it never mentions punctuation in code position or em dashes. A maintainer following the pointer to check why a high byte in the shadow is not necessarily an identifier byte finds no such argument there. The trailing clause ("because an em dash can, in source that does not parse") is also missing its verb. The behaviour the paragraph describes is correct and is pinned at test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:380; only the citation is wrong — cite this rule's own pinned test, or drop the cross-reference.
- experiment: settles the first finding — `mix run -e 'r = Credence.Syntax.FixAssignmentDotSyntax; f = r.fix("a =.foo(b[:k] =.bar())"); IO.inspect(f); IO.inspect(r.analyze(f))'` (expect `"a = foo(b[:k] =.bar())"` and `[]`, i.e. a half-repaired line the rule no longer reports).

## test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs:106 — the test is named "second `=` on the line is not flagged", but the rule only declines an `=` that comes *before* the dot; `x =.foo(a = 1)` also has a second `=` on the line and is flagged and repaired. The rule's moduledoc was amended for exactly this overstatement and the fix test gates the amendment (test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:243-254), so this test name is the last unqualified copy of the wider claim. Rename it to say "a second `=` before the dot", and consider mirroring the `assert analyze("x =.foo(a = 1)") != []` case (fix_test:240) into this file so the analyze side pins both directions of the boundary.
- nit: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs:78 — neither comment test in this file would go red if `Credence.SourceMask` were removed from `analyze/1`. On the raw line `# ref =.make_ref()` the `^`-anchored pattern fails on the leading `#` (not in `[a-zA-Z_\x80-\xff]`), and on `x = 1 # ref =.make_ref()` (line 110) it fails because a `1` follows `= `, not a dot. Both pass by the anchor, exactly the accident the rule's own moduledoc says the "Not flagged" list used to rely on (lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:65-70, docs/22 T3.10). The only analyze-side test where masking is actually load-bearing lives in the sibling file (fix_test:459-467, an assignment inside a `@moduledoc` heredoc); a heredoc case belongs in this file too, so the analyze half is not one file-move away from unguarded.
- experiment: settles the second nit — both results must be `nil`, showing the two comment tests pass on the unmasked line: elixir -e 'p = ~r/^(\s*[a-zA-Z_\x80-\xff][\w\x80-\xff]*\s*=)\s?\.(?=[a-zA-Z_\x80-\xff])/; IO.inspect({Regex.run(p, "# ref =.make_ref()"), Regex.run(p, "x = 1 # ref =.make_ref()")})'

## test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule, not by reading it: `analyze("x = y =.foo()")` returns `[]` (declined) but `analyze("x =.foo(a = 1)")` returns an issue, so the test named "second `=` on the line is not flagged" asserted only the declining half while its name claimed the rule abstains from any line with a second `=`. That is the wider claim the rule's moduledoc was already amended to retract. Renamed the test to "a second `=` before the dot is not flagged" and added the mirrored flagging case — "flags `x =.foo(a = 1)` — a second `=` after the dot is ordinary code" — so this file pins both directions of the boundary instead of one. To prove the new test is load-bearing I temporarily made the rule decline on ANY `=` later in the line (the behaviour the old name described): exactly the new test went red, and the renamed one stayed green. Reverted the mutation; the file is green again (21 tests, 0 failures).
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed with the reviewer's experiment: run against the RAW (unmasked) lines, the rule's anchored pattern returns `nil` for both `# ref =.make_ref()` and `x = 1 # ref =.make_ref()` — the first because the leading `#` cannot start an identifier, the second because a `1` follows `= `, not a dot. Both comment tests therefore pass by the `^` anchor alone and would not notice if `Credence.SourceMask` were dropped from `analyze/1`. Added an analyze case for an assignment that only appears inside a `@moduledoc` heredoc, where the broken line starts its own line and the anchor no longer saves the rule. Watched it fail for the right reason: with masking removed from `analyze/1` (replaced by a plain line split), the new test reported an issue on line 2 while both comment tests stayed green — one failure, exactly the new one. Reverted the mutation. The analyze half is now guarded by masking in its own file rather than only in the sibling fix battery.

## test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs:46 — the comment claims "Only an `=` *before* the dot declines; an `=` after it is an ordinary keyword-less argument and the line is flagged like any other" (restated at :131). That is one notch too wide: `a =.foo(b =.bar())` carries its second `=` *after* the dot and is declined, by the `@second_occurrence` check at lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:121 (the tail after the first match is `foo(b =.bar())`, which matches, so `repair/2` returns `:decline`). The rule's own moduledoc lists that shape as a separate decline (lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:32) and the fix battery pins `analyze("a =.foo(b =.bar())") == []` (test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:225). The real boundary is "an `=` after the dot declines only when it forms a second `=.`" — a maintainer reading these two comments as the spec would expect that line to be repaired. Concrete fix: qualify both comments, and add the `a =.foo(b =.bar())` no-issue case to this file, so the "both directions" the commit message promises is actually the three the rule distinguishes. This is also the only branch of `repair/2` that this analyze battery never exercises; the suite covers it, but only from the fix file.
- nit: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs:91 — "The two comment lines above" points at tests that are not both above it. Only `# ref =.make_ref()` (:87) precedes the comment; the other case it names, `x = 1 # ref =.make_ref()`, is at :138, in a different `describe`. The claim itself is correct — I checked both raw lines against `@bad_pattern` and neither matches without masking, so the heredoc test at :97 really is the load-bearing one — only the locator is wrong.

## test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule, not by reading it: `analyze("a =.foo(b =.bar())")` returns `[]` (the line is declined whole) while `analyze("x =.foo(a = 1)")` returns an issue and `analyze("x = y =.foo()")` returns `[]`. So the two comments in this file, which said an `=` after the dot is always ordinary code and the line is "flagged like any other", described a rule that does not exist — there are three shapes, not two. I also confirmed the second half of the finding, that this battery never reaches that decline: I temporarily disabled the second-occurrence check in `repair/2`, making the rule behave exactly as the comments claimed, and the whole file stayed green (21 tests, 0 failures). Pinned it by adding the missing no-issue case `analyze("a =.foo(b =.bar())") == []`; under the same mutation it was the only failure, quoting the issue the rule should not have reported. Reverted the mutation (the rule file is byte-identical to HEAD). Then corrected both comments to state the boundary the rule actually holds: an `=` before the dot declines, an `=` after the dot declines only when it forms a second `=.`, and a plain `=` after the dot is flagged. The new test's comment also says why the rule declines rather than half-repairs — the pattern is anchored at the start of the line, so a repair could only reach the first `=.` and nothing would report the leftover. Test-only change; full gate green. Commit 3ee4fdf.
- [2 nit] fixed — Confirmed the locator is wrong by looking at where the tests actually are: the comment says "the two comment lines above", but only `# ref =.make_ref()` (line 87) precedes it; the other case it names, `x = 1 # ref =.make_ref()`, is at line 138 in a different `describe`. The claim the comment makes is right, and I checked it by running the rule's own pattern against the raw, unmasked lines: both commented lines return `nil` (so both tests pass by the `^` anchor alone and would not notice if masking were dropped), while the heredoc body line `x =.some_function(a)` does match raw — which is what makes the heredoc test the load-bearing one. Fixed by rewording the comment to name where each case lives ("the one just above and `x = 1 # ref =.make_ref()`, in the last `describe`") instead of depending on relative position, so it cannot go stale again when tests move. Comment-only; no assertion changed. Same commit 3ee4fdf.

## test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs:11 — the `Issue` struct is matched on `rule` and `meta.line` only; `message` is never asserted here, and the fix test does not assert it either, so this rule's user-facing text ("Extra dot after `=` in assignment — …", lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:180) could be emptied or made wrong with the whole suite green. No meta-gate covers issue messages. Sibling analyze tests pin it exactly — e.g. test/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax_analyze_test.exs:13 and test/syntax/fix_ets_match_spec_erlang_less_than_analyze_test.exs:23 both use `message == "…"`; one such assertion on the first flagging test would close it.
- nit: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_analyze_test.exs:45 — the comment opens "The flagging one of the three shapes the second-`=` boundary distinguishes;" which is a fragment with no verb, so the sentence never says what is being claimed about that shape. The rest of the comment is accurate; only the first clause needs a verb (e.g. "This is the flagging one of the three shapes …").

Everything else checked out. Verified by reading against lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex and lib/source_mask.ex:

- All 22 assertions are behaviourally correct against the rule's regexes. Traced by hand: `x =.foo(a = 1)` flags (the tail `foo(a = 1)` has no `=` immediately followed by `.`+identifier, so `@second_occurrence` misses and the repair runs); `x = y =.foo()` and `@attr =.foo()` decline on the `^`-anchor; `a =.foo(b =.bar())` declines on the `@second_occurrence` branch; `f =.(1)`, `rate = .05`, `x =.5`, `x =.5e3` decline on the lookahead; `a == b` / `a >= b` / `a <= b` / `%{a: 1} = expr` decline. Both Unicode cases match the byte-wise class (`café`, `größe`).
- No text-vs-meaning weakness: every negative uses exact `== []`, and every positive is a whole-list struct match that pins arity (so a spurious extra issue fails), not a `=~`/`String.contains?` check.
- Masking is genuinely load-bearing, and the comment at line 92 that says so is correct: unmasked, `# ref =.make_ref()` fails on the leading `#` and `x = 1 # ref =.make_ref()` fails on the `1` after `= `, but the heredoc body at line 101 does start its own line and would be flagged without `Credence.SourceMask`. So the heredoc test at line 99 is the one that would go red if masking were dropped.
- No `defmodule Example` collision: the `defmodule Example` at line 21 is inside a heredoc string handed to `analyze/1`, never compiled. Nothing in this file compiles or parses anything, so failure class 6 does not apply.
- Line numbers are trustworthy: `SourceMask.lines/1` zips `String.split(source, "\n")` with the same split of a byte-length-preserving shadow, so masked multi-line regions cannot shift the index that `Enum.with_index(1)` reports.
- Not inert: seven inputs are asserted to produce an issue, and the end-to-end repair (including the compiled-emission checks) is pinned in the fix test.
- The two shapes from the moduledoc's "Not flagged" list that are absent here (`{:ok, val} =.foo()` and `x =  .foo()`) are covered on the analyze side by the `@declined` loop at test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:188, which asserts `analyze(code) == []` for each. Not a gap.

## test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] fixed — The rule declines to touch `f =.(1)` (anonymous-call syntax), and the test file pinned that as a no-op in a test of its own. But the file also keeps a list, `@declined`, whose comment says it holds "everything the pattern declines", and one test walks that list to check the rule's moduledoc actually names each shape under "## Not flagged". Because `f =.(1)` sat outside the list, that check never looked at it — and the moduledoc indeed never mentioned anonymous-call syntax, so a reader of the rule saw a shorter list of exceptions than the rule really has. Confirmed by executing the rule: `analyze("f =.(1)")` returns `[]` and `fix("f =.(1)")` returns the line unchanged (a real decline), while printing the "Not flagged" section and testing whether it contains `f =.(1)` returned false. Pinned by moving `"f =.(1)"` into `@declined` and deleting the now-redundant standalone test; the consistency test then failed, naming exactly `f =.(1)` as undocumented. Fixed by adding one bullet to the rule's "Not flagged" list: anonymous-call syntax is declined because `(` cannot start an identifier and the two possible repairs mean different things (`f = (1)` versus the call `f.(1)`), so it is left for a human. The bullet is inserted next to the digit bullet — the closest reason — and before the four bullets that a later sentence counts as "the last four", so that sentence stays true. Moving the entry also strengthens the pin, since the `@declined` loop asserts `analyze/1 == []` in addition to the no-op, which the standalone test did not. Proven: the file's 44 tests pass, both batteries for this rule (66 tests) pass, `mix format`, `mix compile --warnings-as-errors`, and the full `mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency` gate (9102 tests, 0 failures) are green, including the self-corruption test that runs this rule's fix over its own source. Committed as 8276ac8.
