## lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- concern: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:125 — the "second `=.` on the line" guard only sees a second occurrence whose left-hand side ends in an identifier byte, because `@second_occurrence` starts with `[a-zA-Z_\x80-\xff]`. A second `=.` written after a closing bracket slips through: on `x =.foo({:ok, v} =.bar())` the rule repairs the prefix and emits `x = foo({:ok, v} =.bar())`, which still does not parse and no longer matches the `^`-anchored `@bad_pattern`, so `analyze/1` afterwards reports the line as clean and nothing ever surfaces the leftover — exactly the half-repaired dead end the moduledoc (lines 43-48) and the test at test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:215 say the rule refuses to create. The same shape with an identifier on the left (`x =.foo(b =.bar())`) is declined, and that is the only shape either test file pins, so the gap is invisible to the suite.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:165 — the match's start offset is bound to `_match_start` and discarded, and `match_len` is then used as an *absolute* byte offset in both `binary_part(shadow, match_len, ...)` (line 166) and `binary_part(line, match_len, ...)` (line 173). That is only correct while `@bad_pattern` keeps its `^` anchor and never gains the `m` modifier; if the anchor is ever dropped the splice cuts at the wrong byte and silently emits a mangled line rather than failing. Writing `match_start + match_len` costs nothing and removes the coupling.
- nit: lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:25 — the `## Not flagged` bullet `String literals (msg = "=.not_a_dot")` is the only entry in that list with no corresponding no-op in the `@declined` battery (test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:171). The moduledoc gate at that file's line 194 checks only the other direction — that every pinned decline is named — so this claim is documented but unpinned. Adding the string to `@declined` closes it.
- experiment: settles the first finding — `mix run -e 'alias Credence.Syntax.FixAssignmentDotSyntax, as: R; out = R.fix("x =.foo({:ok, v} =.bar())"); IO.inspect(out); IO.inspect(R.analyze(out)); IO.inspect(match?({:ok, _}, Sourceror.parse_string(out)))'` — a repaired-but-unparseable `out` with an empty analyze list confirms the dead-end line the guard was written to prevent.

## test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-19 (added, test_syntax)
- nit: test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs:429 — in `@mixed_source` the `@moduledoc` heredoc body (`doc =.example()`) sits in column 0 while its closing `"""` is indented two spaces, so the fixture is an *outdented heredoc line*; Elixir accepts it but emits a tokenizer warning ("heredoc line does not have enough left-hand side indentation ... may cause errors in Elixir v2.0 and later"), which `valid_syntax?/1` at lines 485 and 497 triggers on every run. Moving the closing `"""` (line 430, and the matching one in the `expected` literal at line 474) to column 0 keeps exactly what the fixture is for — a doc line that begins with the shape the rule matches — with no warning and no future-Elixir fragility.
- experiment: settles the nit above — `mix test test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs 2>&1 | grep -i "outdented heredoc"`

## test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs — fix round 2 (2026-08-19)
- [1 nit] refuted — The finding says the `@mixed_source` fixture is an outdented heredoc (doc line in column 0, closing `"""` in column 2) and that this makes Elixir print a "outdented heredoc line" warning every time `valid_syntax?/1` parses it, at lines 485 and 497. The first half is true; the second half — the only part that is a defect — is not. I ran the reviewer's own experiment, `mix test test/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax_fix_test.exs 2>&1 | grep -i "outdented heredoc"`: no matches (grep exits 1), across a run of all 44 tests in the file. I then ran the whole fast gate, `mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency`, and grepped its complete output the same way: 9102 tests, 0 failures, and zero occurrences of "outdented heredoc" anywhere in the suite. So no warning is emitted on any run, let alone every run. The reason is that `valid_syntax?/1` (test/support/rule_case.ex:104) parses with `Sourceror.parse_string/1`, and Sourceror passes `emit_warnings: false` to Elixir's parser (deps/sourceror/lib/sourceror.ex:196). I proved that is what suppresses it rather than guessing, by feeding the identical string to both parsers in one script: `Sourceror.parse_string/1` returned `{:ok, _}` silently, while `Code.string_to_quoted/1` on the same bytes printed the "outdented heredoc line" warning. The fixture is therefore genuinely outdented but structurally silent, and no code was changed — nothing reproduced to fix. Two things worth recording for the maintainer, neither a defect today. First, the column-0 placement of the doc line is not what makes the fixture work: the rule's `@bad_pattern` (lib/syntax/fix_assignment_dot_syntax.ex:118) opens with `^(\s*[a-zA-Z_...`, so it matches an indented doc line just as well, and the reviewer's proposed edit — moving the two closing `"""` to column 0 — would have been behaviour-neutral rather than a repair. Second, the residual risk the finding gestures at is real but hypothetical: Elixir currently warns about outdented heredocs and has said they may become an error in v2.0, so if that lands, this fixture would fail at the point `Sourceror` parses it, not at the point the test file is compiled (the fixture is a `~S'''` string literal, so nothing about it is parsed as a heredoc until the rule's output is checked). That is a decision about pre-emptively editing an oracle fixture against a future compiler, on a tree where the current behaviour is clean; it is not something the present code gets wrong.
- gate: skipped (no commits)

## lib/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type.ex — 2026-08-19 (added, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type.ex:109 — `toggle_heredoc/2` decides "am I inside a heredoc?" by counting `"""`/`'''` per line and flipping on an odd count, so any line whose delimiter count is odd for a reason other than being a delimiter inverts the state for the rest of the file. On input like `@moduledoc """` / `Charlist heredocs open with '''.` / (blank) / `  @type t :: () -> any()` / `"""`, the `'''` line flips the tracker off and the rule rewrites the documentation prose to `  @type t :: (() -> any())` — silently changing the text the module's `@moduledoc` produces, which is exactly the corruption the moduledoc at :46-50 claims this tracking prevents. The same flip runs the other way (a code line containing one `"""`, e.g. `String.split(x, ~s("""))`, turns tracking *on* and the rule goes inert for the remainder of the file with every gate green). The project already has the primitive for this, with the opposite failure direction stated in its own docs: `Credence.SourceMask.lines/1` + `self_contained?/2` (lib/source_mask.ex:224, :255) — "the failure direction is a missed fix on a line inside a multi-line literal, never a corrupted one" — and `PreferSpecArrowOperator` (lib/syntax/prefer_spec_arrow_operator.ex:25-31) documents that it was converted to it precisely because it had been rewriting the `## Bad` example in its own moduledoc.
- blocker (same root cause, evidence angle — do not fix separately): the self-corruption oracle (test/self_corruption_test.exs) is green on this rule only by accident. Line 48 of the rule's own source carries a single `'''` inside the moduledoc, which flips the tracker off for lines 49-51 and then back on at line 51, so lines 52-217 — the entire implementation — are handed to `fix/1` tagged `{:heredoc, _}` and skipped. The `## Bad` example at :11 is protected by the same accident. The gate that exists to catch this class therefore supplies no evidence here, and its own moduledoc says a clean result is weak evidence, not proof of literal-awareness.
- concern: lib/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type.ex:125 — `target/1` judges "self-contained right-hand side" one line at a time, so a typespec whose return type continues onto the next line is flagged and rewritten into a differently-broken shape. On `@type t :: () -> {:ok, term()}` followed by `  | {:error, term()}`, the first line passes every gate (balanced, one top-level arrow, ends on `}`) and becomes `@type t :: (() -> {:ok, term()})`, orphaning the `|` continuation. The net user-visible outcome is a missed repair rather than shipped damage — the round's all-or-nothing rollback (lib/syntax.ex:151) discards it because the result still does not parse — but the check reports a finding the fix cannot actually repair, and it burns the round for any other rule whose repair was riding along. The moduledoc's "self-contained: brackets balanced, no second `::`, no `when` guard" list does not mention the line-locality assumption.
- nit: test/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type_analyze_test.exs:63 — `assert issue.message =~ "Wrap the function type in parens"` is a substring assertion on the one thing the user reads; pin the whole message with `==`, as the recent campaign commits did for the other Syntax rules.
- nit: test/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type_fix_test.exs:284-301 — the 1008-combination battery is vacuous-safe only by luck: if the rule rewrote nothing at all, `rewritten?` would be `false` for every combination, `assert length(analyze(line)) == 0` would hold, and the `valid_syntax?` branch would never run, so the whole loop passes green against a dead rule. Count the rewrites and assert the total (the intended figure is derivable from the fixture lists) so the battery cannot pass on an inert rule.
- experiment: settles the blocker — `mix run -e 'q = String.duplicate(<<39>>, 3); src = Enum.join(["defmodule M do", "  @moduledoc \"\"\"", "  Charlist heredocs open with " <> q <> ".", "", "  @type t :: () -> any()", "  \"\"\"", "end"], "\n"); IO.puts(Credence.Syntax.FixBareTupleZeroInType.fix(src))'` — the `@type` line inside the moduledoc comes back rewritten to `(() -> any())`.
- experiment: settles the "green oracle by accident" claim — `mix run -e 'src = File.read!("lib/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type.ex") <> "\n@type z :: () -> any()\n"; IO.inspect(Credence.Syntax.FixBareTupleZeroInType.analyze(src))'` — returns `[]`, because the tracker believes the rule's own implementation body (and everything after it) is heredoc prose.

## lib/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type.ex — fix round 1 (2026-08-19)
- [1 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule's own `fix/1` over a module whose `@moduledoc` prose contains the sentence "Charlist heredocs open with '''.": the `@type t :: () -> any()` line two lines below it came back rewritten to `@type t :: (() -> any())`, i.e. the rule edited the documentation text the module produces. The cause was the per-line delimiter count in `toggle_heredoc/2`, which flips its "am I inside a heredoc?" state on any odd number of `"""`/`'''` on a line. The opposite direction reproduced too: a line of real code holding `~s(""")` turned the tracking *on*, and `analyze/1` returned `[]` for a perfectly ordinary `@type` line below it — the rule silently going inert with every gate green. The reviewer's second blocker is the same root cause seen from the oracle's side, and it also reproduced: appending `@type z :: () -> any()` to the rule's own source file and calling `analyze/1` returned `[]`, because a lone `'''` in the rule's own moduledoc made the tracker treat the entire implementation body as prose — so the self-corruption gate was supplying no evidence about this rule at all. Pinned with three tests (an analyze no-issue and a fix byte-identity on the charlist-prose moduledoc, plus an analyze/fix pair proving the rule keeps working after a code line carrying a lone triple quote); all failed on the old code. The fix replaces the hand-rolled delimiter counter with `Credence.SourceMask.lines/1` + `self_contained?/2`, the same primitive `PreferSpecArrowOperator` was converted to after it rewrote its own moduledoc; its documented failure direction is a missed fix inside a multi-line literal, never a corrupted one. Re-running both reproductions afterwards: the moduledoc prose comes back byte-identical, and the appended-typespec probe now reports the issue at line 267 instead of `[]` — so the self-corruption gate now actually exercises this rule.
- [2 concern] fixed — Confirmed on `@type t :: () -> {:ok, term()}` followed by `  | {:error, term()}`: `analyze/1` flagged line 1 and `fix/1` produced `@type t :: (() -> {:ok, term()})` with the `|` line left dangling. One correction to the finding's own reasoning, which matters for severity: this is not discarded by the round's all-or-nothing rollback. Comparing the parsed syntax trees of the two forms shows the rewritten source *does* parse and compile — Elixir treats a line opening with `|` as a continuation — but as `(() -> {:ok, term()}) | {:error, term()}`, a union of a function type and a tuple, rather than the `() -> {:ok, term()} | {:error, term()}` the author wrote. So the output is silently a different type, which is the failure mode nothing downstream can catch, not a missed repair. Pinned with six tests (analyze and fix, for a `|` continuation, a `|` continuation separated by a blank line, and a `when` guard on the line below); all six failed on the old code. The fix threads the following line into the one shared predicate — `target/1` became `target/2`, still called by both `analyze/1` and `fix/1`, so there is still exactly one copy of the decision — and declines when the next non-blank line opens with `|`, `,`, `->`, `::`, `when` or a closing bracket: tokens that cannot start a statement, so they are the tail of the typespec above. Blank lines are looked past because a blank line does not end a typespec either (verified against the parser).
- [3 nit] fixed — The message assertion was `assert issue.message =~ "Wrap the function type in parens"`, a substring check on the one string the user reads. Replaced with a whole-string `==` against the exact message the rule builds. This one passes both before and after by construction — it is a strengthening, not a defect repair — and it is committed with the rest.
- [4 nit] fixed — The 1008-combination battery only ever asserted "check and fix agree" and "the rewrite parses", both of which hold trivially if the rule rewrites nothing, so the loop would have stayed green against a dead rule. The loop now collects each combination's `rewritten?` flag and asserts the total is `5 * 3 * 5 * 3` = 225: the five attribute prefixes the rule accepts (`@typedoc` and `def f` are not typespec attributes), all three separator spellings, the five wrappable right-hand sides out of twelve, and the three suffixes that are not a trailing comment. Verified the figure is the one the current rule actually produces, and verified it is load-bearing by the fact that it is an exact count rather than a lower bound.
- note: the moduledoc's "What is deliberately left alone" list says `@spec f(a) :: () -> any() when a: var` is skipped because "guard belongs outside", but the actual reason the rule declines it is the blanket `not Regex.match?(~r/\bwhen\b/, tail)` in `wrappable?/1` — which also declines a return type that merely *contains* the word `when` inside a nested type name (e.g. `() -> when_clause()`). Harmless today (it only costs a fix), but the stated reason and the implemented reason are not the same predicate. Not fixed: outside the numbered findings.
- commits: 2c059a63515b69ebcec4e4de984824201b5f2dc7
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_brace.ex — fix round 5 (2026-08-20)
- [1 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by executing the rule, not by reading it. On `defmodule E do` / `  def foo do` / `    x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()` / `  end` / `end` the rule committed `x = {1, 2 |> IO.inspect()}`, i.e. the tuple `{1, IO.inspect(2)}`. I then parsed both readings: `x = {1, 2} |> IO.inspect()` parses too and gives a different syntax tree, so the rule was silently picking one of two meanings — the one thing this rule promises never to do, and the very shape it has refused since fix round 1 when the author happens to break the line after the `2`. The cause is structural: the "could the `}` have belonged somewhere else?" scan only ever appends braces to the *end* of lines *above* the line it repairs, so when the literal opens on that same line the scan has nothing to try, and a placement in the middle of that line is never tried on any input. The second shape the finding names — `x = {1,` / `    2 |> IO.inspect()` — reproduced for the same reason (the line above ends in `,` and is skipped). Pinned with four tests that failed on the old code showing exactly the wrong output: two in the fix battery asserting each source comes back byte-for-byte unchanged, and two in the analyze battery asserting no issue is reported (the rule must never flag what it will not fix). Fixed by weighing placements *inside* the line being repaired as well: every position in that line is tried, with the missing braces shared between that position and the end of the line, exactly the way rival placements are already shared between two lines. Braces landing inside a string, charlist or comment on that line need no masking — they close nothing, so such a candidate is left short of closers and simply cannot parse; the rule's source now states that argument. Proven: a differential over 15,625 generated truncated sources against the previous commit gives 15,590 byte-identical answers, 0 newly repaired, 0 repairs changed, and 35 newly refused — and for every one of those 35 I reconstructed a rival placement that parses to a different syntax tree than what the old rule emitted, so no refusal is a false alarm. One existing test needed attention: the exhaustive split-search test guards itself against becoming vacuous by requiring more than 500 rival completions to have been searched, and a stricter rule repairs fewer sources, so that count fell from 621 to 456. I did not touch the assertion; I widened the search's fragment list with one two-brace opening that has no rival of its own, which puts the count back at 621, and said so in the fixture's comment. Fast gate green: mix format on the three touched files, mix compile --warnings-as-errors, mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency (9118 tests, 6 properties, 0 failures). Committed as 759be3c with both test files.
- [2 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by executing the reviewer's pair. On `defmodule E do` / `  def foo do` / `    x = {1 # oops,` / `    |> g()` / `  end` / `end` the rule committed `|> g()}` — the program `{1 |> g()}` — while the identical source with the comma deleted from the comment came back untouched. So a comma inside a *comment* flipped the rule from refusing to committing. The reason is that the "a line ending in `,` is already pinned" exemption was a raw text test that ran *before* the rival-placement probe, so it also skipped the check for a brace being swallowed by a trailing comment — the deliberate guard that makes the control source refuse. Both readings parse and differ (`{1 |> g()}` against the author's equally faithful `{1} |> g()`), which I checked by parsing each. Pinned with two tests that failed on the old code — a fix test asserting the source comes back byte-for-byte unchanged and an analyze test asserting no issue — sitting next to the existing `# }` swallowed-brace tests they mirror. Fixed by making the trailing-comma test against the SourceMask shadow of the source, in which comments, strings, heredocs and sigils are blanked out: only a comma in code now pins the next line inside the literal, which is the only case the reasoning behind the exemption ("closing after a trailing comma drops an element") actually covers. The moduledoc bullet that states that reasoning now says so too. Same commit, 759be3c, same green gate; the 15,625-source differential above covers this change as well.
- note: for the maintainer — the within-the-line scan I added covers the line the repair is committed to, not the earlier lines of the literal, which are still only probed at their ends. A rival placement in the *middle* of an earlier line therefore remains untried. I did not widen it there because the cost is the length of every line of the literal times a whole-file reparse each, and the rule already has a timing test guarding against exactly that kind of blow-up on long literals; whether that trade is right is a maintainer's call.
- commits: 759be3c10aeab60e5b08a9b0ec04451b52d6d843
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — fix round 6 (2026-08-20)
- [1 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by running the rule on the reviewer's input and printing what it emitted: on `defmodule Server do` / `  @doc """` / `  Starts the server.` / `  use GenServer` / blank / `  def start_link(opts), do: ...` / `end` the closing quotes landed above `def start_link`, so `use GenServer` came out *inside* the doc string — the module silently lost the GenServer behaviour, its default callbacks and `child_spec/1` — and `Sourceror.parse_string` on that output returned `{:ok, _}`, so nothing downstream would ever revert it. Pinned with two decline tests in the rule's own fix battery: the `use GenServer` case, and an Ecto `schema "users" do … end` block sitting between the doc and its `def`. Both were watched failing on the old code, each reporting the opener on line 2 and rewriting the file. Fix: a new `@module_code_word` recognises module-level code that is neither a definition nor an attribute — Elixir's four directives (`use`/`import`/`alias`/`require`), Nx's `defn`/`defnp`, and any line that opens a `do` block (which is how an Ecto `schema`, a `test`, or any library block macro shows up at the module's own indentation). `swallows_module_code?/3` checks it across exactly the lines that would become doc text (the first `end_offset` lines, at the `@doc`'s own indentation only, so a deeper-indented example inside the doc is unaffected), and the rule declines when one is there — the same answer it already gives an undocumentable definition form. Verified on the strings the rule actually emits: both damaging inputs now come back byte-identical with `analyze == []`, while the shape the rule exists to repair still repairs (the `@doc` → `@spec` → `def` fixture emits the closer above the `@spec`, parses, and is idempotent), and running the rule's `fix/1` over its own source file still returns it unchanged (the self-corruption check). Committed 4d3e8ab.
- [2 concern] deferred — The mechanical half is landed under [1]: the constructs the reviewer named by hand (`use`, `import`, `alias`, `require`, `defn`, and `do`-block openers such as Ecto's `schema`) now make the rule decline instead of swallowing them. What is left is the policy the reviewer asked a maintainer to settle, and it is still reachable: I ran the rule on `defmodule Router do` / `  @doc """` / `  The browser pipeline.` / blank / `  plug(:fetch_session)` / blank / `  def call(conn, opts), do: super(conn, opts)` / `end` and it repaired the file, folding `plug(:fetch_session)` into the doc string and deleting the plug; the output parses. Any macro call at the module's own indentation that is not one of the recognised words is still read as doc prose. The one question: should the rule flip its default and **decline on any line at the `@doc`'s own indentation that it cannot positively recognise as prose** — rather than declining only on the forms it can name? That buys safety against every unnamed library macro, but it needs a definition of "prose-shaped" that will not veto ordinary doc text (the rule's commonest input is a sentence, and `Adds the two numbers.` must keep repairing), and it will decline a class of files this rule currently fixes. I did not make that call: it is a user-visible trade of repairs for safety, and two reasonable maintainers could land on different sides of it.
- [3 nit] fixed — Confirmed by executing `analyze/1` on the `@doc` → `@spec` → `def` fixture: it reports `"… the closing \"\"\" is missing before the next `def`."` while `fix/1` on the same input inserts the closer above the `@spec`, not above the `def`, so the message points a reader at the wrong line. Pinned with a test asserting the exact message on that fixture, watched failing on the old wording. The message now reads `"Unclosed `@doc \"\"\"` heredoc — the closing `\"\"\"` is missing before the definition or module attribute that follows the doc text."` No other file in the tree asserted the old text. Committed with [1] in 4d3e8ab.
- [4 nit] fixed — The moduledoc's Known-limitation section said a file whose earlier doc is broken and whose later doc is correctly closed is "left to the next round". Confirmed the sentence is false by executing three consecutive rounds of `fix/1` over such a file: rounds 1, 2 and 3 all returned the source byte-identical, and `analyze/1` on the result was `[]` — the veto is a pure function of the same unchanged source, so no later round can answer differently. The text now says nothing in this rule will ever repair such a file and why. This is a documentation defect, so there was no red-on-current-code test to write; instead I added one that makes the corrected sentence load-bearing — it asserts `fix(x) == x` *and* `fix(fix(x)) == x` for that shape, so it goes red if a second pass is ever made to repair it and the doc text drifts back out of truth. Committed with [1] in 4d3e8ab.
- commits: 4d3e8abf2aa2f34f3d168e35af15fa47f53ecd83
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_fn_delimiter.ex — fix round 4 (2026-08-20)
- [1 blocker] fixed — Confirmed by running the reviewer's input through `fix/1`: a file whose `fn` body continues past the repaired `)` with a complete `if … do` / `end` block before the dangling `end` came back with the *`if`'s own* terminator deleted, so `r * 2` ended up inside the `if` and the function returned `nil` whenever the condition was false. The result parses and compiles, so nothing in the rule could object; `analyze/1` also reported line 5 with "a stray `end` follows", which is a false diagnosis of that line. Cause: the rule went to the first `end` token below the repair, on the assumption that the stray one is always the next one down. That assumption is false whenever the `fn` body carries a complete block of its own in between. Pinned with a new test in the rule's own battery ("deletes the stray end, not the terminator of a complete block above it") which failed first, printing the emitted source with `length(row)` sitting inside the `if`. The fix: the downward scan now tallies `do`/`fn` openers it passes (still over `SourceMask`'s shadow, so literals and comments stay invisible, and `do:` is excluded because a keyword is not a block opener) and takes the first `end` at depth zero — the first one that closes nothing opened below the repair. On the reviewer's input the rule now deletes the real stray and emits source that parses; the assertion is an exact whole-string comparison, and the string the rule *actually emitted* is then compiled and executed through `RuleHelpers.compile_and_capture/1` with a call that raises unless `go([[0, 2]]) == [2]` — it returns `{:ok, []}`. Every existing fixture is unaffected (the two candidate lines coincide unless a block opens in between). The moduledoc's third bullet and the `delete_stray_end` comment now say the bound is two-sided: walking *past* the stray and stopping *short* of it both delete a real terminator and both can rebalance the file into something that parses.
- [2 concern] fixed — Confirmed the branch was untested, and found the one path that actually rejects. Measured: after the deletion the front end can only fail in two ways. A tokenizer failure (mismatched delimiter, missing terminator) is always reported at or below the deleted line — the text above it is byte-identical and tokenized without complaint in the previous step — so the integer comparison `end_line > deleted_idx` is always true and never rejects anything. What does reject is a *parser*-phase fault, which cannot be seen on the input at all (tokenizing fails first, on the stray `end`) and becomes reachable only once the deletion has made the file tokenize; its complaint carries a `line` and no `:end_line`, so the rule cannot place where the front end stopped and refuses the deletion. Executed on a genuine occurrence plus a `x = = 1` line: the leftover complaint is `{[line: 10, column: 9], "syntax error before: ", "'='"}` — not a mismatched delimiter and naming no opener, so the first two conditions both pass and this one alone decides. Pinned with a test that asserts the decline plus a control that is the same file with `x = = 1` written as `x = 1`, which *is* repaired to an exact expected string — so the fixture cannot rot into a decline for some other reason. Verified the guard is what decides by temporarily forcing it to `true` and re-running: only that test failed, printing the repair the guard suppresses. The comment on the third condition now states what it rejects on in practice instead of implying the arithmetic carries the weight.
- [3 nit] fixed — The comment above `clears_stray_end?/3` said "Two things must hold" and listed three bullets (the third was added in fix round 3). Changed to "Three things must hold, and none of them is …". Committed with [1] and [2].
- note: the guard in finding [2] costs a real repair. With it forced to `true`, the `ParserFault` fixture is repaired correctly — the genuine occurrence is fixed and the unrelated `x = = 1` is left alone — so any file carrying a parser-phase fault anywhere vetoes an otherwise-sound repair. That is the "declines on a second fault elsewhere" behaviour fix round 1 deliberately removed from this rule, arriving again by a different door. Left as-is (out of scope for these findings); the new test pins the current behaviour so changing it would be a deliberate act.
- note: the `end`-token regex (`(?<![[:alnum:]_])end(?![[:alnum:]_?!])`) also matches `end:` in a keyword list and `:end` as an atom. Before this change a false positive could only make the rule decline; now that openers and closers are counted, one could also shift the depth and move the candidate line. Both forms are vanishingly rare in the region between a repaired `)` and its stray `end`, and no fixture exercises them, so this was not changed — but the failure mode is no longer one-directional.
- commits: 29d199c15e6d22722961cfba322e9808d3b91e53
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency · tree clean)

## lib/syntax/close_unclosed_doc_heredoc.ex — fix round 6 [2], resolved out of band (2026-08-20)
- [2 concern] fixed — Round 6 deferred this as a policy call and it was made: take the
  conservative default. Rather than naming the macros, `@module_code_word` now recognises
  module-level code by SHAPE — a parenthesised call closing on the same line
  (`timestamps()`, `plug(:fetch_session)`), or a bare word whose first argument is an atom,
  a quoted string, a capitalised module path or a capture and terminates at a comma or end
  of line (`plug :fetch_session`, `field :name, :string`,
  `action_fallback MyAppWeb.FallbackController`). Both branches are gated on the line
  carrying no backtick. The name list was rejected on measurement, not taste: it would run
  to ~75 entries across Phoenix/Ecto/Absinthe/Ash/Oban, any library can mint another, and
  `deps/bunt` already writes `defsequence(:reset, 0)` directly under an `@doc` — the
  `defn|defnp` already in the pattern were that list's second version. Around 97% of
  module-level macro idioms omit the parentheses, so the no-paren branch is the load-bearing
  one. Cost measured over the 5,632 doc-text lines at a `@doc`'s own indentation in `lib/`:
  4 false declines, the same price the `\bdo$` branch alone already carried, for 36 of 47
  catalogued idioms instead of none. Proved it is a free narrowing by running the previous
  and current rule over every real `@doc` in `lib/` with its closing quotes deleted: 52/333
  repaired both ways, 0 repairs lost, 0 gained, 0 emitted bytes different. Every clause was
  watched failing — the six decline fixtures go red on the previous predicate, the
  stray-paren fixture goes red without the same-line `)`, the backtick fixture goes red
  without the backtick gate (an earlier version of that fixture opened with a backtick and
  proved nothing, since both branches are anchored on `^[a-z_]`). The 11 idioms still
  swallowed and the separate `@def_word` hole (`deftransform foo(x), do: x`) are recorded in
  the moduledoc rather than guessed at — widening further costs declines and this repo has
  no DSL code to measure against. Committed 6705f0d.
- commits: 6705f0dd
- gate: green (mix format (touched) · compile --warnings-as-errors · mix test --exclude corpus --exclude idempotency — 9132 tests, 0 failures)

## lib/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type.ex — 2026-08-24 (added, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type.ex:92 — A multiline range return such as `@type t :: () -> 1` followed by `.. 10` is not recognized as a continuation, so the fix closes the function type on the first line and changes it to `(() -> 1) .. 10` instead of wrapping the complete `1..10` return type.
- concern: lib/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type.ex:225 — `balanced?/1` counts bracket depth but does not require matching bracket kinds, so input such as `@type t :: () -> [integer()}` is reported and rewritten even though the rewrite cannot repair it.
## test/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-24 (added, test_syntax)
- concern: test/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type_analyze_test.exs:44 — The positive tests call the rule’s `analyze/1` directly; none proves that the automatically discovered Syntax pipeline selects and repairs this realistic unparseable module, so the rule could ship inert while this file and the direct fix tests remain green.
- experiment: settles the concern — `mix run -e 'src = "defmodule M do\n  @type task_func :: () -> any()\nend\n"; expected = "defmodule M do\n  @type task_func :: (() -> any())\nend\n"; unless Credence.Syntax.fix(src) == expected, do: raise("rule did not fire end to end")'`
## test/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-24 (added, test_syntax)
- blocker: test/syntax/fix_bare_tuple_zero_in_type_fix_test.exs:297 — Every repair test calls the rule’s `fix/1` directly; none runs realistic invalid source through `Credence.Syntax.fix/1`. The suite can therefore pass if the new rule is missing from discovery, never reached in round order, or reverted/rolled back by round guards, while users receive the original broken typespec.
## lib/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax.ex — 2026-08-24 (added, rule_syntax)
- blocker: lib/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax.ex:84 — Only whole-line comments and double-quoted strings are masked. Valid source such as `msg = 'compare &> here'`, `msg = ~s(compare &> here)`, or `x = 1 # compare &> here` is rewritten, changing literal values or non-code bytes.
- blocker: lib/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax.ex:48 — The leading-boundary regex omits valid term boundaries such as `=` and `->`, so malformed captures in inputs like `cmp=&>` or `fn ->&> end` are neither reported nor repaired.
- blocker: lib/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax.ex:89 — Any odd `"""` byte sequence toggles heredoc state even inside a comment or ordinary literal; for example, `# """` causes a later `cmp = &>` line to be silently skipped instead of repaired.
## test/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax_analyze_test.exs — 2026-08-24 (added, test_syntax)
- blocker: test/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax_analyze_test.exs:110 — The masking tests cover only whole-line comments and double-quoted strings. Inputs such as `x = 1 # compare &> here`, `msg = 'compare &> here'`, and `msg = ~s(compare &> here)` are valid code but are reported by the rule, allowing its fix to corrupt non-code bytes while this suite stays green.
- blocker: test/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax_analyze_test.exs:9 — Every positive fixture places whitespace or an opening delimiter before the capture. Real malformed inputs such as `cmp=&>` and `fn ->&> end` are silently missed by the rule, so the tests do not prove the repair fires across valid term boundaries.
- blocker: test/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax_analyze_test.exs:118 — The heredoc test covers only a normal heredoc. A `"""` sequence inside a comment or ordinary literal incorrectly puts the analyzer into heredoc mode, causing a later real capture such as `cmp = &>` to be skipped without any test failure.
## test/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-24 (added, test_syntax)
- blocker: test/syntax/fix_capture_operator_syntax_fix_test.exs:155 — The masking tests cover only whole-line comments, double-quoted strings, and heredocs. Valid inputs such as `x = 1 # compare &> here`, `msg = 'compare &> here'`, and `msg = ~s(compare &> here)` are rewritten inside non-code text, changing comments or literal values while this suite stays green.

- blocker: lib/syntax/no_atom_as_function_name.ex:130 — `Regex.run(..., return: :index)` returns byte offsets, but `drop_colon/3` passes that offset to grapheme-based `String.slice/3`; when non-ASCII text precedes `:name(` on the same line, the fix deletes a later character instead of the colon and can repeatedly corrupt the call.

- blocker: lib/semantic/fix_negation_in_guard.ex:80 — Replacing every `!value` with `not value` changes truthiness semantics: `!nil` is `true`, while `not nil` fails the strict-boolean guard and rejects the clause. A guard such as `when !x` therefore gives the wrong answer for `nil`.

For `IO.puts("NimbleCSV.parse_string"); NimbleCSV.parse_string(csv)`, it changes the string and leaves the invalid call untouched; `should_report?/2` still claims the diagnostic, starving the fallback rule.

If `NimbleCSV.define(MyParser, ...)` is at top level and the diagnosed call is inside `defmodule Loader`, the replacement `MyParser.parse_string(...)` resolves to `Loader.MyParser`, not the defined top-level `MyParser`, leaving an undefined-module call.

- blocker: lib/semantic/fix_plug_dependency_module_order.ex:228 — The hand-written `do`/`end` scanner reads heredoc contents as code. - blocker: lib/semantic/fix_plug_dependency_module_order.ex:253 — Intervening top-level code is moved from before the dependency module to after it. - blocker: lib/semantic/fix_plug_dependency_module_order.ex:171 — Plug calls are searched in raw text without masking strings, heredocs, or moduledocs. If an earlier module contains a literal line such as `plug Foo.Dep` and a later router contains the real call that caused the diagnostic, the fixer selects the earlier module and moves the dependency across unrelated module code, changing compile-time behavior beyond the required repair.

## lib/semantic/no_bare_return_in_unless.ex — 2026-08-25 (added, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/no_bare_return_in_unless.ex:127 — The catch-all strips `return` without preserving early-exit control flow. The same corruption occurs for `return` in non-final `case` branches and conditional branches with `else`.
- blocker: lib/semantic/no_bare_return_in_unless.ex:161 — `return(nil)` is used as the helper’s “not found” sentinel, so an early-exit guard such as `if invalid?, do: return(nil); continue()` is not restructured. The later catch-all merely removes `return`, and `continue/0` runs on the invalid path.
- blocker: lib/semantic/no_bare_return_in_unless.ex:155 — Early-exit detection only accepts a branch whose entire body is the `return` call. A realistic branch with preceding work, such as `if invalid? do; log(); return(:error); end; continue()`, is instead handled by the catch-all, causing `continue/0` to run after the supposed early return.

## lib/semantic/outdented_heredoc.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/outdented_heredoc.ex:119 — Outdented sigil heredocs with closing modifiers, such as a `~r"""` heredoc closed by `"""i`, produce the matched compiler warning but are never repaired because the closing-line search accepts only a bare `"""` or `'''`; the semantic pass therefore reports a no-op and leaves the warning in place.
## lib/semantic/prefer_explicit_range_step.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/prefer_explicit_range_step.ex:80 — pre-existing: one real range warning causes the fix to rewrite every descending range in the file, including ranges inside `quote`; quoted syntax is program data, so changing `quote(do: 5..1)` to `quote(do: 5..1//-1)` changes the returned AST and can change program behavior.
## lib/semantic/prefer_kernel_max_over_local.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/prefer_kernel_max_over_local.ex:132 — pre-existing: `defps_named/2` combines clauses from every module in the file instead of locating the module named by the diagnostic. If two modules each contain one complementary `max/2` clause, their unrelated partial implementations are accepted as one canonical pair; the fixer then deletes both and rewrites their calls to total `Kernel.max/2`, changing inputs that previously raised `FunctionClauseError` into returned values.
## lib/semantic/prefer_rescue_before_catch.ex — 2026-08-25 (added, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/prefer_rescue_before_catch.ex:96 — The unrestricted `Macro.postwalk/2` descends into `quote` bodies. If a file has one real out-of-order `try` diagnostic and also quotes an out-of-order `try` as data, the fix reorders both, changing the quoted AST returned at runtime even though the quoted form caused no warning.
## test/semantic/prefer_rescue_before_catch_check_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_semantic)
- nit: test/semantic/prefer_rescue_before_catch_check_test.exs:100 — The test claims the compiler emits the distinct `... in def` diagnostic, but only checks a shared substring; an unrelated warning containing those words would satisfy it. Assert the exact expected diagnostic message instead.
## lib/semantic/require_defmodule_wrapper.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/require_defmodule_wrapper.ex:131 — pre-existing: relocation treats attribute names as unique, so an orphaned `@spec f()` or `@type first()` is silently deleted whenever the module already contains any `@spec` or `@type`, even for a different function or type; the repair loses valid declarations and changes the program’s documented/type-checked meaning.
- blocker: lib/semantic/require_defmodule_wrapper.ex:36 — pre-existing: `match?/1` claims every `redefining @...` diagnostic, including duplicate attributes wholly inside an ordinary module, but `fix/2` returns that source unchanged because there is no attribute before a top-level module; the semantic dispatcher therefore assigns the diagnostic to a repair that does nothing.
## test/semantic/require_defmodule_wrapper_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (modified, test_semantic)
- blocker: test/semantic/require_defmodule_wrapper_fix_test.exs:83 — pre-existing: `compiles?/1` delegates to raw `Code.compile_string/1`; a fixture containing a nonterminating top-level expression can hang or exhaust the test runner instead of being bounded by `RuleHelpers.compile_and_capture/1`.
- blocker: test/semantic/require_defmodule_wrapper_fix_test.exs:100 — every test calls `RequireDefmoduleWrapper.fix/2` directly with a fabricated diagnostic; no test sends realistic failing source through diagnostic generation and Semantic dispatch, so an unregistered or dispatch-starved rule can remain completely inert while this file and its check test pass.
## lib/semantic/rule.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_semantic)
- concern: lib/semantic/rule.ex:19 — The shared analyzer now calls an optional `should_report?/2` hook, but this behaviour does not declare it as a callback or optional callback. Misspelled or incorrectly shaped implementations therefore receive no behaviour warning and are silently ignored, causing diagnostics the rule intended to suppress to be reported.
- nit: lib/semantic/rule.ex:3 — The contract still says semantic rules fix compiler warnings, although the shared Semantic pipeline now dispatches both warning- and error-severity diagnostics.
- nit: lib/semantic/rule.ex:15 — The `diagnostic()` type excludes `position: nil`, even though the shared pipeline explicitly accepts missing positions and semantic rules/tests handle them; callers relying on this public contract receive an incorrect type specification.

- nit: test/semantic/undefined_function_decline_test.exs:32 — The fixture calls `send_resp/3`, but its fabricated diagnostic says `send_resp/2`; this does not test the source/diagnostic pair described and could conceal an arity-sensitive regression.

- blocker: test/semantic/undefined_function_local_fix_test.exs:593 — The new `exit/2` repair is tested only by calling `UndefinedFunction.fix/2` with a hand-written diagnostic. They can all pass even if production dispatch never selects the rule; no test proves these repairs fire end-to-end on realistic compiler diagnostics.
- concern: test/semantic/undefined_function_qualified_fix_test.exs:1197 — The comment says each repaired module was compiled, but these tests only compare output text and never compile or otherwise verify the replacement targets exist and preserve the claimed meaning.
## lib/semantic/undefined_function.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/undefined_function.ex:490 — pre-existing: `replace_drop_module/5` edits raw text, so when a string or comment containing `Enum.length(` precedes the real call on the diagnostic line, it rewrites the non-code text and leaves the undefined call unchanged; the same unmasked corruption exists in `rename_negate_arg_on_line/5` at line 666 and `to_range_on_line/3` at line 810.
- blocker: lib/semantic/undefined_function.ex:625 — `rename_add_arg_on_line/5` only uses the mask to establish that some code call exists, then `do_rename_add_arg/4` selects the first raw-text occurrence; input such as `{"List.second(x)", List.second(xs)}` corrupts the string and leaves the real call undefined. `wrap_args_on_line/4` has the same split masked-check/raw-selection defect at line 774.
- blocker: lib/semantic/undefined_function.ex:639 — pre-existing: byte offsets from `:binary.match/2` are passed to grapheme-based `String.slice/2`; a multibyte character before `List.second(...)` makes the splice start at the wrong location and can produce malformed code. The same byte/grapheme mismatch occurs in the negate, wrapping, and range helpers.
- blocker: lib/semantic/undefined_function.ex:851 — pre-existing: three-argument Python `range(a, b, step)` excludes `b`, but the replacement `a..b//step` includes `b` whenever the step lands on it; for example, `range(0, 10, 2)` gains an extra `10`, changing the program’s answer.
## lib/semantic/undefined_string_alphanumeric.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/undefined_string_alphanumeric.ex:86 — pre-existing: the direct-call regex stops at the first closing parenthesis, so `String.alphanumeric?(String.trim(c))` becomes `String.match?(String.trim(c, ~r/^[a-zA-Z0-9]$/))`; this changes the nested call and can fail at runtime.
- blocker: lib/semantic/undefined_string_alphanumeric.ex:83 — pre-existing: replacement is applied to every matching byte sequence on the diagnostic line without masking strings or comments, so a valid call beside `"String.alphanumeric?(x)"` repairs the call but also silently changes the string value.
- blocker: lib/semantic/undefined_string_alphanumeric.ex:86 — pre-existing: a multiline call such as `String.alphanumeric?(\n  c\n)` is admitted by the compiler diagnostic but the line-scoped regex cannot match it, so the rule reports the issue and leaves the undefined call unrepaired.
## lib/semantic/unused_variable.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/unused_variable.ex:185 — Collision detection searches only the diagnostic’s line. For a multiline pattern such as `def f(\n  _ref,\n  ref\n), do: :ok`, fixing unused `ref` produces a second `_ref`; repeated variables in a pattern impose equality, so calls with different arguments stop matching. The generated name must be unique across the whole binding pattern, not merely the current line.

- concern: test/semantic/unused_variable_check_test.exs:75 — The message assertions use substring matching, so the tests still pass if `to_issue/1` or end-to-end analysis truncates or otherwise changes the compiler diagnostic while retaining the variable name; assert the exact expected message instead.
## test/semantic/unused_variable_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (renamed, test_semantic)
- blocker: test/semantic/unused_variable_fix_test.exs:331 — pre-existing: end-to-end fixtures define globally named modules such as `Nested`, `Multi`, and `Both`, which are also defined by other test files; parallel tests can redefine/purge the same modules and become order-dependent or flaky. Use uniquely namespaced fixture modules.
## lib/semantic/used_underscore_variable.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_semantic)
- blocker: lib/semantic/used_underscore_variable.ex:90 — Renaming does not check whether the stripped name already exists in the clause. On `def f(_x, x), do: {_x, x}`, it produces `def f(x, x), do: {x, x}`, changing which arguments match and the returned value. (pre-existing)
- blocker: lib/semantic/used_underscore_variable.ex:88 — The ASCII-only variable regex makes the rule claim diagnostics it then leaves unrepaired for valid Elixir identifiers such as `_café` or `_ready?`; under warnings-as-errors, the reported warning remains and no later Semantic rule can own it.
- blocker: lib/semantic/used_underscore_variable.ex:141 — Clause boundaries are detected from unmasked text and indentation rather than syntax. A `def`/`end` line inside a heredoc can be mistaken for the enclosing clause, and a valid nested block whose `end` shares the function’s indentation can truncate the range; the fix can then rename only the use but not the parameter, producing undefined-variable code. (pre-existing)

- blocker: lib/pattern/avoid_graphemes_enum_count_with_predicate.ex:249 — `graphemes_call?/1` accepts any arity, so parsed partial code such as `String.graphemes() |> Enum.count(&(&1 == "a"))` is reported, but `fix_pipe/2` cannot extract a subject and deletes the `Enum.count/2` step, producing `String.graphemes()` instead of repairing the call. ## lib/pattern/avoid_length_guard_less_than2.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/avoid_length_guard_less_than2.ex:176 — A guarded variable nested inside a parameter pattern (for example, `def f({list}) when length(list) < 2, do: :ok`) is reported by `check/2`, but `replace_param_with_patterns/3` only replaces top-level parameters and emits no patch, so the pipeline leaves a reported defect unrepaired.

For example, `case true and :unknown do true -> :yes; false -> :no end` raises `CaseClauseError`, but the auto-fix returns `:yes`; a `ready?()` function returning `:unknown` has the same behavior change.
## lib/pattern/no_case_tuple_guard_dispatch.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_case_tuple_guard_dispatch.ex:176 — On an otherwise eligible `case`, a comment inside the scrutinee tuple or a discarded tuple pattern is lost when the node is rebuilt as `cond`; the shared comment-preservation gate then rejects the patch, but `check/2` still reports the issue, leaving the diagnostic unrepaired.
## lib/pattern/no_chunk_by_identity_for_dedup.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_chunk_by_identity_for_dedup.ex:51 — A direct-looking `Enum.map(Enum.chunk_by(list, & &1), &List.first/1)` used as the right side of another pipe is actually an `Enum.map/3` call after pipe insertion, but the recursive walk still reports and rewrites it. For `foo |> Enum.map(Enum.chunk_by(list, & &1), &List.first/1)`, the fix produces `foo |> Enum.dedup(list)`, changing the invalid call and its compiler error rather than performing the advertised equivalent repair.
- nit: lib/pattern/no_chunk_by_identity_for_dedup.ex:32 — The non-default priority 510 has no comment explaining its ordering dependency, contrary to the rule-ordering policy; name the rule or construct that requires this rule to run after the default band.
## lib/pattern/no_codepoint_string_reverse.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_codepoint_string_reverse.ex:162 — pre-existing: the nested-call guard accepts the unintended cross-product `IO.join(...)` and `Enum.iodata_to_binary(...)`; if `IO` or `Enum` is aliased to an application module implementing that valid call, the rule replaces its arbitrary behavior with `String.reverse/1`, changing the program’s answer.
## lib/pattern/no_cond_two_clauses.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- concern: lib/pattern/no_cond_two_clauses.ex:155 — `check/2` accepts any parseable `cond` keyword list containing a matching `do` entry, but the fix assumes `do` is the first entry. On parseable, non-compiling input with another keyword before `do`, the newly broadened pipeline reports the issue and `fix_patches/2` crashes, leaving it unrepaired.
- experiment: Run `mix run -e 's = "cond(foo: :bar, do: (true -> :a; true -> :b))"; a = Sourceror.parse_string!(s); IO.inspect(Credence.Pattern.NoCondTwoClauses.check(a, source: s)); IO.inspect(Credence.Pattern.NoCondTwoClauses.fix_patches(a, source: s))'` to settle the concern at lib/pattern/no_cond_two_clauses.ex:155.
## lib/pattern/no_crypto_hash_pipe_swapped_args.ex — 2026-08-25 (added, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_crypto_hash_pipe_swapped_args.ex:104 — The predicate accepts every literal atom, including invalid algorithms such as `:bogus` or `nil`. On `"data" |> :crypto.hash(:bogus)`, the rewrite produces `:crypto.hash(:bogus, "data")`, which still raises `ArgumentError`, so the rule records an applied repair without fixing the program; restrict admission to algorithms accepted by `:crypto.hash/2`.
## test/pattern/no_crypto_hash_pipe_swapped_args_check_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- nit: test/pattern/no_crypto_hash_pipe_swapped_args_check_test.exs:29 — The test name incorrectly says the piped value is already the data: this pipe expands to `:crypto.hash(data, :sha256, salt)`, an invalid three-argument call. The clean expectation may be intentional scope, but its stated rationale is false.
## test/pattern/no_crypto_hash_pipe_swapped_args_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- blocker: test/pattern/no_crypto_hash_pipe_swapped_args_fix_test.exs:38 — The tests omit valid one-argument pipes such as `:sha256 |> :crypto.hash(:payload)`, which already means `:crypto.hash(:sha256, :payload)` and returns a digest; the rule admits this input and rewrites it to `:crypto.hash(:payload, :sha256)`, turning valid code into an `ArgumentError`.
## lib/pattern/no_dead_map_update.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_dead_map_update.ex:72 — The matcher treats every syntactic `Map` alias as Elixir’s standard `Map` module. On input such as `alias Custom, as: Map`, it rewrites calls to `Custom.update/drop/delete` under assumptions that need not hold, changing behavior or removing side effects.
## lib/pattern/no_destructure_reconstruct.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_destructure_reconstruct.ex:198 — pre-existing: the fix always introduces the variable `items` without checking whether that name is already bound. For example, fixing `def f([a, b], items), do: {[a, b], items}` makes both arguments share one binding, so the clause only matches when they are equal and returns the wrong value otherwise.
## lib/pattern/no_doc_false_on_private.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_doc_false_on_private.ex:33 — Pre-existing: the rule only detects adjacent AST statements, so valid ordering such as `@doc false`, then `@spec helper(integer()) :: integer()`, then `defp helper(x)` is silently missed and the redundant private documentation remains.
## lib/pattern/no_double_filter.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_double_filter.ex:116 — The first assignment may rebind the source or operand variable used by the second filter. For `numbers = Enum.filter(numbers, &(&1 >= 0)); neg = Enum.filter(numbers, &(&1 < 0))`, the original second filter reads the newly filtered `numbers`, but the rewrite partitions the original list, changing the answer.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_double_filter.ex:172 — `slice/2` assumes every admitted source and predicate range is single-line and treats Sourceror columns as grapheme offsets. A multiline capture such as `&(\n &1 >= 0\n)` can produce a negative slice length and crash fixing; non-ASCII text before a range can extract the wrong bytes and yield a rejected or incorrect patch.
## lib/pattern/no_double_sort_same_list.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_double_sort_same_list.ex:62 — `Enum.sort(list, :desc)` is not equivalent to `Enum.reverse(Enum.sort(list))` when distinct terms compare equal. For `[1, 1.0]`, stable descending sort preserves their input order, while reversing the ascending result swaps them, so the fix changes the returned values.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_double_sort_same_list.ex:27 — Sorts are grouped only by source-variable name across the entire AST, ignoring function scope and statement order. Separate functions using an `arr` parameter, or a descending sort before the ascending binding, can therefore be rewritten to reference an undefined or unrelated ascending variable, producing invalid or incorrect code.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_double_sort_same_list.ex:148 — A piped sort is identified only by the pipeline’s root variable. Different pipelines such as `arr |> Enum.map(f) |> Enum.sort()` and `arr |> Enum.filter(p) |> Enum.sort(:desc)` are treated as sorting the same list, and the latter is replaced with the reverse of the former despite operating on different values.
## lib/pattern/no_duplicate_function_clauses.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- nit: lib/pattern/no_duplicate_function_clauses.ex:4 — pre-existing: the moduledoc says identical argument patterns make a later clause unreachable, but identical patterns with different guards can both match; the rule actually requires normalized arguments, guards, and bodies to be identical.
## lib/pattern/no_duplicate_spec.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_duplicate_spec.ex:42 — The fix removes an `@spec` while declaring no safety assumption, but duplicate specs are observable through `Code.Typespec.fetch_specs/1`: input containing two identical specs returns two type entries before the fix and one afterward, so the rewrite does not preserve the exact answer for every admitted input.
## lib/pattern/no_eager_with_index_in_reduce.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_eager_with_index_in_reduce.ex:120 — pre-existing: replacing eager `Enum.with_index/1` with lazy `Stream.with_index/1` changes evaluation order for admitted effectful enumerables. If enumeration and the reducer have side effects, or the reducer raises early, the original fully enumerates before reducing while the fix interleaves or stops enumeration, changing observable behavior.
## lib/pattern/no_empty_map_new.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_empty_map_new.ex:155 — pre-existing: the matcher ignores lexical aliases, so `alias MyMap, as: Map; Map.new()` is rewritten to `%{}` even when `MyMap.new/0` returns a non-map value, changing program behavior.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_empty_map_new.ex:136 — pre-existing: the generic AST walk descends into quoted code, so `quote do: Map.new()` is rewritten to `quote do: %{}`, changing the AST value produced at runtime rather than replacing an evaluated empty-map construction.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_empty_map_new.ex:58 — pre-existing: every pipe RHS is discarded wholesale, so a standalone nested call such as `items |> process(Map.new())` is neither reported nor repaired even though the pipe supplies an argument to `process/2`, not to the nested `Map.new/0`.
## lib/pattern/no_enum_count_for_length.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- nit: lib/pattern/no_enum_count_for_length.ex:12 — The documented `Map.keys(config) |> Enum.count()` example says the idiomatic rewrite is `length(Map.keys(config))`, but this rule actually rewrites that input to `map_size(config)`, so the moduledoc misstates its own fix.
## lib/pattern/no_enum_into_empty_mapset.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_enum_into_empty_mapset.ex:68 — Pre-existing: the rule treats any alias spelled `Enum` or `MapSet` as the standard module. With `alias CustomSet, as: MapSet`, it rewrites `Enum.into(items, MapSet.new())` to `MapSet.new(items)` even though those custom functions need not be equivalent, changing behavior or causing a runtime failure.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_enum_into_empty_mapset.ex:107 — Pre-existing: both check clauses accept arbitrary trailing arguments, so calls such as `Enum.into(items, MapSet.new(), fun, extra)` produce a diagnostic, while every fix clause requires exactly the supported arity and emits no patch. The rule therefore promises a repair it silently declines.
## test/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values_equivalence_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- blocker: test/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values_equivalence_test.exs:16 — The test marks every admitted match as an always-failing repair, but `Map` can be a local alias. For example, with `alias ListMap, as: Map` where `ListMap.values/1` accepts a list, `Map.values(Enum.sort(m))` returns normally; the rule rewrites that valid call to `Enum.map/2` and changes its answer. This test therefore certifies an unsafe rule without covering the alias-resolved input that disproves its premise.
## lib/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values.ex — 2026-08-25 (added, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values.ex:187 — The fix assumes every sorted element is a `{key, value}` pair, but the check admits any enumerable. For `Map.values(Enum.sort([2, 1]))`, it rewrites the existing `BadMapError` into an `Enum.map/2` whose tuple pattern raises `FunctionClauseError`, so the reported defect remains unrepaired.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values.ex:132 — The matcher treats the source aliases `Map` and `Enum` as the standard library modules without resolving lexical aliases. Code such as `alias MyEnum, as: Enum` can make `Map.values(Enum.sort(x))` valid when `MyEnum.sort/1` returns a map; the rule then rewrites valid code to a call to `MyEnum.map/2`, changing its answer or causing an undefined-function crash.
## test/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values_check_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- concern: test/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values_check_test.exs:53 — The arity coverage tests only valid nested calls and never pin the boundaries for piped calls. Consequently, the rule accepts nonexistent `Enum.sort/3` and `Enum.sort_by/4` pipe stages (and nested `sort/0`/`sort_by/1`), reporting a misleading `BadMapError` diagnosis and rewriting code whose actual problem is an undefined function.
## test/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- blocker: test/pattern/no_enum_sort_then_map_values_fix_test.exs:33 — The purported realistic `payments |> Enum.sort_by(& &1.date)` case only checks rewritten text. For a map, `Enum.sort_by/2` passes `{key, value}` tuples to the sorter, so `& &1.date` fails before `Map.values/1`; for a list of payment structs, the emitted tuple-pattern mapper fails. The test therefore does not prove this advertised field shape is repaired end-to-end and can bless a rewrite that still crashes on every plausible input.
## lib/pattern/no_enum_take_negative.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_enum_take_negative.ex:146 — pre-existing: the diagnostic tells users to sort in the opposite direction and take a positive count, which changes results for unsorted inputs; for `[2, 1, 3]` with `-2`, `Enum.take/2` returns `[1, 3]`, while the suggested descending sort and positive take returns `[3, 2]`.
## lib/pattern/no_explicit_product_reduce.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_explicit_product_reduce.ex:58 — pre-existing: the rule treats every syntactic `Enum.reduce/3` as the standard library call, even when `Enum` is aliased to a custom module; valid code such as `alias MyEnum, as: Enum` is rewritten from `MyEnum.reduce/3` to `MyEnum.product/1`, which may return a different value or be undefined.
## lib/pattern/no_explicit_sum_reduce.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_explicit_sum_reduce.ex:81 — pre-existing: the matcher ignores the initial accumulator, so `Enum.reduce(list, 10, fn x, acc -> x + acc end)` is rewritten to `Enum.sum(list)`, losing 10; an effectful initializer is skipped entirely.
- nit: lib/pattern/no_explicit_sum_reduce.ex:28 — pre-existing: priority 501 has no adjacent explanation of its ordering dependency, contrary to the rule-ordering policy.
## lib/pattern/no_fetch_then_update.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_fetch_then_update.ex:237 — For `Map.update/4`, the rewrite drops the default argument without evaluating it. On a present key, an input such as `Map.update(map, key, raise("boom"), fun)` originally raises but is changed to a successful `Map.put`, and any side effects in the default are also lost.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_fetch_then_update.ex:160 — The walker descends into quoted code. An `Map.update!` expression inside `quote do ... end` in the successful fetch branch is rewritten even though it is AST data rather than an executed update, changing the value returned by the program.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_fetch_then_update.ex:186 — The rule identifies `Map` textually and does not verify that it refers to Elixir’s `Map` module. Under `alias CustomMap, as: Map`, it rewrites `CustomMap.update!/3` or `update/4` into `CustomMap.put/3`, which need not have equivalent behavior.
## lib/pattern/no_filter_then_count.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- nit: lib/pattern/no_filter_then_count.ex:44 — The `## Good` example still names its module `BadNFTC`, so maintainers copying the recommended example get a module explicitly labeled as bad.
## lib/pattern/no_filter_then_first.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_filter_then_first.ex:59 — Pre-existing: for a valid pipeline with steps after the match, such as `Stream.filter(nums, pred) |> Enum.at(0) |> inspect()`, `Macro.prewalk/3` emits overlapping patches for both the whole pipeline and its matching left subtree. Sourceror then applies original-coordinate replacements to the same bytes, which can corrupt the repaired source or cause the safety gate to discard the fix while the rule still reports the issue.
## lib/pattern/no_find_value_default_case.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_find_value_default_case.ex:148 — A variable default is treated as eagerly safe, but the rewrite moves it before evaluation of the callback expression. For `Enum.find_value(list, fun = fn _ -> nil end) || fun`, the original binds `fun` before reading it; the fix produces `Enum.find_value(list, fun, fun = fn _ -> nil end)`, which reads `fun` before that binding and can fail compilation or read a different outer binding.
## lib/pattern/no_grapheme_palindrome.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_grapheme_palindrome.ex:178 — Variable bindings and comparisons are matched globally by name, without lexical scope. If one function binds `g = String.graphemes(x)` while another function compares its own `g == Enum.reverse(g)`, the rule can rewrite the unrelated comparison using `x`; when that function also has an `x` variable, the result compiles but changes its answer.
## lib/pattern/no_group_by_for_frequencies.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_group_by_for_frequencies.ex:291 — The rule treats every local `length(group)` call as `Kernel.length/1`. In a valid module that excludes the Kernel import and defines its own `length/1`, the original callback can return another value or perform side effects, while the fix replaces it with `Enum.frequencies_by/2`, changing behavior.
## lib/pattern/no_guard_equality_for_pattern_match.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_guard_equality_for_pattern_match.ex:262 — pre-existing: multiple equalities for the same parameter are collapsed with `Map.new/2`. For `def f(x) when x == :a and x == :b, do: :impossible`, both guards are removed and only the last literal becomes the head pattern, producing `def f(:b), do: :impossible`; input `:b`, which previously fell through, now executes this clause.
## test/pattern/no_guard_equality_for_pattern_match_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (modified, test_pattern)
- concern: test/pattern/no_guard_equality_for_pattern_match_fix_test.exs:252 — The repeated-variable regression only asserts the generated text; it never executes the before/after clauses on the stated counterexample `f(:a, {:b, 2})`, so the test does not prove that the silent dispatch miscompilation is actually prevented end-to-end.
- nit: test/pattern/no_guard_equality_for_pattern_match_fix_test.exs:235 — The comment says `f(1)` and `f(false)` return `:other`, but the fixture defines no catch-all clause; those calls would raise `FunctionClauseError`, making the documented execution claim inconsistent with the test input.
## lib/pattern/no_hallucinated_ets_keytype_option.ex — 2026-08-25 (added, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_hallucinated_ets_keytype_option.ex:143 — When an option list contains `keytype: :term` more than once, the rule deletes only the first pair; the remaining invalid pair still makes `:ets.new/2` raise, so the single-pass repair reports success without repairing the call.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_hallucinated_ets_keytype_option.ex:67 — The matcher never verifies that the call has exactly two arguments. It therefore rewrites the last list argument of nonexistent `:ets.new/1`, `:ets.new/3`, and other arities, changing unrelated invalid calls while leaving their actual arity error unfixed.
## test/pattern/no_hallucinated_ets_keytype_option_check_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- blocker: test/pattern/no_hallucinated_ets_keytype_option_check_test.exs:6 — The tests never assert that non-`/2` calls are clean. The current matcher consequently flags nonexistent `:ets.new/1`, `:ets.new/3`, and other arities when their last argument contains `keytype: :term`, misdiagnosing and rewriting calls whose actual defect is their arity.
## test/pattern/no_hallucinated_ets_keytype_option_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- blocker: test/pattern/no_hallucinated_ets_keytype_option_fix_test.exs:6 — No case covers multiple `keytype: :term` entries in one literal option list. The implementation deletes only the first, so input such as `:ets.new(:t, [:set, keytype: :term, keytype: :term])` remains invalid after the single repair pass.
## lib/pattern/no_hd_tl_when_cons_bound.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_hd_tl_when_cons_bound.ex:244 — The matcher treats every unqualified `hd(var)` or `tl(var)` as a Kernel call. If the module excludes Kernel’s function and imports a custom `hd/1` or `tl/1`, the fix replaces that custom call with cons destructuring and changes the returned value.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_hd_tl_when_cons_bound.ex:274 — Fresh-name generation gives up after `head1000`/`tail1000`. A valid clause whose patterns already bind all 1,001 candidate names is still reported, but the replacement name becomes `nil`, so the matching `hd/1` or `tl/1` call is left unrepaired.
## lib/pattern/no_identity_enum_map.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_identity_enum_map.ex:57 — The matcher treats any written `Enum.map/2` as Elixir’s standard `Enum`, even when `Enum` is a local alias for another module. On input such as `alias Custom, as: Enum`, the fix changes `Custom.map(value, identity)` into `Custom.to_list(value)`, which need not return the same answer and may not exist.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_identity_enum_map.ex:138 — The rule assumes written `&Function.identity/1` is the standard identity function. With `alias Custom, as: Function`, that capture can transform its argument, yet the rule removes it and rewrites the call to `Enum.to_list/1`, changing the result.
## lib/pattern/no_identity_function_in_enum.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_identity_function_in_enum.ex:52 — The matcher treats any `Enum` alias as the standard library module and likewise assumes `Function` in `&Function.identity/1` is canonical. With `alias MyEnum, as: Enum` or `alias MyCallbacks, as: Function`, it rewrites calls to user modules into unrelated standard-library calls, changing results or causing runtime failures.
## lib/pattern/no_if_empty_for_enum_min_max.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_if_empty_for_enum_min_max.ex:16 — pre-existing: the rule is not equivalent for stateful enumerables. On a `Stream.resource/3` whose result changes each time enumeration starts, the original calls `Enum.empty?/1` and then starts enumeration again for `Enum.min/1`, while the rewrite enumerates only once, so it can return a different minimum; the new moduledoc’s claim that these forms “rewrite identically” is therefore false.
## lib/pattern/no_if_true_false.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_if_true_false.ex:136 — pre-existing: the rule treats any AST call spelled `Enum.all?/2`, `Enum.any?/2`, or `Enum.empty?/1` as boolean without resolving aliases. With `alias MyEnum, as: Enum` and a function returning `5`, `if Enum.all?(...) do true else false end` returns `true`, but the fix returns `5`, changing the program’s answer.
## lib/pattern/no_is_nil_guard.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_is_nil_guard.ex:124 — The fix checks whether a nil-tested parameter is used only in the function body, not in the remaining guard. For `def foo(x, y) when is_nil(x) and x == y, do: :ok`, it emits `def foo(nil, y) when x == y`, where `x` is unbound; the production compile-safety gate then reverts the repair and leaves the reported issue unfixed.
## lib/pattern/no_kernel_op_in_pipeline.ex — 2026-08-25 (unchanged_rule, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_kernel_op_in_pipeline.ex:99 — A quoted pipeline fragment with a zero-arity head is rewritten unless it contains `unquote`; a literal fragment such as `quote do: Enum.count() |> Kernel.==(0)` can later be spliced after an upstream pipe, and the fix changes it to an infix comparison that the outer pipeline cannot accept, causing macro expansion to fail.
## lib/pattern/no_keyword_get_integer_key.ex — 2026-08-25 (modified, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_keyword_get_integer_key.ex:89 — `:timeout |> Keyword.get(5000)` is claimed and rewritten to `:timeout |> Enum.at(5000)`, even though the atom-first-argument sibling rule owns this defect and would repair it to `5000`; the piped detector ignores the pipe’s left-hand side, causing diagnostic contention and a wrong repair.
## test/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key_equivalence_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- blocker: test/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key_equivalence_test.exs:20 — This test performs none of the 12 claimed probes: `mark_equivalence_repair/1` returns `:ok` for any non-empty string. It never proves the original crashes, the rule fires, or the repaired code returns the intended values, so it passes even if the repair is inert or wrong.
## lib/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key.ex — 2026-08-25 (added, rule_pattern)
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key.ex:60 — The matcher treats every `Keyword.get` alias as Elixir’s standard `Keyword` module. If code declares `alias MyKeyword, as: Keyword` and that module’s `get/2` accepts a keyword-list argument, the rule rewrites a valid call to `get/3`, changing behavior or causing an undefined-function error.
- blocker: lib/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key.ex:106 — Nested matches such as `Keyword.get(Keyword.get(opts, inner: 1), outer: 2)` produce overlapping patches for the outer and inner calls. The outer replacement still contains the unfixed inner AST, so applying both ranges can corrupt/reject the repair or leave a reported inner defect unfixed.
## test/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key_check_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
- blocker: test/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key_check_test.exs:110 — The test codifies a false negative for `opts |> Keyword.get(partial: false)`. This is still a two-argument `Keyword.get(opts, [partial: false])` call and always crashes; the unambiguous repair is `opts |> Keyword.get(:partial, false)`, but the test requires the rule to ignore it.
- nit: test/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key_check_test.exs:120 — The metadata test only proves that some line number exists, so an incorrect location would pass; use a multiline fixture and assert the exact expected line.
## test/pattern/no_keyword_get_keyword_key_fix_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)

## test/pattern/no_keyword_get_with_atom_first_arg_equivalence_test.exs — 2026-08-25 (added, test_pattern)
